# 8. Brand and positioning

## What a brand is and when to build one

### Brand beyond the logo

- A brand is the set of beliefs, emotions, perceptions and attitudes a customer holds toward a company and its product. Logo, colors and typography are representations that reinforce these feelings, so they are not the brand itself. [L1044.001]
- A brand means everything that shapes how people perceive a company, not only its logo. It includes the aesthetics (logo, website layout, store or office look), the verbal style, the company persona, the customer experience, and the expectations customers bring to it. [L0487.001]
- A brand comprises five elements: aesthetic design, verbal language (verbiage), company persona, customer experience, and customer expectations. Together they create the complete perception of your company. [L0237.001]
- Brand equity is the value a brand earns beyond its product, built from its values, tone of voice and the culture it represents. A brand sells who you represent, not only the item itself. [L0561.022]
- Brand perception is governed mainly by how the business acts: how it handles customers, its tone of voice, and its conduct on social media. A name only sets the first impression, so consistent good conduct matters more than the perfect name. [L0990.012]
- A dropshipping store becomes a brand when the product gets a name and people recognise it by that name rather than by its generic category. Brands show faces, custom packaging, labels, and custom content in their ads and store. [L0638.031]
- Building a loyal customer base through good support and consistent branding is what turns a dropshipping product into a brand. Brands often reach this stage with branded packaging and branded creative. [L0638.032]
- When branding is done correctly, the specific products you sell are irrelevant. The brand succeeds through connections built with customers via content, not through product features or benefits. [L0237.002]
- Consistent design across logo, colors and typography lets customers recognise the store immediately and attach their past experiences to it. Inconsistency weakens these associations. [L1044.004]
- Visual choices must match customer expectations for the niche: green and earthy tones for gardening products, luxury type for luxury goods, and friendly type for family or children's products. Designs that clash with the niche make customers doubt the store. [L1044.005]
- Brand attachment can be built even on mundane everyday products such as toothpaste, shampoo or coffee. These brands gain loyalty through trust in the name and by reinforcing their differences in a commodity market. [L1044.016]

### What a brand creates

**Lower customer acquisition cost**
- Building a brand lowers customer acquisition costs because referrals come without ad spend. [L0237.004, L0487.004]
- Brand awareness moves the buying journey upstream. Customers who already know the company find it when they are ready to buy, instead of comparing product listings. Product selection matters less when the relationship comes first. [L0487.002]
- A real brand attracts customers who know your name before they search for the product. [L0237.003]
- Your dream customers are your biggest source of new traffic through word-of-mouth and referrals. Building content they engage with creates a self-sustaining growth engine. [L0237.014]
- A dream customer buys, engages with content, and recommends the brand to others, so the goal is an advocate rather than a one-time buyer. Advocates bring in new people and reduce the cost of acquiring them. [L0487.003]

**Repeat purchases**
- Building a brand increases repeat purchases. [L0237.004, L0487.004]
- Brands that generate returning customers can afford higher customer acquisition costs. Stores that rely only on new buyers become unsustainable as ad costs rise. Trust built through good product quality, on-time delivery and working tracking drives repeat purchases. [L1251.011]
- Customers return when their first order went well: the product was on time, tracking worked and the quality matched expectations. Trust is the core reason repeat purchases happen. [L1251.012]
- A brand creates value through identity alignment, trust and loyalty, emotional attachment, a clearly defined market segment, and influence on how customers buy, post and recommend. Each of these increases repeat sales and organic reach over time. [L1044.002]

**Identity and loyalty**
- Customers defend what they identify with, so a brand that represents a customer's identity has a strong position. Designing the store around the customer's self-image is more effective than designing it around the founder's taste alone. [L1044.003]
- Large sportswear brands often sell a culture and values rather than the product itself, so their ads may barely show the shoe. A brand that represents a lifestyle can charge more than a product-focused competitor. Brand building is a long-term discipline that must be earned through consistent messaging. [L0561.063]

**Pricing power**
- Commodity products can carry high prices when the brand supplies identity and status. For example, a running shoe with little material difference from cheaper alternatives can sell for around 120 to 140 dollars because of the brand story and the athletic identity it represents. [L1044.015]
- Strong brand equity makes new products easier to sell, since customers pay more for a known name with comparable quality. The speaker reported that 70% to 80% of new items sold for his established brands. [L1508.029]

**Resale value and market recognition**
- Building a brand increases business valuation if sold and establishes market recognition. [L0237.004]
- A recognisable brand is also harder to copy, which raises the valuation when the business is sold. [L0487.004]

**Ownership of the customer relationship**
- On marketplaces such as Amazon, the platform owns the customer relationship. Buyers see Amazon as the service provider, and the seller cannot build a brand identity or community from customer data. [L0760.015]
- Build the brand for the long term, aiming to be competitor-proof, marketplace-proof and timeless. Treat your own website as the main sales channel even if a marketplace like Amazon also sells, because the own store has higher margins and lets you control the customer interaction. [L0334.005]

### Brand versus commodity

- A seller who looks and sounds the same as competitors becomes a commodity and can only compete on price. This is a losing position because someone will always undercut you. Price-only competition also fails for resellers who cannot lower prices further. [L0411.005]
- A business that customers see as essentially the same as its competitors becomes a commodity, and commodities are much harder to sell. Being perceived as unique makes selling easier. [L0893.004]
- Competing only on product features or technology turns a product into a commodity that buyers compare mainly on price. Stores should add identity, experience and a clear promise so customers have a reason to choose them. [L1044.014]
- Selling a generic product sourced from a marketplace such as AliExpress without your own branding leads to the same item appearing from many sellers in Google Shopping. Shoppers then see the same product across the whole platform and have no reason to choose you. [L1508.028]
- The product must be desirable. It does not have to be totally different from competitors, but it must give buyers a clear reason to want it. Desirability comes first, and the rest of the marketing builds on it. [L1438.034]
- Brand and product support each other. Some buyers purchase because they love the brand, others because they love the product. A strong brand identity helps buyers choose your label over copies and lookalikes. [L1438.035]
- Competition can serve as inspiration, but the aim is a brand that is desirable regardless of how saturated the market is. Other brands leave room for many players if the product is distinct and wanted. [L1438.039]
- A brand you create yourself usually has zero search volume at launch, so generic keyword ads will not bring in the first customers. [L0768.003]
- Ads that target competitor brand and product names work for dropshipping early on, but this approach does not carry over to your own brand. Do not expect the same ad approach to bring the first customers for your own label. [L0768.004] *(older source, 2021-22)*

### Naming the brand

- A general store name is acceptable when the owner is testing several products, but the store should still define what it stands for. Colors and typography should reflect how the store wants to be perceived, even if the positioning is somewhat vague. [L1044.018]
- Give the brand a name you can build, so that people can search for it once demand exists. [L0768.003]
- A recognizable store name only helps once customers search for it. [L0664.081] *(older source, 2020)*

### Staging: validate with sales first

**Core rule**

- Validate a product with dropshipping sales first, then build a brand around it once there is proven demand. [L0661.056]
- A brand should not be built before a drop-shipping store works. Learn the skills by running a profitable drop-shipping store first, then apply them to a brand once several six figures are generated. [L0561.026]
- Starting a brand from scratch can cost thousands of dollars and requires extensive testing to confirm product-market fit, so a drop-shipping store is a cheaper way to build skills first. [L0561.027]
- Do not build a brand before you have sales. [L0664.081] *(older source, 2020)*
- Branding builds loyalty and repeat purchases, and it works best after a product has proven sales. Build it through custom images, a clear story, a visible face, reliable shipping and good customer support. [L1377.049]
- A brand built in a single high-demand niche can perform well, as shown by a leather jacket store doing strong daily sales. Brand work often starts after general stores already produce steady revenue. [L0660.025]

**What to avoid before sales**

- Beginners waste weeks on logos, colors and brand storytelling for a pump-and-dump product. A simple brand name and product images are enough to test. [L0347.026]
- Spending time on a logo, a custom branded store or polished brand assets before a product has any sales wastes money and attention. Publish content first, learn from its performance, and build the brand once sales arrive. [L0820.049]
- Spending too much time on branding, such as styled photos, models and Instagram posts, slowed down sales testing. Focus first on making sales from the product, then build the brand later. [L1444.054]

**Minimum viable brand early**

- Minimum professional standards are sufficient for startup success. You do not need multi-thousand-dollar branding packages to look professional and attract customers and suppliers. [L0323.010] *(older source, 2021-22)*
- In the early stage, spend on paid traffic and sales instead of brand design, social followers or custom photography. Build social channels after traffic and an email list exist, and link to them from emails. [L0853.091] *(older source, 2021)*
- A minimum viable brand (MVB) is the smallest brand that is enough to go live and give customers a credible perception of the store. It avoids a full brand exercise at launch, and it is expanded as the store grows and the test results show what works. [L1044.017]

**Early-stage creative spending**

- Early stage, do not hire an expensive creative director. That salary margin can be pumped straight into ads, and with a good product, more ad spend grows your brand faster than brand planning. [L0041.011]
- The product is the brand in early stage. Invest in the product itself and let it speak through advertising and word of mouth. Good products go viral. Do not pretend you are too sophisticated for direct response. [L0041.012]
- Athletic Greens is the highest-performing direct-to-consumer brand ever. It prioritised direct response advertising and conversion metrics over design aesthetics, achieved massive scale, and now has Joe Rogan as a promoter. This shows that a brand grows from performance and ad spend, not from early-stage creative direction. [L0041.010]
- Do not sacrifice advertising performance for brand consistency in the early stage. Brand continuity (colour palettes, tone of voice, iconography) elevates you, but only if it does not reduce ad spend. [L0041.009]

**Testing a product before its own design**

1. Test a product by using an existing design from another seller. [L1508.030]
2. Shoot your own photos for it, so the listing does not match the photos competitors use. [L1508.030]
3. Once results show demand and the brand has traction, move to your own designs. [L1508.030]

### Full brand later

- Brand continuity and consistency pay off once demand is proven and the ad spend is working. [L0041.009, L0334.005]
- Three critical requirements for brand success: you must know your customer better than anyone, you must invest significantly in content creation, and you must commit to playing the long game rather than seeking quick results. [L0237.012]
- CrossFit demonstrates complete brand alignment. Its aesthetic is not a normal gym, its verbiage includes specialised terminology (Box, WOD, AMRAP, Games), its persona is polarising (love it or hate it), its customer experience emphasises family and community, and its expectations centre on consistency. Every element reinforces a cohesive identity that attracts dream customers. [L0237.013]
- Build the brand for the long term, aiming to be competitor-proof, marketplace-proof and timeless. [L0334.005]

### Timing: where the sources disagree

Sources differ on when a brand investment makes sense. Each value applies to a different stage.

| Position | Rule | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| No brand before sales | Build the brand only after a product or store has proven sales | Product still being tested | [L0664.081] *(2020)*, [L0561.026], [L0661.056], [L1377.049] |
| Minimum brand at launch | Professional basics are enough; spend on traffic before design | Store launch, limited budget | [L0323.010] *(2021-22)*, [L0853.091] *(2021)*, [L1044.017] |
| Brand after steady revenue | Brand work starts once general stores produce steady revenue | Niche store with steady income | [L0660.025] |
| Ads before brand consistency | Keep ad spend ahead of brand continuity | Early stage | [L0041.009], [L0041.011] |
| Full brand investment | Invest in content, customer knowledge and long-term identity | Committed long-term brand | [L0237.012], [L0334.005] |

#### Further details

- A brand built from scratch requires research into the niche and sub-niches, a clear customer avatar, and a sample that proves product-market fit before scaling. Skipping this makes brand building far riskier than dropshipping tests. [L1217.031]

## Customer research and avatars

### Research before the store and the copy

- Understanding an audience means looking past demographics (age, gender, socioeconomic status) to behaviour and the underlying motivations, meaning the reasons why people act. This distinction separates effective advertisers from those who work by trial and error. [L0258.001]
- Before branding, the store owner must know what the product is, what it does and how it benefits customers. The owner must also know who the customers are, how they behave, and what their needs, goals and motivations are. [L1044.010]
- Understand the customer before designing the store. Review a customer Facebook group, the photos members post and what they watch online. This removes guesswork about layout, imagery and messaging. [L0709.030]
- Before writing any copy, define the target market: the problems, desires and other facts about prospective customers. Copy works as a conversation with those people, so it only persuades when you know them well. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.001]
- Keep the customer avatar sheet as a one-page reference that anyone can check before writing copy for a single product, email or ad. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.002]
- Customers are defined as the group of people who experience the problem your product solves, so customer research must confirm that this group exists. Knowing the ideal customer in detail is required before you can communicate the problem and solution effectively. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.004]
- Empathy and the ability to see your business from the customer's perspective are core marketing skills. The more you put yourself in the customer's position, the better the marketing becomes, especially for fields that must be estimated by intuition. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.034]
- Customer research is not a one-time task. Review the customer research document at least once a year and update it whenever you learn something new, because the customer base can shift over time. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.013]
- Completing the avatar profile before writing copy shortens the copywriting process. Treat the avatar as a living document and update it as you learn more about the market. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.029]
- Audience research should cover four pillars:

  | Pillar | What it describes |
  |---|---|
  | Demographics | Who they are |
  | Psychographics | What they value |
  | Behaviours | How they act: research habits, loyalty, online communities |
  | Goals, needs and motivations | What they want and what belonging they seek |

  Use these pillars so that all brand messaging speaks to a defined group rather than everyone. [L1071.005]
- Before filling in the audience section of a brand document, prepare two earlier research outputs: a detailed customer research spreadsheet and a shorter summary document. Base demographics, psychographics, behaviours and goals on these sources rather than on assumptions. [L1071.022]
- For performance-oriented products, audience psychographics typically include a desire for quality and results, a strong interest in the activity the product supports, and appreciation of brand status and reputation. The brand image and product descriptions should reflect these values directly. [L1071.023]
- When defining a brand's target audience, the overall group can be broad, spanning both genders, several age groups and low to high income levels. Name one focused core age range where the product category is most active, such as 15 to 45 for a sports brand, and let that core guide the messaging. [L1071.021]
- Enter the research findings into the research form to sharpen the ICP and USP before any creative is produced. [L1019.027]

### The four layers: demographics, psychographics, behaviour and motivation

- The four segmentation components:

  | Component | What it covers | Examples |
  |---|---|---|
  | Demographics | Static attributes of a person | Age, gender, income level, education, marital status |
  | Psychographics | Personality, values, beliefs and attitudes | Openness, conscientiousness, level of organization, tendency to worry; values such as control or belonging; beliefs about the world, religion, opinions |
  | Behavior | What people do, their context and where the problem fits into their daily life or browsing situation | Using a product in a kitchen, scrolling a feed while queuing in a store |
  | Goals, needs and motivations | The underlying why behind what people do | Status, power, security, freedom, belonging |

  [L0993.005]
- Demographics are the most surface-level layer and should be considered last when defining a target audience. They matter when they change whether the product fits. Example: a menstruation product, since people over 50 generally no longer menstruate, as the average age of stopping is above 50. [L0993.006]
- Demographics alone often fail to explain buying. Buyers of one jewelry piece can range from 20 to 50 years old. The underlying need for status is usually a stronger basis for targeting than age or income. [L0993.007]
- Psychographics cover how a person is wired and what they believe: personality traits, values such as control or belonging, beliefs about the world, and attitudes and opinions. Use them to choose the tone and angle of the message. [L0993.008]
- For a viewer persona, needs and motivations matter more than demographics. Capture what the person desperately wants, what they fear not getting, what stands in their way and what outcome they want. Age, income and education serve only as background. [L1427.003]
- Behavioural segmentation asks what the person is doing and in which context the problem appears. Place the product at the point in the daily routine where the problem or trigger occurs, not only where they browse. [L0993.009]
- Example: a vegetable slicer's trigger is the moment someone cuts vegetables by hand, finds it tough and annoying, and gets an uneven cut. Describing the product through that exact moment makes the problem and solution immediately recognizable. [L0993.010]
- Sharpen the buyer persona with concrete details such as reading habits, where they shop, income level, occupation and how often they buy. For example, a buyer who purchases four times a year as a gift for friends. These details guide ad and website messaging. [L1016.021]
- Premium beauty-tool example: the buyer invests in appearance and wellness, buys from Instagram and Facebook ads, and trusts beauty influencers and stylists. Describing the target customer and their psychology this concretely gives the audit enough to write relevant messaging. [L0886.028]
- The persona does not need every field completed. Fill in as much as you can to get a usable picture of the customer. Demographics are static facts, psychographics cover values and personality, behaviour covers what they do and the context of their problem, and motivations cover the deeper needs and feelings. Desire, feature and persona together are enough to start writing a decent headline. [L0516.004]

### The iceberg model

- Use the iceberg model to understand the customer in layers. Demographics (age, gender, location) are the visible surface, behaviour is what they do, the need is the reason behind the behaviour, and the motivation is the deep human driver. Marketing that reaches the deeper layers converts better and builds a sustainable brand. [L1426.034, L0993.015, L0998.018]
- Goals, needs and motivations explain why people act. Demographics and behaviour are what you usually observe, while the reasons behind them make a message powerful. They usually drive purchases more strongly than demographics or behaviour do. [L0998.017, L0993.012]
- Think of an iceberg: demographics and behaviour form the visible tip, while needs, tensions, fears and motivations sit underneath. Your task is to uncover the layer below what people do. [L0998.018]
- Motivation chain, from visible purchase to underlying need:
  - A commuter buys coffee and a bagel on the way to work. The visible behaviour is the purchase.
  - The need is that they have no time to make breakfast.
  - The fear is being late, which could damage their reputation at work or cost them a promotion.
  - The motivation is avoiding shame, losing control of their life and running short of money.

  Use this chain to move from the visible purchase to the underlying need and motive when building a buyer profile. [L0993.015, L0998.019, L0644.046]
- Status can be a hidden buying driver even when convenience appears to be the reason. A buyer may choose a premium café because its setting makes them look successful while working, not only because it is close by. [L0998.022]
- Coffee-chain example: people do not buy mainly for the coffee quality. They buy for a pleasant social environment and the status of looking successful while working with a laptop, and proximity is secondary. The deeper status and environment need keeps them coming back. [L0993.013]
- Ask why the buyer really buys and how the product fits into their life. The surface action, such as buying a quick snack on the way to work, often hides a deeper motive like protecting their reputation or sense of control. [L0644.036]
- Deeper motivations matter more than surface demographics. A buyer often wants to avoid a deeper consequence, such as missing work and losing a promotion, not just to remove a single pain. Writing copy about that underlying need speaks more intimately. [L0999.046]
- Ask why repeatedly to move from a visible behaviour to the need behind it. Keep asking until you reach a need such as security, status, freedom or belonging. [L0998.021]
- Procedure for finding the real motive behind a purchase, asking why at each layer:
  1. What does the customer do?
  2. What need does that action meet?
  3. Why does that need matter to them, for example to protect their reputation or control at work?
  4. What deeper motivation sits underneath, such as identity, control, belonging or status?

  [L1426.043]
- Procedure for working the iceberg with an audience:
  1. Record the surface facts about the audience: demographics and observable behaviour.
  2. Ask why they do this behaviour and identify the need it serves.
  3. Ask what they fear will happen if the need is not met.
  4. Ask what deeper motivation drives them, such as control, status, security or growth.
  5. Use the deepest layer to shape the message, without necessarily stating every motive openly in the ad.

  [L0993.016]
- The deepest motives do not always need to appear in ads, but you must understand the audience closely enough to speak to them at that level. Ads that touch the motive often outperform ads that only describe the behaviour. Knowing these motives is what makes a message feel relevant. [L0993.017, L0998.020]

### Human drivers and motivations

- Nine core human drivers motivate purchasing: power and control over outcomes, freedom from pain, conserving or gaining resources (time, effort, money, physical), status and superiority, connection and belonging, sexual selection, social conformity (keeping up with peers), personal growth, and providing for loved ones. Map the product to one or more of these drivers. [L0258.002]
- Base human needs to check against an audience include: power, control over outcomes, freedom from pain, gaining or conserving resources (time, energy, money), status and superiority, sexual selection, survival, connection, love and belonging, keeping up with peers and not falling behind, growth, and caring for loved ones or having a purpose bigger than oneself. Each need is felt to a different degree depending on the person. [L0993.018, L0998.023, L0997.023, L0997.024]
- Strong messages tie a product to a fundamental human need instead of just describing its function. Common drivers include power, control over outcomes, freedom from pain, and gaining or conserving resources such as time, energy and money. [L0997.023]
- These needs are present in every audience but weighted differently depending on the person. Other core needs include status and superiority, connection, love and belonging, keeping up with peers, growth, looking after loved ones, and having a purpose bigger than oneself. [L0997.024, L0998.023]
- Buyer motivations often sit in opposing pairs, such as control versus enjoyment on the personal side and power versus belonging on the social side. Decide which pair the product speaks to before writing the message. [L0644.037]
- Opposing motivations create tension that good marketing can address: control versus enjoyment, power versus belonging. Identify which side a product serves and speak to that need in the copy. [L1426.035]
- Map both directions for each segment. Buyers move toward a goal they want or away from a pain they want to escape. Ads and offers work better when they name the specific pain to remove or the specific goal to reach. [L0497.026]
- Negative emotions such as fear, shame, guilt, anger, helplessness and grief narrow attention onto one point, which makes them useful for hooks and problem agitation. Positive emotions such as hope, relief, pride, belonging and love broaden attention, which makes them useful for presenting the solution and the transformation. [L0613.024]

### Status, identity and social comparison

- People compare themselves to others and feel envy toward those who have something they lack, while they scorn those who lack something they already have. Brands can use this status comparison dynamic to position a product as the visible sign of being ahead of others. [L0497.025]
- Status and belonging are powerful because standing within a group affects access to resources and opportunities. Today that means career progression, social acceptance and keeping pace with peers rather than physical survival. [L0997.025]
- Status, attractiveness and social standing are sensitive needs. Blunt claims about them can backfire, so phrase them carefully and indirectly rather than shaming the viewer. The need should be understood without being stated in a crude way. [L0993.019, L0998.024, L0997.026]
- Status, comparison and social claims should be articulated in a way the customer can accept about themselves, because blunt wording can insult or alienate the audience. [L0997.026]
- Desires can include social status, such as wanting others to see the buyer as cool. Status is a strong angle for copy and should be recorded next to practical desires. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.021]
- Stopping at the product as the only desire misses the status, identity and pain angles that drive buying. Always list what the customer wants beyond the product. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.022]
- Record the identity the customer wants, meaning who they want to be. For example, a parent may want to be a reliable parent, and a host may want to be a relaxed host. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.026]
- A customer who wants to be seen as successful by friends may buy products to project that identity. A parent buying a new bed for a child may want to be seen as providing the right things and being a good parent. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.033]
- Complete the motivation fields by asking what the customer wants to gain, what they want to be, what they want to do, and what they want to avoid. The avoid field captures the negative experiences the problem causes, which the purchase helps the customer escape. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.032]

### Customer avatars: worksheet and fields

- Define the ideal customer avatar with a worksheet before setting up the store's structure:
  1. Open a customer avatar worksheet built for your niche.
  2. Fill in every field for the person who should land on the site and buy.
  3. Use the answers to decide which visitors to attract and how to convert each of them into a buyer.

  [L0355.003]
- Define one dream customer with a name, age, relationship status and income level, and choose products that fit her daily life and needs. Check every new product against this customer profile before testing. [L0388.022]
- Example: a dream customer described as a 40-year-old single woman with money maps to different products by life situation: home decor for a home-improvement-minded person, stretch bands for someone who goes to the gym, and a relaxed hoodie for evenings at home. [L0388.023]
- Segment customers by use case or buyer type (avatars) rather than by job title, because the best customers may come from very different professions. In the example, the top customers spent over $1,000 per year and included people from very different fields, so avatars built on use cases were more useful for targeting. Build a bundle or promotion for each main avatar. [L0334.059]
- A business with one customer type may fill in only one avatar column. Businesses with different groups of buyers, such as B2B and B2C or customers of different genders, should keep a separate avatar for each because their needs can differ greatly. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.016]
- The first pass at the avatar document should contain only what you already reasonably know. Accuracy is not required at this stage, because the document is a living resource that grows with each review. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.017]
- Give each customer avatar a name, because team members and agencies can then refer to it quickly, for example when briefing an ad for a specific avatar. The name can be anything. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.018, L0907.013]
- The customer avatar document is not confidential. Share it with anyone who creates work for you or interacts with your customers. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.019]
- A one-page customer research document can be handed to agencies, freelancers, consultants, and new employees or customer service staff so they understand who the customers are without lengthy briefings. Agencies, especially traffic agencies, will ask about target market, customer attributes, and what customers look like. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.005]
- Customer avatar tool: a spreadsheet, either a Numbers or Excel file on Apple or Windows, or a Google Sheets copy made in your own Google Drive that you can share with a team. Spreadsheets are easier to customize and expand than PDFs, whose formatting is hard to manage. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.005]
- Avatar sheet fields (three avatar slots, one column each):

  | Field | What to record |
  |---|---|
  | Customer Avatar 1 / 2 / 3 | One column per avatar; the sheet has three avatar slots |
  | Avatar Name | Any memorable name, used as shorthand in team discussions |
  | Age Range | Basic demographic data |
  | Gender | Basic demographic data |
  | Average Income | Basic demographic data |
  | Experts Followed | Bloggers, YouTubers, authors, TV personalities, athletes and influencers the customer follows |
  | Favorite Online Spaces | Facebook groups, online forums, Reddit communities, Quora, fan pages |
  | Prior Relevant Purchases | Other versions of your product, supporting products, accessories, and products bought alongside yours |
  | Primary Problem(s) | The main problems the avatar wants to solve by buying your product; one or several |
  | Wants / Desires | Desires that follow from the primary problems and relate to your product |
  | Emotions Currently Feeling | How the customer feels now about the problem, e.g. frustrated, sad, excited |
  | Beliefs | What they believe will happen if they buy, what they believe about your product, and what they believe about themselves |
  | What do they want to gain? | Outcomes beyond the physical product, such as pleasure or status |
  | What do they want to be? | The identity they aim for |
  | What do they want to do? | The actions they want to take, as specific as possible |
  | What do they want to avoid (pain)? | The pains they are trying to escape |

  (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.006]
- Start the profile with basic demographics (age range, gender and average income), then move to the deeper fields such as problems, desires, beliefs and emotions. The basic demographic fields to record are age range, gender and average income target. Record gender separately for each avatar if customer groups differ in ways that matter. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.008, L0872.020]
- Procedure for building or checking avatars (older advice, 2021-22):
  1. Check whether you already have customer profiles. If they are current, review them for accuracy and stop there; if not, fill in the full sheet.
  2. Decide how many avatars you need. One avatar is enough if one product solves one problem for one type of buyer; otherwise make one per distinct buyer type, such as B2B and retail, or different experience levels within retail.
  3. Give each avatar a short, memorable name.
  4. Record age range, gender and average income.
  5. List the experts, bloggers, YouTubers, authors, TV personalities, athletes or influencers this avatar follows.
  6. List prior relevant purchases: other product versions, supporting products, accessories and products bought alongside yours.
  7. List favourite online spaces: Facebook groups, forums, Reddit communities, Quora and fan pages.
  8. Brainstorm the primary problems the avatar wants solved by your product; several are fine.
  9. Turn each problem into specific wants and desires that relate to your product.
  10. Record the emotions the avatar feels right now.
  11. Record beliefs about what happens after buying, about the product, and about themselves.
  12. Record what they want to gain beyond the physical product.
  13. Record who they want to be.
  14. Record what they want to do, as specifically as possible.
  15. Record what they want to avoid (pain).
  16. Research anything you do not know, and keep the sheet updated as your knowledge grows.

  [L0907.007]
- Procedure for customer research (older advice, 2021-22):
  1. Start with your own knowledge and experience from serving customers, and the signals customers have already given you.
  2. Open the customer avatar spreadsheet and fill in every field you already know or can reasonably estimate, without aiming for perfect answers.
  3. Give each avatar a name so it can be referenced quickly in team and agency work.
  4. Use Google to research the fields you do not know yet, such as experts, books, magazines, movies and TV, and online spaces.
  5. Fill in the remaining fields: prior purchases, primary problems, wants, emotions, beliefs, and the gain, be, do and avoid questions.
  6. Carry out the exercise within the current month, then keep updating it as you learn more.

  [L0872.012]
- Record prior relevant purchases, such as other product versions, supporting products and accessories. If your product is an accessory, note which main products your customers already own, for example which phone models buyers of a phone case use. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.016, L0872.023]
- Fields you cannot answer yet can be left empty and completed later. For passion-based niches, find relevant books and magazines by searching Google for titles about that passion. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.024]
- A half-hearted profile leads to half-hearted marketing and weak results. Treat the exercise as serious research and look up anything you do not know instead of guessing. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.011]
- Describing the customer as a broad group such as 'everyone interested in the activity' is the lazy approach. Dig into sub-groups, since their knowledge, needs and problems usually differ. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.010]

### Problems, desires, beliefs and motivations in the avatar

- Primary problems are the problems the customer experiences that the product or business solves, and there can be several per avatar. Note every problem you can identify, even if the list grows over time. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.026]
- Define primary problems as the main problems the product helps solve. There may be one or several, and there is no single right answer. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.019]
- Ask why the customer wants the product to find the deeper problem behind it. A person who wants a rowing machine may really want to feel fitter after a stressful year, and that underlying problem drives the purchase more than the item itself. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.027]
- Wants and needs are different. A customer can want a product without needing it, and the want usually comes from an underlying problem such as a status concern. Record both the want and the problem behind it. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0872.029]
- Turn each problem into a desire tied to how the product is used, not just the product itself. For example, a first-time home cook's problem is making a meal quickly, so the desire is a meal kit that matches their skill level. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.020]
- Record beliefs in three areas: what the customer thinks will happen if they buy, what they believe about the product, and what they believe about themselves. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.024]
- Customers buy for more than the physical product, so list what they want to gain, such as the pleasure of use or social status. Each gain should connect back to their original desires. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.025]
- Record what the customer wants to do, and make it as specific as possible. A precise action, such as mastering one particular technique, gives sharper copy than a vague goal. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.027]
- Core problem excavation, for each avatar:
  1. Surface problem: how people describe it publicly (at least 10 quotes).
  2. Real problem: what they think at 2 a.m. and never say aloud (at least 15 quotes).
  3. Identity problem: the negative labels and self-concept the problem created (at least 10 quotes).
  4. Relational problem: strain on partners, family, friends, and avoided social situations (at least 10 quotes).
  5. Trajectory problem: what they fear becoming and the worst case they imagine (at least 10 quotes).

  [L0613.038]
- Target avatar research inputs to request before starting:
  1. The brand website or key product information.
  2. Facebook ad account data: gender split with spend and performance by gender, performance by age cohort, time-of-day and day-of-week spend patterns, top-performing creative themes, and geographic concentration if relevant.

  [L0613.036]

### Desires, emotions and distress

- A persona is the same as an avatar or an ideal customer profile (ICP). The most attributed persona in a competitor's data is the audience its ads target most, so study that persona first. [L0374.013]
- A desire is the result the customer wants, such as fading acne scars, even skin tone or smoother skin texture. Desires work better when an emotion is attached to them. [L0374.014]
- Emotions attached to a desire make the message more effective. For an acne product, love or attraction can be the emotion: the acne stops a person from being seen as a partner, and the product is the fix. Broad, non-targeted love messaging can also be strong for acne products. [L0374.015]
- Top emotions in a competitor's data, such as hope and relief, are a data-backed signal of what to target. Use the emotion with the most attributed ads as the starting point for your own messaging. [L0374.016]
- Distress segments:

  | Segment | Intensity range | Baseline state | Attention | Message receptivity |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Low (curious or preventive) | 1-4 | Mild curiosity, optimization focus | Broad, can process positive and negative cues | Responds to aspirational and lifestyle messaging |
  | Moderate (problem-aware) | 5-7 | Frustration, concern, mixed hope and skepticism | Beginning to narrow | Needs problem acknowledgment first; wants proof; distrusts overpromising |
  | High (desperate) | 8-10 | Chronic shame, fear, desperation, anger, helplessness | Severely narrowed | Weak to positive cues; needs extensive validation; responds to 'finally, someone who understands' |

  [L0613.027]
- Label-emotion map, one entry for each of at least 10 significant labels:
  - Label.
  - Primary emotion.
  - Secondary emotions.
  - Attention state while the label is active.
  - Behavioural consequences (what they do or avoid).
  - Which messages can reach them in that state.
  - The aspirational label that could replace it.

  Also map the transition path from each of the top 5 negative labels: trigger, required proof, and typical duration of the change. [L0613.030]

### Interviews and surveys

- In the early stage of a new product, run customer interviews yourself instead of delegating them, even when your time is expensive. Raw answers in the customer's own words are the source for headlines and copy, and a summary removes the phrasing you need. [L0378.117]
- Before any content work, build a customer profile with a questionnaire of about 15 questions covering at least these areas:
  1. Demographics: age, sex, race.
  2. What a typical day in the customer's life looks like.
  3. Their biggest pain points.
  4. What they value most.
  5. Their goals.
  6. The shopping experience they expect.
  7. Which magazines and blogs they read.

  Use the answers for every article, email and image, and phrase content in the customer's own language. [L0380.005]
- Survey procedure for current and future customers:
  1. Ask current or future customers what they have at the moment and how they use it.
  2. Ask how your product would help them and what they would need to know before buying.
  3. Run the questions as a survey funnel, so a prospect answers a few questions before getting the offer.
  4. Segment the respondents by their answers (for example colour, resistance level, whether they train with a partner).
  5. Use these segments later: a new product or variant goes only to people who already said they want that feature.

  [L0643.006]
- The 'Ask' approach (book 'Ask' by Ryan Levesque) is about asking customers directly instead of guessing what they want. Perceived value matters more than the number of added extras: a message that addresses the buyer's specific problem makes the same offer feel more valuable. [L0643.007]
- Survey template question: "What led you to make a purchase with us?" [L0757.016]
- Customer research for offers:
  1. Survey new customers with the questions in the survey template.
  2. Read the answers and note what the buyer cared about most when choosing.
  3. Decide what your specific customer would value the most.
  4. Write down your own store ideas as you work through the offer steps, so the list is ready when you need it.

  [L0757.017]
- Use the following prompt, unchanged, to pull core mass desire, benefits, pain points, proof and transformation stories from a product description and customer reviews:

  ```
  Act as a senior direct-response researcher who receives only two inputs: (1) a product description covering technical details, features and specifications, and (2) customer reviews containing genuine feedback and testimonials. Working exclusively from these two inputs, pull out the core mass desire, the main benefits and features, the customer pain points, the proof points and testimonials, and the transformation stories.

  Core mass desire:
  1. Study how customers phrase things. Spot emotional themes that recur across the reviews and map the before and after states they describe.
  2. From the feedback, answer: What is the deepest emotional driver? Which transformation appears again and again in the reviews? What do customers say they can now do or feel after using the product? Which pain or frustration are they most relieved to be rid of?
  3. Phrase the core mass desire in one of these two formats: To [emotional benefit] without [pain point], or To finally [desired outcome] despite [previous obstacle].

  Required inputs:
  Product description: [insert the complete product description]
  Customer reviews: [insert 3-5 detailed customer reviews]

  Do not make up any facts that are absent from these inputs.
  ```

  [L0887.020]

### Identity markers and negative labels

- Identity marker research for identity-based products, in order:
  1. Document at least 10 common values of the identity group, with examples.
  2. List visual markers (clothing, accessories, aesthetics).
  3. Collect at least 20 group-specific phrases or terms.
  4. List at least 15-20 influencers and authorities the group respects.
  5. Analyse at least 10 other brands that market to the identity.
  6. Document at least 5-7 shared experiences.
  7. List the podcasts, YouTube channels and publications the group consumes.
  8. Describe their aspirations and fears, how they signal belonging, which opposing identities they define themselves against, and how the identity has changed over the past 5 years.

  [L0613.008]
- Negative identity label research: collect labels in five categories (body or health, capability or intelligence, relational or social, financial or success, moral or character), with at least 10 labels per category. Each label needs an exact quote, primary emotion, intensity 1-10, frequency, origin (self-applied, applied by others or both), duration, and source link. Also collect at least 20 aspirational labels, each with the emotion it would trigger, the distance from the current identity on a 1-10 scale, what would make the person feel they had earned it, what blocks them now, and a source link. [L0613.029]
- Avatar research counts:
  - At least 20 negative identity labels.
  - 15 aspirational labels.
  - 10 failed solutions, with why they failed and how that felt.
  - 20 failure phrases.
  - 10 skepticism patterns.
  - 10 things the avatar refuses to do, each with the real reason.
  - 25 hurtful quotes across relationship types (5-7 per relationship type: partner, children, parents, friends, coworkers, self).
  - 3-5 secret conversation scenarios.
  - 5 quotes in each post-transformation category (supporters, doubters, self, strangers).
  - Top 10 objections, with the real concern under each.
  - 5 label transition paths.
  - 3-5 complete avatars.

  [L0613.039]

### Complete avatar profile and transition stories

- Complete avatar profile sections, in order:
  1. Demographic snapshot: name, age range validated by ad data, gender validated by ad data, location and situation, income, life stage.
  2. Ad data validation: share of spend, engagement rate, conversion rate, time patterns, resonant creative themes.
  3. Core problem: surface, real and identity.
  4. Emotional architecture: distress level, five ranked emotions with intensity and quotes, attention state, emotional entry point, emotion that shuts them down, transition threshold, and the permission needed to hope.
  5. Identity profile: primary negative label with duration and origin, secondary labels, aspirational label with distance on a 1-10 scale, identity wound summary in 2-3 sentences.
  6. Top 5 fears, with quotes.
  7. Relationship impact on partner, children, parents, friends and self, each with a hurtful quote.
  8. Failed solution history (5 items with result and quote) and refusals (3 items with real reason and inner monologue).
  9. Transformation they want: a 3-5 sentence genie-wish description, how relationships would change, and the identity shift.
  10. Top 3 objections with the real concern under each, skepticism triggers, and what would make them believe.
  11. Purchase psychology: emotional trigger, proof, risk reversal, permission, purchase-trigger emotion, breaking-point scenario.
  12. Messaging strategy: emotional sequence, hook approach (problem, mechanism, identity or aspiration), tone, format, and best time to reach.
  13. A mirror-moment opening line.
  14. A 5-7 sentence raw monologue of what they would say to a close friend after drinks.

  [L0613.040]
- Transition story procedure:
  1. Write down the negative label the person started with.
  2. Write down the positive label they now claim.
  3. Note what made the change possible.
  4. Record how long the transition took.
  5. Identify the turning point in the story.
  6. Trace the emotional path from the starting state to the end state.
  7. Compare how they describe themselves before and after.
  8. Attach the exact source quote and its link.

  Collect at least 10 complete transition stories. [L0613.068]

### Persona brief before scripting an ad

- Fill in this persona brief before scripting an ad:

  ```
  Persona brief to fill in before scripting an ad:
  - Demographics: age range, gender mix, income level, education, location.
  - Psychographics: attitudes toward life and general psychology.
  - Behavior: what the person does in a typical day.
  - Goals: what they want more than anything.
  - Fears: what they are afraid of not achieving.
  - Desires: the outcome or feeling they are after.
  - Pain points: the problems blocking that outcome.
  - Motivation: the underlying drive behind their behavior.
  ```

  [L1427.004]

## Market awareness, sophistication and segmentation

### Two dimensions to check before writing any ad

- Establish two dimensions before creating ads: customer product awareness (how well the target knows the problem and that a solution exists) and market sophistication (how exposed the market is to the problem and to existing products). Read the Breakthrough Advertising copywriting framework to see how these shape the whole marketing message. [L0258.004]
- Place prospects on the awareness scale before you look at Facebook or build ads. Most advertisers target only product-aware people because it is easy, but speaking to the other levels can make sales far easier and more relevant. Spend two to five hours on this mapping. [L0643.004]
- Audiences who already know the product well and fully unaware audiences are both among the hardest to sell to. Unaware buyers offer the highest revenue ceiling but need the most effort to move down the funnel. [L0374.018]
- Map the audience through awareness stages (problem aware, solution aware, product aware) and match each stage to a marketing angle built on the pain point. This is what lets the audience convert. [L1030.020]
- Position the product by where it sits in the market and how exposed that market is to similar items before writing copy. In a saturated market the product must stand out with unique features; in a less crowded market a clear problem-and-solution message can work. [L0552.028]

### Product awareness: the five stages

- **Most Aware:** knows the product and wants it, so it only needs an offer and a call to action. [L0497.016]
- **Product Aware:** knows the problem and some products, so reinforce what your product does differently. [L0497.016]
- **Solution Aware:** knows the problem and that solutions exist, but not your product, so show how it solves the problem. [L0497.016]
- **Problem Aware:** knows the problem but no solutions, so introduce the solution. [L0497.016]
- **Completely Unaware:** does not recognise the problem or need, so show the problem, for example through a surprising fact. [L0497.016]
- The same five levels appear in reverse order in the Schwartz scale: unaware, problem aware, solution aware, product aware, most aware. Each level needs a different message, and the same logic applies to ad copy, homepage, sales page and product page. [L0643.002]
- Example from a dry-scalp journey: the person first becomes problem aware, then solution aware (several ways to treat a dry scalp), then product aware (narrowing to certain shampoos), and finally most aware, having chosen a brand and waiting for a discount. For the completely unaware stage, one can market a hidden problem, for example the fact that over-washing hair causes a skin condition. [L0497.017]
- Buyers sit at different stages of product awareness. When they know the problem but not the solution, lead with the problem. When they already know many solutions, they need the product's distinct benefit to stand out. [L0644.038]
- Product positioning depends on market awareness. A product in a crowded market needs a new mechanism or functionality to stand out, while a product that solves a new problem can be introduced directly. Each stage (problem unaware, problem aware with unknown solution, solution aware with an alternative) needs different copy. [L1426.036]

### Reading awareness from ads and sales data

- In the Awareness Stages view, many Unaware ads suggest a new brand buying cold, top-of-funnel traffic. Many Problem Aware or Product Aware ads suggest an established brand running brand marketing or launching offers. [L0892.019]
- Check awareness and sophistication against sales data. When volume falls even as the ad budget rises, and a direct statement of the product's function or the desire it satisfies no longer sells, the product needs to be reborn. Treat it as an unaware-market problem and restart the analysis at the identification stage. [L0330.046]
- Products that lose public acceptance are often reborn by identification rather than by a new claim. The reader is brought into the ad through a situation or self-image he recognises, not through a desire he has already rejected. [L0330.047]
- A declining product was revived when its headline changed to an identification one. The approach addressed a fear that daily habits were damaging the reader's health, then offered a healthy alternative with no direct comparison to the old product. The brand recovered its market, while the old direct claims no longer worked. [L0330.095]

### Market sophistication: the five stages

- **First to Market:** the claim is new, so just launch the product. [L0497.018]
- **Second to Market:** copy the successful claim and amplify it. [L0497.018]
- **Third:** the market knows the claim, so introduce a new feature or mechanism that makes the claim fresh and believable. [L0497.018]
- **Fourth:** elaborate on a secondary claim, since the main claim has already been made. [L0497.018]
- **Fifth:** all features are known, so attach the product to the customer's identity, as Nike does with "we represent athletes like you." [L0497.018]
- A market with many competitors sits at the fifth or fourth stage, and its products need new mechanisms or features to stand out. [L0497.019]

### Writing for a first-to-market product

- Write for a market where you are first, with no prior sophisticated competitors, in four steps:
  1. Keep it simple and direct; do not be fancy.
  2. Use either the need or the claim as the headline, nothing more.
  3. Dramatise the claim in the copy so it is as powerful as possible.
  4. Bring in the product and prove that it works.

  The prospects have no previous information, so once interested they become more enthusiastic, believe more and buy more readily. [L0330.049]
- Being first can come from a technology breakthrough that makes a new product, a radically better product, or a familiar product at a very low price. It can also come from applying an established product to a market it never served, reaching an unused medium, or discovering a hidden performance that opens a bigger market. [L0330.050]

### Second stage: enlarge the claim

- When competitors already make a direct claim that still works, you are in the second stage. Copy the successful claim and enlarge it, pushing it to the outer edge of what is legal and believable, and outbid the competition on size. [L0330.051]
- The second stage eventually breaks down. Headlines grow longer and larger, words lose meaning, readers discount claims by about half, regulators start to investigate, and sales curves turn down. The sign to change approach is when enlargement stops working and piles on promise instead of proof. [L0330.053]

### Later stages: identification and mechanism

- At the fifth stage the field looks exhausted: the market no longer believes the advertising, and competitors leave en masse. Revival comes through identification, not promise or mechanism. The copy brings the prospect into the ad through his own self-image and the roles he wants to play. [L0330.057]
- When the market no longer believes product claims, the copy has to sell an identity instead of features. This is the hardest form of marketing. A brand that ties the product to who the buyer wants to be can break out of pure claim-based competition. [L0516.013]
- The cigarette industry passed through all five stages, which is a classic case:
  - **Stage 1:** ads focused on pleasure and taste with direct claims.
  - **Stage 2:** they grew by comparisons the market could not measure.
  - **Stage 3:** they introduced mechanisms, such as toasting processes or longer filters.
  - **Stage 4:** competitors copied these mechanisms and elaborated on them.
  - **Stage 5 (early 1950s):** with the government restricting health claims, motivation research showed brands could sell with visual identification alone, such as rugged, virile men in the ads.

  The same industry faced two side challenges. First, it opened a market to women by linking a man and a woman in an identification scene with a four-word headline. Second, a cancer scare in the late 1950s caused four reactions: holders and pipes failed, the industry funded its own research, sales dipped, and a new market for filter cigarettes opened. That market went through the same five stages in turn, returning to flavour in the fifth stage. [L0330.094]
- Sources differ on the fourth-stage response. Source C says to elaborate on a secondary claim, since the main claim has already been made [L0497.018]. The Schwartz table says to elaborate on the mechanism and prove it works differently [L0613.014]. The cigarette case shows competitors copying and elaborating mechanisms at this stage [L0330.094].

### Positioning response per sophistication level

Schwartz scale: market sophistication levels and the response each calls for.

| Level | Market state | Positioning response |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | New product with little competition | Be first, be direct, state the claim simply |
| 2 | Competitors claim similar benefits | Enlarge and extend the claim |
| 3 | Market saturated with similar claims | Introduce a new mechanism |
| 4 | Market disillusioned with previous claims | Elaborate on the mechanism and prove it works differently |
| 5 | Market highly educated and skeptical | Use identity marketing: connect to who they are |

Rate the market's level 1-5 with explicit evidence, and rate customer awareness level 1-5 with a detailed analysis. [L0613.014]

### Mapping the message: formulas and patterns

- **TMO message formula:** describe what the prospect has at the moment, framed neutrally and not as a negative; then what they want to achieve; then present your product as the way to get there. Example: *You travel a lot and have no time for the gym? Keep your strength training in the hotel room with these resistance bands for $39.95.* [L0643.005]
- **Outcome formula:** state the result the customer wants, then name the life outcome it unlocks, such as freedom, security, time, status or care for family. Speaking to the unlocked outcome is more powerful than describing the result alone.
  - Example: *A five-minute morning routine that clears your inbox means you leave work on time for your kids' school pickup.*
  - Second example: *A bookkeeping tool that cuts monthly admin to one hour means you stop dreading tax season and sleep better.* [L0993.014]
- **Fear-of-falling-behind formula:** promise the look or result the buyer already wants, then attach the fear of falling behind a current trend or technology. It names both the desire and the threat to that desire, so the viewer feels the message is about their own tension.
  - Example: *Keep your home office looking polished without falling behind on the latest smart-desk setups.*
  - Second example: *Look timeless at the gym without missing the newest fitness-tracking features everyone else is using.* [L0993.024]
- **Ask-the-customer prompt:**

```
Before you came across (BRAND), which methods were you relying on to deal with (PROBLEM), and what made them fall short? Goal: map the solutions the customer already knows, how aware and advanced they are, where rival products let them down, and the precise language they use to describe that gap. Apply it to the angle that other options have already let them down.
```
[L0906.007]

- **Marketing angle pattern:** a marketing angle is the same product presented through a different buyer motivation. Build it as: target audience, their specific situation, and images, description and reviews that speak to that situation.
  - Example 1: wireless earbuds for runners, with sweat-proof visuals.
  - Example 2: the same earbuds for commuters, with visuals of train travel and noise reduction. [L1219.036]
- The same product can perform very differently by angle, because the wrong audience can hide a good product. Test several angles before dropping a product. [L1219.035]
- The marketing angle and the offer decide profitability. A product can fail with one offer and work after the offer and the interest targeting are changed. [L1176.034]
- Case: a necklace product spent about 1,000 USD on ads and returned about 600 USD in sales, and it was not profitable at first. The offer was weak and the targeting used broad celebrity interests with a very diverse audience. After changing the offer and switching to niche interests tied to a specific community in the US, the product became profitable, though the break-even ROAS remained a constraint. [L1176.035]
- Test niche interest targeting before broad celebrity interests, since narrow, specific audiences can outperform wide ones. [L1176.036]
- A broad hook such as "stay warm this winter" often fails. Narrow the message to a specific audience, for example cyclists or football fans, using the same product, and test several niches to find the one that converts. [L1316.029]
- Use a holiday gifting angle for gift products, and show what buyers gift it for in ads and on the storefront. Keep gifting messaging running through the holiday period. [L1225.085]

### Choosing a segment

- Market segmentation means splitting a broad market into smaller groups of people who share characteristics or needs, so each group can receive a focused message it responds to more strongly. Segmenting still helps in a saturated market, because a narrower angle can stand out among many competing offers. [L0993.001]
- Targeting a huge group, such as every snowboarder or every motorcycle rider, dilutes the audience and makes the message generic. Narrow the group by context, problem or need instead. [L0993.002]
- Example: a pet hair removal brush sold to everyone competes with countless similar brushes. Positioning it for German Shepherd owners, whose dogs shed heavily and lie on the bed, gives one specific problem to speak to: a dirty bed and flaring allergies. The ad can carry this most specific message even if the whole store is not built around it. [L0993.003]
- Example: a generic ad for a watch case that says it keeps all your watches in a box speaks to anyone. A message aimed at frequent travellers who must match a watch to each outfit on a trip, for example to Dubai, is more potent because it names a context and a need. [L0993.004]
- A company with a small budget should not fight larger rivals for the whole market. It should choose a smaller segment defined by social profile rather than geography. That choice then sets the headline, the copy, the proof and the media, such as specialist magazines and recommendations from doctors instead of drugstore shelves. [L0330.099]
- When one desire splits into sub-groups with different reasons for wanting it, keep the core appeal but change the mechanism for each group. For example, weight loss for appearance favours speed and ease of results, while weight loss for health favours safety and lasting results. [L0330.098]
- Once a campaign to a narrow segment succeeds, widen it to the larger market and use the segment's results as the point of difference. Concrete results from the first group, such as how much more often the group relied on the product than on alternatives, give the wider campaign a reason to be believed. [L0330.100]
- A persona for a motorcycle brand targeted carefree, somewhat rebellious riders who reject conventional gear styles. Speaking to that attitude gave the message more pull than a general message aimed at every motorcyclist. [L0450.038]
- Choosing a market based on products alone, or focusing research on product lists, leads to weak markets. Start from the customer and the interest that brings them together. *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)* [L0771.061]
- Store-level segment rules *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)*:
  - A store that tries to appeal to every segment often appeals to none. Choosing one segment and serving its interests better than any competitor works, and the segment must be large enough, which depends on the market size. [L0905.005]
  - Segmentation can be based on product style instead of how the buyer uses the product. The principle is the same: one defined group of buyers is served directly. [L0905.006]
  - Personal familiarity with the target customer's hobby or lifestyle helps but is not required. The owner of a successful focused store had no personal interest in the product's use. [L0905.008]
  - A segment-focused store still sells to buyers outside that segment. The focus means it captures most of that segment's traffic and sales compared with other stores. [L0905.024]
  - When a store is built around one buyer segment, the content, design and social posts all need to address that segment's specific use, so a visitor recognises the site as made for them. Competitors that speak to everyone are then easier to stand out from each time that buyer finds the store. [L0905.028]
- A reseller selling another brand's product faces competitors selling the same product. If a competitor captures a buyer's attention at the Problem Aware stage and guides them through the journey, that buyer is unlikely to switch to another seller later. *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)* [L0904.017]

### Niching down

- Niche down and build a brand instead of running a general store, because specialist stores compete on research and expertise rather than price. Buyers in specialist niches often buy from the store where they have researched, instead of comparing prices on Amazon or Walmart. [L0554.020]
- Procedure for turning a general store into a niche brand:
  1. Run ads for several products in the general store and track which one sells.
  2. Once one product is clearly winning, focus the store on it.
  3. Buy a new domain that fits that product.
  4. Create a new logo for the product's niche.
  5. Build a niche store around the winner and its variants. [L0562.024]
- Pick a niche and build the store around a single product category, because a specialised store makes the core products clearer and easier to market than a general store. [L1147.023]
- Specialise each store around a single niche to become the recognised expert, which large generalist marketplaces cannot match. Niche specialisation also raises conversion rates. *(older advice, Source A 2020)* [L1111.094]
- A niche store focused on one customer type can serve that customer's needs better than a generalist such as Amazon can. Competing on this focus is realistic even against much larger companies. *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)* [L0771.060]
- Specialise the store on one product area and the buying experience around it. A tightly focused store with a clear identity converts better than a general store with mixed categories. *(older advice, Source A 2021)* [L0801.174]
- If a single product type meets all research criteria, build a dedicated store for that product type to become the authority for it. Broaden to a wider niche store only if the narrow product type fails the criteria. [L0934.025]
- When a niche is sold across several niches on competitor sites, a sub-niche can be split out and owned as its own niche, which lets you become the recognised authority in that specific niche. [L0835.129]
- Combining two niches, such as personalised jewellery with a religious identity, can create stronger customer identification and a more distinctive offer. This works when the two niches appeal to the same buyer. [L1172.049]

### Brand-dominated niches

- A seller usually cannot compete in brand-dominated niches. Big manufacturers are unlikely to grant authorised retailer status to a small store. Even with authorisation, large retailers such as Best Buy, Target and Costco sell these products at a scale a small store cannot match. [L0988.003]
- Leading manufacturers in brand-dominated niches spend hundreds of millions or billions of dollars on distribution and marketing to win shoppers. A new seller cannot outspend them, so this is a reason to avoid those niches. [L0988.004]
- Brand preference is acceptable; brand loyalty is the problem. A shopper who simply prefers one brand in a niche with many options is fine. A niche is a problem when most buyers think of the same few brands first for that product type. [L0988.005]

### Identity-driven and lifestyle marketing

- When a product is a commodity, such as shoes or a period belt, marketing must be identity-driven rather than feature-driven. [L0497.019]
- Case: a heated period belt that vibrates. The target audience is women under 50 with severe period pain, who may take time off work; further segmentation is possible. The belt entered a market with many belts already on sale, so it sits at the fourth stage of market sophistication. The brand must create new features or mechanisms and enlarge its claims to show it solves problems earlier solutions could not. [L0497.020]
- Male grooming is a narrower market than female beauty, which is larger and more crowded. Marketing to men needs a tone that fits masculine identity, not generic beauty language. *(Source C, undated)* [L0524.011]
- Fashion and passion products can be sold on aspirational needs such as self-expression, status and looking good, rather than on a problem to solve. Show how the product looks and what style it gives the buyer. [L1315.037]
- A brand can sell the lifestyle and identity around a product instead of the product itself, as energy drink brands do when they feature motocross and surfing. Viewers buy the feeling the brand connects to, so ads should show that world, not only the item's features. [L1426.042]
- A brand's jewellery or fashion products can be positioned as a form of self-expression, which connects strongly with a defined audience. Content about the meaning of the piece adds value and helps the brand stand out. [L1439.015]

## Positioning, USP and differentiation

### Work order: define the business before the product

- Start with the business differentiator. It is the most important input for finding your place in the market, so define it before choosing brand perception, creatives or messaging. [L1267.002]
- Know your products well enough to name two or three facts every buyer should understand. These points feed directly into your messaging and ad angles. [L1268.005]
- Before any creative is made, define the USP (unique selling point) and the ICP (ideal customer profile), because every creative decision depends on them. [L1019.002]
- Before building any ad, write one sentence that states who the product is for and which pain point it removes. [L1019.003]
- Judge each competitor from the buyer's perspective. Imagine a shopper with your site and two competitor sites open, and ask which concrete advantages would make that shopper choose you. [L0894.006]
- A new opportunity is only worth testing if you can name a unique selling proposition for it. If you cannot, the idea is not unique. Generic claims such as eating healthy and exercising, or investing in the stock market, do not qualify. [L1365.003]
- Choosing a positioning approach: pick one of five options. For each, give concrete implementation examples, the risks, examples from comparable markets, and the proof it needs. [L0613.015]
  1. A new mechanism: explain in depth what makes it distinct.
  2. A superior product: name the differentiators and proof points.
  3. Identity marketing aimed at a specific group: spell out the identity markers and emotional connection points.
  4. Depositioning competitors: pinpoint their weaknesses to target.
  5. Repositioning toward a different desire: name the alternative desires to satisfy.

### Defining the USP

- The USP is the set of reasons a business gives customers to buy from it. Not every business needs one. It may be a single statement or a multi-point list. [L0893.016]
- The USP should state why a customer should buy from this business instead of a competitor. It should focus on the benefits of buying from the business, in addition to the product's own benefits, such as shipping, returns or price guarantees. [L1310.021]
- USPs are the unique selling propositions a brand repeats in its ads. In one example they were a 90-day money-back guarantee, a microcrystal technology as the main mechanism, bundle pricing under $40, two formulations for different skin needs, and bundle savings. [L0374.019]
- Make the USP obvious across the whole store, including the About Us page, product pages, images, graphics and all marketing. Visitors should understand within seconds what makes the store different. [L0413.023]
- Make the USP visible everywhere in the store, not on the About page alone. [L0413.023]
- The USP is the first element to dissect when analysing competitors, followed by their other marketing elements. Your own USP should be defined against what those competitors already claim, so that it stands apart. [L0898.005]
- A USP that matches competitors' USPs is not a USP, because the U stands for unique. Compare your USP against every competitor's and change it until it is distinct. [L0893.017]
- A unique selling angle is the main reason a customer buys from you instead of a competitor. Options include:
  - a lower price;
  - higher quality for buyers with more purchasing power;
  - more colours and sizes;
  - a different focus, for example sustainability instead of comfort;
  - above all, a strong offer such as BOGO (buy one, get one), buy two get one free, or bundles. [L1183.017]
- Be specific: a clearly stated, honest difference is what makes an offer stand out. [L1183.017]
- Define yourself by the outcomes you deliver, not by a single service category. A label such as one service category sets expectations about price and scope, while outcomes let you set your own pricing. [L0378.101]
- Being different is often easier and more effective than being better. Use a distinctive trait that sets you apart, as long as it is honest and relevant to buyers. [L0378.102]
- Find a seeming advantage even when products are similar. Discover a specific feature, process or fact that competitors have not yet told the market, and lead with that point. [L1261.116]
- Brand differentiation comes from good customer support, fast shipping, a clear niche and a visible story. Together these let a store charge more than competitors selling the same product. [L0871.059]
- If you copy a competitor, still beat them with better branding, a better landing page, a better offer and better ads. The copied product alone does not guarantee sales. [L1177.035]
- Differentiate beyond the base product when possible. Methods include:
  - a custom formula or unique composition;
  - premium packaging instead of generic plastic;
  - bonus items included;
  - superior quality in a specific area.
  Even small differentiators justify premium pricing. [L0227.029]

#### USP formulas and patterns

- USP formula: "[Brand] is the [category] that [specific differentiator], focused on [result the customer gets]." It names one clear reason to choose this brand over alternatives. [L1071.008]
  - Example: "Lumen Shoes is the running shoe brand that uses recycled foam, focused on lighter, longer-lasting training."
  - Example: "Kettle Coffee is the morning coffee brand that roasts in small batches, focused on fresher taste every day."
- Multi-point USP formula: a headline claim followed by three to five short points. Each point proves a specific advantage such as speed, range, price or support. Each point should be specific and checkable so it is clearly different from competitors. [L0893.035]
  - Example: "Same-day dispatch, free sample kits, and a 60-day fit guarantee" (footwear brand).
  - Example: "Vegan ingredients, refillable packs, and carbon-neutral shipping" (skincare store).
- Single-idea headlines and multi-point USPs apply to different places. A headline has room for one main idea, and each further thought must hold the reader to pull them into the next sentence. [L0330.013]
- A multi-point USP block lists several checkable advantages. Use it where the USP is shown as a list, and keep the headline to one idea. [L0330.013, L0893.035]

### Primary promise, hook and proof points
