- The primary promise is a headline that directly reflects the number one problem the customer wants solved when considering the product. It usually leads to the product's number one benefit, because buyers pay for the improvement in their life rather than for features. [L0416.004]
- The primary marketing promise is the main thing a business says it will deliver to customers. It is similar to the hook, so record it separately from the USP and the hook. [L0893.029]
- The hook is a one-line phrase, often a tagline, that is memorable but is not the USP. If a business has none, record no hook rather than inventing one. [L0893.028]
- Hook and promise formula: a short phrase that names the customer benefit in one or two plain words, such as ease or speed. The short wording is easy to repeat and remember across the whole site. [L0893.034]
  - Example: "Fast fixes" (repair service).
  - Example: "Simple sizing" (clothing store).
- First-line USP pattern: state the single most valuable selling point as a short, punchy headline. Choose between tangible value (free or fast shipping, a 30-day guarantee) and ownership claims (local or family owned). The shopper reads the value before the brand. [L0567.020]
  - Example: "Free delivery on every order over $80."
  - Example: "Made by a family studio, dispatched within 24 hours."
- Positioning formula: name a specific customer and the frustration they already feel. Show that your product removes it safely, and tie the benefit to an emotional need such as control, competence or relationship. Buyers respond to a message that mirrors their own situation and fears more than to a feature list. [L0395.006]
  - Example A: "Remote workers with cluttered desks: a compact cable organizer that ends the daily search for chargers, so they feel in control of their workspace."
  - Example B: "First-time home bakers who keep burning bread: a digital proofing timer that tells them when dough is ready, so they bake with confidence."
- The brand message should answer why the product is the preferred choice over every other option the buyer could pick, including doing nothing. Prepare a short summary sentence that states that reason in one line. [L0395.007]
- Proof points are evidence that backs up a business's claims. Examples include customer review counts and average ratings, the number of items shipped, or years in business. Big, bold proof points carry more weight than small ones. Record them with how they are displayed. [L0893.018]
- Treat every proof point as a claim that needs checking. A high rating with a large review count is strong evidence only if the reviews look genuine. [L0893.043]
- Founding year and trading history are proof of longevity and trust. A store may use its founding year, years of trading and family ownership as proof. Record the founding year and how prominently the history is told, because established stores often rely on this kind of proof. [L0893.044]
- Catalogue size is a proof point of range. A store can show the number of products, for example in the search box. Record such counts next to the other proof points so the range claim can be compared across competitors. [L0893.045]
- Statistical data works well as a USP on landing pages and ads, because people respond to figures. For example, a survey result showing a percentage of people losing productivity from a problem. [L1158.023]
- Build credibility with a verifiable figure about the product's content or quality, such as a percentage of actual ingredient or measured output. Buyers trust a measurable comparison more than a general claim of quality. [L1369.022]
- Pairing a customer count with a question, such as why thousands of people choose the product, adds social proof and sets up the comparison with competitors. [L1207.036]
- Compare your product with competitors on the points where you win. When you frame yourself as better than the alternatives, your brand looks stronger to the shopper. [L1207.035]
- Once a store has more reviews than its competitors, the review count and rating become part of its unique selling point. For example, claim to be the highest-rated seller of a product. A large review base also raises the valuation of the business when it is sold. [L1012.006]
- Example (older, Source B 2021-22): a large cosmetics brand was reported to have about 16,500 reviews, most of them five-star. It built that volume into its core positioning. A very large, mostly positive review base can become a headline selling point. [L1012.007]

### Unique mechanism and product-level USP

- The unique mechanism acts as a product-level USP and complements the USP of the business as a whole. Each product should have its own small USP, and the mechanism is a large part of it. [L0465.006]
- In a crowded market, a product needs a new mechanism or feature to stand out. For example, a dish brush with a built-in soap reservoir offers a different mechanism than a standard brush for the same job. [L0644.039]
- A new mechanism explains how the new opportunity is delivered and why only your solution works. The reader must believe only your method can meet their need. Prospects have usually heard every claim, so a mechanism that is new is what makes a familiar promise feel possible again. [L1365.006]
- Typical mechanisms by niche:
  - financial offers: a proprietary algorithm behind a trading system;
  - business-opportunity offers: a mentorship programme teaching a new kind of online business;
  - health: a specific ingredient combination in a single pill. [L1365.007]
- Example: a dog hair removal brush enters a crowded pet grooming market. Its new mechanism is a static pick-up that lifts hair into a holder for easy disposal, which sets it apart from older spiky brushes. [L1426.037]
- Naming formula: [memorable branded name that describes the payoff] for the new mechanism, used in the headline, proof and offer. A short label gives the reader something specific to remember and repeat, and ties the mechanism to the benefit. [L1365.051]
  - Example 1: "Morning Margin Tracker", a daily dashboard that shows profit per product before the store opens.
  - Example 2: "Idle Cash Routine", a weekly transfer method that moves unused balances into interest-earning accounts.
- Work-out procedure for product mapping (one row per product, columns in this order): [L0416.021]

| Column | What to write |
|---|---|
| Product name | The exact product name |
| What is the Primary Problem this product solves? | The single main problem buyers want solved, which becomes the basis of the primary promise |
| What are the Secondary Problems this product solves? | Other problems the product solves, used as fallback headline angles |
| What is/are the Unique Mechanism(s) of the product? | The feature or technology that makes the product different |
| How does the Unique Mechanism work? | A plain explanation of how the mechanism works, with any numbers that have evidence |
| What are the product's features? | List of the product's features |
| What are the benefits of each feature? | For each feature, the benefit it gives the buyer and why it matters |

- Analysing a product's performances (Source C): [L0330.012]
  1. List every performance the product delivers to the buyer, such as transport, dependability, economy, power, recognition, value and novelty.
  2. Group each performance under the mass desire it satisfies.
  3. Feature the single performance that will bring the most sales power at that time. Use the others only to reinforce it.
- A dominant performance must be squeezed for all its power in the ad. The reader must be convinced that only this product delivers that satisfaction. [L0330.013]
- Worked example (older, Source B 2021-22): an infrared sauna for two people. Its mapped primary problems were health benefits such as better sleep, relaxation, circulation, relief from joint and muscle pain, and better skin. The secondary problem was using a sauna at home rather than paying to travel to one. Its unique mechanism was a full-spectrum infrared heating system producing 1000 W, which the manufacturer claimed is 25 times the output of the closest competitor. [L0416.022]
- Product facts used in the infrared sauna copy, all as the manufacturer states them (older, Source B 2021-22): [L0416.025]
  - full-spectrum heater output of 1000 W;
  - electromagnetic field of 1 mg against a recommended maximum of 3 mg;
  - 360-degree heating;
  - non-toxic materials;
  - lifetime warranty;
  - chromotherapy lighting;
  - assembly by two people in about one hour;
  - reservation mode that preset start up to 36 hours in advance;
  - Bluetooth and AUX audio;
  - app control via Android tablet or smartphone;
  - made from Eco-certified grade A cedar or basswood.
- When selling your own product, list its features and deliverables and compare them with competitors' lists. This matters less for resellers, but features mentioned beyond the USP are still worth recording. [L0893.030]
- If the attribute has no name yet, create one. A self-coined name that no competitor uses makes even a similar attribute look unique to the customer. [L0465.012]
- Perception is decisive. A customer who sees a named technology that competitors do not name will believe the product is different, even if the underlying attribute is similar. Name the mechanism clearly and present it consistently across the product page and packaging. [L0465.013]
- Sell a concept (positioning, big idea, unique selling proposition, gimmick), not the bare product. The concept is what makes the product stand out, and the product becomes the vehicle for it. [L0366.021, L1366.103]
- Exception: a genuinely new product can be its own concept. Early ads can focus on its features. [L1366.022]
- Once the category gets crowded, each ad needs a distinct angle that differentiates the product through a concept. Examples are the thinnest version, the most premium version, or a new manufacturing method. [L1366.022, L1366.104]
- A technical production detail that explains why a product is special can become the headline concept. Ask the engineers how a feature is made and why competitors cannot copy it, because that answer often contains the strongest angle. [L1366.023]
- Two products that sell well can be combined under a single concept headline. One ad then promotes both and draws attention to the catalogue. The shared concept should describe what both products achieve for the buyer. [L1366.024, L1366.107]
- The concept a buyer perceives depends on price. Identical copy can read as serious electronics at a high price, a sophisticated gadget at a middle price, and a toy at a low price. Set the price with the intended concept in mind. [L1366.027]
- Example: a pocket CB radio sold at 39.95 read as a serious electronic product similar to a full-size CB radio. At 29.95 it read as a sophisticated walkie-talkie, and at 19.95 as a toy, with almost the same copy. Use these price bands as a check on which concept a price communicates. [L1366.028]
- Adding a recognisable ingredient or variant to a basic product can give it a distinct angle against plain versions. Examples are honey, lemon, avocado or berries in ice cubes. Test the variant against the plain version rather than assuming the twist will sell. [L0657.050]
- Licensing a known franchise or an established brand differentiates a product from copycats. It borrows that brand's audience and authority. The value depends on how large a stake the licensing partner takes. [L0524.012]
- A made-in-USA positioning should be repeated across the logo, product pages, about page, mission statement, exit popups and social media banners. A single mention on the about page is not enough to convert. [L0359.061]

### Competitor analysis

#### Research procedures

- Competitor research for offer optimisation (Source B 2021-22, older): [L0898.004]
  1. Identify the direct competitors in your market and the offers your target customers already buy from them.
  2. Document each competitor's offer and marketing elements in a competition research worksheet.
  3. Dissect each competitor's USP (unique selling proposition) and the other marketing elements they use.
  4. Identify the additional value each competitor gives customers beyond the core product.
  5. Compare these findings with your own offer to find where yours is unique or can become unique.
  6. Keep the completed worksheet at hand and use it when you plan conversion rate optimisation changes.
- The competitor analysis worksheet is filled with data from your main competitors, gathered from their websites and from traffic tools. Include the questions about their USP and email marketing, even though some competitors will have no answer for them. (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0441.009]
- Competitive analysis for a unique value proposition (Source A 2019, older): [L0243.011]
  1. Identify the top three direct competitor websites selling the same products.
  2. Review each competitor's product pages, about page, shipping policy and return policy comprehensively.
  3. Document what all competitors are doing (e.g., all charge $1,000 with free shipping).
  4. Identify gaps competitors are not filling or mentioning (e.g., estimated ship date, delivery timeframe).
  5. Identify what competitors offer but do not highlight (e.g., a one-year warranty that competitors never mention).
  6. Build unique value by addressing competitor gaps: provide estimated ship and arrival dates, offer an extended warranty, include bonus gifts with orders.
- Competitor research for a product in Google Shopping: [L1508.032]
  1. Search the product term in Google Shopping and note which sellers appear.
  2. Open the websites of the top sellers and review their offers, bundles, design and pop-ups.
  3. Look for a gap, such as no unique offer, and build a stronger offer on your own store.
  4. Differentiate with your own photos, since identical photos make a product look identical to shoppers.
- Competitor check via Google Shopping and the competitor's store (Source A 2021, older): [L1112.084]
  1. Open the competitor's store and their Google Shopping listing for the product.
  2. Note what they do well, such as photos, price, promotions and product page content.
  3. Note what they do poorly or leave out.
  4. Write down what you would change on your own product page to beat them.
- Brand Tracker lists, per brand, the Ad Copies, Ad Headlines, Ad Hooks, Landing Pages, Personas, Concepts, Angles, Desires, Emotions, Awareness Stages, USPs and Ad Rank. Use these tabs to see how an advertiser frames offers, proof and urgency. [L1164.029]
- Top e-commerce brands with strong creative execution to study include Gymshark, Snocks, Obvi, Spacegoods, Lion's, TRX and Angles90. Reverse-engineer their ad frameworks and angles across platforms for patterns applicable to your niche. [L0258.025]
- The top data of a tracker gives four shortcuts: use top USPs to skip angle testing, top landing pages to copy proven funnel structures, top personas to find new audiences for the same product, and top desires to build a product around a need that already sells. [L0374.020]
- When studying a direct-to-consumer competitor, list what it does well and what it does poorly, such as an outdated website or no ads. Build your store to beat it on the weak points. Shoppers often care more about a stronger offer than about how long a competitor has been in business. [L0857.074]
- Example procedure for a downloadable sewing pattern with 919 total ads: it led with Instant PDF Download, Beginner Friendly and Step-By-Step Instructions as main USPs, with Promotion / Download Pattern as its top concept. Digital product sellers can position on instant access and skill level in the same way. [L0892.040]

#### Reading competitors

- Judge each competitor by matching the basics first. Match the basic features that the best competitor already offers, such as high-quality photos, displayed free shipping, estimated delivery, live chat, a price match policy and financing. Then add differentiators that the competitor lacks. [L0894.007]
- Recurring complaints about a competitor's failure point are an opening. Position your brand on a better product, better service or a clear fix for that exact problem. [L1164.027]
- Turn recurring cons from competitor reviews into your own selling points. If many listings share one complaint, address it openly on your page as a fix, which shows you listened to customers. [L1158.026]
- Use Amazon review pros as copy for landing pages and ads, and use cons to identify what customers are not getting. If cons dominate, the product is a poor choice, so consider switching products. [L1183.016]
- Identify common complaints in competitor reviews. Research all competitor reviews in the product category, identify the common complaints in negative reviews, and design your product to overcome these specific complaints. [L0227.028]
  - Example: if competitors' spatulas fall apart in the dishwasher, make yours dishwasher-safe and market this feature.
  - Highlight these improvements in marketing and listing copy. [L0227.028]
- Limitations that buyers mention about competing products reveal gaps you can fill. For example, a brush that is not suitable for knit fabrics means a product that is safe on upholstery and furniture can make that exact point its unique selling point. [L0450.037]
- Look at the parts of a competitor's offer. A competitor's product is not your only lever. [L0502.030]
- If a competitor sells the same product, differentiate through the parts of the offer: better reviews, better photos, ebooks, detailed descriptions, video tutorials, guarantees, a better checkout and better local payment providers. Each of these raises the perceived likelihood of achievement, lowers the perceived time delay or effort in the buyer's mind. [L0502.030]
- Compare each competitor's product pages and policies, not just their prices, to find where a gap exists. [L0243.011, L0898.004]

### Differentiating without the price fight

#### Identical products and low-price competitors

- When competitors sell identical products at lower prices, compete on product page quality and customer service, not price. Optimise product pages for trust with reviews, descriptions, live chat and a phone number. Launch high-quality blog content and build an email list. [L0274.042]
- Low-margin competitors that cut prices typically provide poor customer service because their margins are unsustainable. They accumulate negative reviews and fail within years. Build sustainable margin to fund good service, which builds long-term competitive advantage. [L0274.041]
- When suppliers enforce MAP (minimum advertised price), competing stores show the same price. Shoppers then choose on other factors: product information quality, responsive support, phone answers, bonus gifts, coupons and retargeting. [L0966.096]
- Stores that undercut MAP usually fail long term, because they run on thin margins, struggle to serve customers and collect bad reviews. [L0966.097]
- Under MAP, differentiate the store with extras that do not lower the advertised price, such as bonus gifts, white-glove shipping or extended warranties. (Source A 2019, older) [L1069.055]
- Include bonus items to beat competitors on perceived value. Three bonus e-books with professional covers (e.g., $20 on Fiverr) instead of basic text PDFs, plus a $50 gift card for a complementary category (e.g., weight-loss products for resistance-band customers) with a diagram explaining how to redeem it. Competitors offering only one e-book or no bonuses cannot compete on perceived value. [L0228.024]
- Differentiate by identifying common complaints in competitor reviews and solving them. Example: if competitors' products generate odour, include a mesh bag with air holes. If customers ask for extra handles for couples, negotiate a small cost increase to include them and highlight this feature in your listing. [L0228.023]
- Do not run negative ads or attack rivals. This looks unfair and is usually not permitted in respected media. Position the brand on its own benefits instead. [L1261.115]
- When a rival is a cheaper substitute, do not warn readers against substitutes. Invite them to try the rivals too. This signals confidence, and buyers who compare will still choose the brand whose advantage is real. [L1261.148]
- Large cheap marketplaces such as Temu compete on price and delivery speed. A dropshipper differentiates by promoting specific products tied to a specific customer problem, building a store around that product, offering better customer service, and building a brand with ads. [L1216.030]
- Competing only on price against large marketplaces such as Temu, AliExpress or Amazon is a weak position for a dropshipper. Compete on trust, customer service and a focused product story instead. [L1216.031]

#### Niche stores and specialisation

- A niche-specific store can position itself as an expert in its industry. Broad marketplaces such as Wayfair cannot do this, so selling the same product types is not a problem. (Source A 2019, older) [L1068.075]
- A niche-specific store positions the business as the authority in that niche. Large competitors such as Wayfair are not a reason to avoid a niche, because the store's focus is the niche itself. [L0970.110]
- A niche store competes with large general sites such as Amazon or Wayfair by becoming the expert in one niche. It gives pre-sale product knowledge that generalist stores do not offer. Even a fraction of a percent of a giant's sales can be a multi-seven-figure store. [L0805.050]
- Brands in the niche can be used to pick a specific store positioning, such as premium-tier products. A premium position supports higher prices and fewer price-driven shoppers. (Source A 2021, older) [L0801.176]
- Diff Eyewear is a sunglasses brand that manufactures its own products rather than dropshipping. It sells through large retail stores in the United States and runs a Shopify store focused only on sunglasses. This shows a brand can build strength by specialising deeply in one product type. [L0838.015]
- A long-term positioning is customer service and building a brand that customers return to. Stating this in supplier conversations builds trust, because suppliers care most about whether the business will treat customers well. (Source A 2018, older) [L1085.094]

#### Offer, service and identity

- Perceived value can justify a higher price through faster shipping, more generous return policies, round-the-clock customer support or a money-back guarantee. Show these benefits clearly on the store. [L0436.014]
- Position products around identity and community instead of appearance alone. A map necklace can be sold as a way to show pride in one's heritage, and a star-sign piece as self-expression. This gives buyers a reason beyond looking good. [L0549.036]
- Framing your product against weaker competitor options on the points where you are stronger makes your brand look better by comparison. This is related to the halo effect, where people trust a brand more when it looks good overall. It is a separate principle, so use competitor comparison deliberately rather than assuming it works the same way. [L1207.049]
- Give buyers a reason to choose you over alternatives by identifying where you win. Use the win points in your comparison, not a generic claim of quality. [L1207.035, L1207.049]

### Polarising and complaint-based positioning

- Build a brand around one customer complaint in this order (Source A): [L0334.057]
  1. Find the single biggest complaint or reason people hesitate before buying, by polling customers, email subscribers, or people who know the industry.
  2. Fix that complaint in the product or offer, then take the opposite stance in the brand message.
  3. State the fix boldly on the homepage and the most-visited pages, not only in a footnote.
  4. Repeat the same message in ads, emails, blog content and video testimonials.
- Solve-Own-Reinforce method (Source A): [L0334.001]
  1. Solve: identify the single biggest complaint or resistance point before the first purchase. Ask customers, email subscribers or people in the industry, e.g. with a poll, and fix it in the product or offer.
  2. Own: state the fix boldly on the website, especially on the homepage and the most-visited pages, so the problem is addressed up front instead of avoided.
  3. Reinforce: repeat the same message at every touchpoint: ads, emails, blog and content, and video testimonials.
- Turn the most common customer complaint into the brand's positioning by taking the opposite stance. Expensive becomes affordable, cheap and short-lasting becomes premium, boring becomes beautiful, slow becomes fast, and hard or complex becomes easiest. [L0334.002]
- Bold category claim pattern: "[Our category] is broken because of [the specific industry problem]; we are the first [brand type] that solves it with [your specific differentiator]." It puts the industry problem at the front of the conversation, so a first-time visitor immediately sees why the brand is different. [L0334.003]
  - Example 1: "Most bakery mixes hide their flour sources, so we are the first bread-mix company to publish the origin of every ingredient on the pack."
  - Example 2: "Most sleep trackers guess at results, so we are the first wearable brand to publish its sensor accuracy against a clinical lab on every product page."
- Polarisation takes a clear stand that splits the market into for and against. It creates attention, traction and free leads, but also draws criticism and complaints. [L0643.011]
  - Formula: "Everything else in [category] sucks, here is why [your option] is the only real choice."
  - Example 1: "Most dog food is marketing fluff. Our recipe is the only one with vet-tested ingredients."
  - Example 2: "Stop buying generic spreadsheets. Use this template built by bookkeepers."
- A strong brand persona is polarising: some people love it and others dislike it. Deliberately attracting one segment and repelling others is better than trying to appeal to everybody. [L0487.006]
- Educational hook formula: state a hidden harm the audience has not linked to their problem. Connect it to a symptom they already feel, then position the product as the fix. The customer learns something new, and the symptom makes it personal. [L0450.034]
  - Example: "Did you know the dust that settles in your carpet could be behind the stuffy nose you wake up with?"
  - Example: "Did you know car seats can hold more pet dander than you think? Itchy eyes on road trips may come from there."
- Reseller and own-brand split for hooks: for a reseller, the product page must carry the store's own USP (why buy from this store) in addition to the product's marketing message. For an own brand, the product itself carries most of the brand message. (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0409.024]

### Perceived uniqueness

- Buyers must be persuaded about the business as well as the product. A great product on a sketchy-looking store will still lose sales, and prospects judge the offer first when choosing between you and a competitor. (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0411.003]
- What the customer perceives is the reality that drives buying. An offer must be perceived as unique and valuable even when parts of it are not truly unique. Perception must always stay honest and never claim things that are untrue. (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0411.004]
- Uniqueness can come from actual uniqueness or from the perception of it. Honest differentiation, such as a clearly stated, stronger policy, can shift perception without any lying. (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0411.010]
- A named element, such as a branded policy or framework name, makes an offer sound unique and creates curiosity, which makes buyers more likely to engage. Naming should stay truthful and apply to things that are actually real. (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0411.013]
- When a product looks much like rivals, the maker's name and track record can become the main reason to buy. Name the maker and state its record for quality, because buyers weigh the integrity behind a product when the product itself is hard to tell apart. [L1369.116]
- A soap maker had its flakes analysed against competing brands. The lab found rivals contained about 15 percent water, while its own product contained about 5 percent. Laundries bought soap by the barrel. A laundry paying a barrel price about 10 percent higher for pure soap still paid the same per pound of actual soap, and the extra freight charged on the barrel made the competitor's effective premium larger. The seller mailed every laundry in its territory asking whether it was buying soap or water, and suggested it could get its own water from the faucet rather than pay freight for it. The mailing caused a sharp reaction among competitors and won the seller a much larger account for its own business. [L1369.021]

### Price and premium perception

#### Premium justification

- Price shapes how customers perceive a brand. Selling a $1,000 flagship phone for $400 turns it into a $400 phone in the public's eyes and lowers the brand's perceived value. [L1422.009]
- Brand presentation can justify a higher price. A branded, well-designed store can charge more than a cheap competitor and still convert well, because customers do not always equate a low price with a good product. [L1172.048]
- Brand differentiation lets a store charge more than competitors selling the same product when it has good support, fast shipping, a clear niche and a visible story. [L0871.059]
- Example (Source C): a bathroom cleaner sold at about 80 euros, with a clean, branded product page, a 25 percent offer and a bundle with an accessory. The estimate was that a generic page would support only about 40 euros, which shows how branding can support a higher price. [L0692.042]
- Avoid apologising for a premium price. Frame the product as affluent and worth it, like a gift the buyer is proud to give. [L1016.019]
- Use the value proposition to choose two of three levers: save the customer money, deliver better quality, or save time. Delivering all three is the ideal, but a clear two-lever message is easier to communicate. [L1016.022]
- Premium fashion pricing comes from image and photography. High-end, editorial-style product photos and people with status wearing the item can justify prices several times higher than cheap mass-market competitors. [L0561.023]
- Example: a streetwear varsity jacket priced at 195 pounds was compared with cheap drop-shipping varsity jackets priced at about 28 to 55 pounds. The difference was justified by in-house production, editorial photography and brand association rather than by the product itself. [L0561.024]
- Premium-tier positioning supports higher prices and fewer price-driven shoppers. (Source A 2021, older) [L0801.176]

#### Clothing: price needs a product reason

- A new clothing brand in 2023 should avoid a fast-fashion positioning, since it is a poor fit for building a distinct brand. (Source C, older) [L0779.001]
- A product range with items that do not fit together visually (not cohesive) weakens the brand and should be avoided. [L0779.003]
- Using only a brand name as the design on a garment gives customers no reason to pay a higher price. [L0779.004]
- Every clothing price needs a justification that comes from the product itself, such as design, materials or construction, rather than from the name alone. [L0779.005]

### Mistakes to avoid

- Competing on price alone against large marketplaces is a weak position. Compete on trust, service and a focused product story. [L1216.031]
- Using a USP that matches competitors' USPs gives no reason to buy. [L0893.017]
- Generic claims are not a USP. If you cannot name what is unique, the idea is not worth testing. [L1365.003]
- Inventing a hook where none exists. Record no hook rather than inventing one. [L0893.028]
- Launching a copied product without better branding, landing page, offer and ads. [L1177.035]
- Warning readers against cheaper substitutes or attacking rivals. [L1261.115, L1261.148]

### Summary for operators

- Define the business differentiator, then the USP and the ICP, before any creative. [L1267.002, L1019.002, L1019.003]
- Write the primary promise from the number one problem; the hook is separate and optional. [L0416.004, L0893.028, L0893.029]
- Back claims with proof points that are specific, checkable and displayed. [L0893.018, L0893.043]
- Give each product its own mechanism, named and shown consistently. [L0465.006, L0465.012, L0465.013]
- Use competitor reviews and gaps to choose the fix you own and repeat. [L0227.028, L0334.001, L1158.026]
- Set price with the concept you want to communicate, and back premium prices with the product, service or brand reason. [L1366.027, L1016.022, L0779.005]

#### Further details

- You can sell the same product as competitors by out-branding them and marketing differently. Differentiate through better branding, higher perceived quality, and reaching a different audience segment with a different problem or context for the same product. [L0067.034]

- Sell a concept (positioning, big idea, unique selling proposition, gimmick), not the bare product. The concept is what makes the product stand out, and the product only becomes a sales vehicle for it. [L1366.021]

## Brand foundation: vision, mission, values and the brand document

### Build order and layout of the minimum viable brand document

1. Define the business: write a one-sentence business description, then a brand vision (long-term, ambitious, forward-looking) and a brand mission (why the brand exists, what it does, who it serves). [L1071.001]
2. Identify the target audience using the four pillars: demographics, psychographics, behaviours, and goals, needs and motivations. Base this on the customer research from earlier work. [L1071.001]
3. Develop the unique selling proposition (USP). [L1071.001]
4. List the brand values. [L1071.001]
5. Choose the brand name. [L1071.001]
6. Create the logo concept, colour palette and typography, and assemble them into a brand kit. [L1071.001]
7. Define the brand voice keywords and how the brand should be perceived. [L1071.001]
8. Write the basic brand messaging: tagline, product descriptions, and About Us section. [L1071.001]
9. (Optional) Build a mood board showing the look and feel the brand should have. [L1071.001]

Steps to fill out the minimum viable brand document:

1. Define the business: write the business goals, vision, mission and reason for existing.
2. Identify the target audience: describe demographics, psychographics, and goals, needs and motivations.
3. Develop the unique selling proposition: state what makes the brand different from competitors.
4. Define the brand values: list what the company values publicly, and make them internal as well.
5. Choose the brand name: pick a name that represents the niche, or a general name for a general store.
6. Create the logo, color palette and typography: select the logo concept, colors and typeface that reinforce the brand.
7. Establish the brand voice and tone: choose adjectives that describe how the brand is perceived.
8. Write the brand messaging: define the tagline, copy style and product description approach.
9. Develop a mood board (optional): collect colors, images and graphics that represent the brand.

[L1044.030]

Minimum viable brand document layout:

| Section | What to enter |
|---|---|
| Business | Business goals, vision, mission, reason for existing |
| Target audience | Demographics, psychographics, goals, needs, motivations |
| Unique selling proposition | What makes the brand different when competing with others |
| Brand values | Public-facing values, aligned with internal values |
| Brand name | A name representing the niche or a general name |
| Logo, colors, typography | Logo concept, color palette, typeface |
| Brand voice and tone | Adjectives describing how the brand is perceived |
| Brand messaging | Tagline, copy style, product description approach |
| Mood board (optional) | Colors, images and graphics that represent the brand |

[L1044.031]

Minimum viable brand document, fill-in form (replace each bracket with your own content):

```
Step 1: Define Your Business
Business Description: [One or two sentences stating what the business mainly does: the products or services it designs, makes or sells, and the categories it covers.]
Brand Vision: [Long-term, ambitious, future-oriented goal the brand works toward, written as one clear statement.]
Brand Mission: [Statement of why the brand exists, what it does, and who it serves; it should guide everyday operations and objectives.]

Step 2: Identify Your Target Audience
Demographics: [Gender, age range, income level, and the broadest and most focused groups.]
Psychographics: [Values, interests, personality traits, and what they care about.]
Behaviours: [How they spend time, what they research before buying, how loyal they are, and which communities they join online or offline.]
Goals, Needs and Motivations: [What they want to achieve, what they need from a product, and what community or identity they want to belong to.]

Step 3: USP: [Single statement of what the brand does better than others and why it matters to the customer.]

Step 4: Brand Values: [List of the core values the brand stands for.]

Step 5: Brand Name: [Chosen name and the meaning or association it creates.]

Step 6: Logo Concept: [Description of the logo idea and what it communicates about the product or brand.]
Colour Palette: [Colours used and the feeling they create.]
Typeface: [Font style chosen and the look it gives the brand.]

Step 7: Brand Voice Keywords: [Adjectives describing how the brand speaks.]
Perception Goals: [How the brand should be perceived, e.g. trustworthy, powerful, friendly.]

Step 8: Tagline: [Short, memorable phrase.]
Product Descriptions: [Approach used to describe products: features, benefits, technology.]
About Us: [Short story of the brand's commitment and purpose.]

Step 9 (Optional): Mood Board: [Images and graphics that show the look and feel of the brand.]
```

[L1071.002]

### Vision and mission: the reason the brand exists

- A brand should have a reason for existing, which is expressed through a vision and a mission. The vision is the long-term, ambitious future state the company wants to help create, while the mission states why the business exists, what it does, who it serves and what guides daily operations. [L1044.006]
- The brand vision is a long-term, ambitious, future-oriented statement that acts as the driving force of the company. The mission is a shorter statement that explains why the brand exists, what it does and who it serves, and it guides daily operations. Keep the vision for the future and the mission for the present. [L1071.004]
- Vision statement formula: To [lead or transform a specific field] by [ambitious, long-term action]. It works because it gives the company a direction that is ambitious and outlasts any single product. Example in this pattern: To make healthy eating the easiest choice for busy families. Second example: To turn every home into a place where people learn to repair what they own. [L1071.006]
- Vision and mission formula: the vision describes the long-term future state the company wants to help create, while the mission uses present tense to say why the business exists, what it does, who it serves and how. Example vision: A world where every kitchen is stocked with affordable, healthy food. Example mission: We sell affordable plant-based meal kits that help busy families eat well in under twenty minutes. [L1044.036]
- Mission statement formula: To [accelerate or deliver a specific outcome] by [what the brand actually does] for [who it serves]. It works because it links daily work to a concrete result and a named audience. Example in this pattern: To make outdoor gear affordable by selling durable designs direct to hikers. Second example: To help small cafés keep more profit by offering simple inventory tools for independent owners. [L1071.007]
- Write a mission statement that says what the company stands for beyond its product, for example the personal benefit the customer gets. Write it before producing content, because a clear mission makes every later content decision easier. [L0487.005]
- Older advice (Source B, 2021-22): in the vision, describe how well known the brand is, who knows it, what people think of it, and how many reviews it has. Also note whether people talk about the business without being asked. [L0439.017]

### Brand brief: answer before writing any copy

Product and brand brief: answer each question before writing any brand copy:

```
What is the product or niche?
What community does it serve? (describe who they are and what they want)
Is there a play on words that fits the product or brand?
What does the product do?
What are the benefits? (list each one as a short point)
What is the brand tone? (list three or more adjectives)
```

[L1071.003]

- The play-on-words question in the product and brand brief is optional. If no wordplay fits the product or brand name, leave it blank and move on to the benefits and tone questions. [L1071.024]
- Completed brief, golf swing trainer brand. Product: a golf swing trainer in the golf niche. Community: amateur men who want to improve their game and skills. Product function: the trainer leaves a mark where the golf club hits the floor, showing how accurate the swing was and where the golfer can improve their direction. Benefits: a better golf swing, taking strokes off their game, beating their friends, and becoming a better golfer. Brand tone: playful, manly and competitive. The brand copy and visuals should reflect these benefits and tone throughout. [L1071.020]

### Personality, values and voice

- A product or store has a personality made of several traits, but one trait usually summarises it best; for a luxury car this is quality. Build headlines around that dominant trait and blend in other traits only to add variety, always linking them back to the dominant one. [L0330.062]
- A brand should behave like a consistent person: its public image should match how it acts privately, and it should have its own values, personality and tone. Authentic brands make these two sides as close as possible. [L1044.012]
- Brand values should be chosen deliberately, for example quality, empowerment, customer focus, fast shipping or a great customer experience. Public-facing values should also be reflected internally, so the team actually lives them. [L1044.008]
- Brand values formula: choose four to five single-word values that reflect how the company acts, such as performance, honesty, innovation, responsibility. It works because short, consistent values can guide product choices, customer service and marketing alike. Example in this pattern: Our values are clarity, speed, reliability and care for customers. Second example: Our values are craft, transparency, comfort and fair pricing. [L1071.009]
- Older advice (Source B, 2021-22): core values can be any traits, such as honesty, compassion or having fun. A value is right when reading it makes the owner feel motivated to start the day. [L0439.016]

### Brand promise and unique selling proposition

- The unique selling proposition is the specific way a brand differs from competitors selling similar products. The brand promise is what the brand consistently delivers to customers that sets it apart from the rest. [L1044.007]
- Brand promise formula: state the concrete result you deliver, the feature that delivers it, and the frustration it replaces. Example: We deliver back support for eight-hour workdays through ergonomic seating that suits a modern living room, instead of clinical office chairs. This works because a shopper can judge in seconds whether the store solves their specific problem. [L1044.035]

### Brand name and identity

- Elements that build a clothing brand identity:
  1. Choose a logo and brand name that represent the meaning of the brand.
  2. Define a photography style.
  3. Define a website style.
  4. Define a typography.
  5. Define the portrayal of a specific lifestyle or emotion the brand stands for.

  [L0779.008]

### Mood board and visual direction

- Steps to create a mood board when starting from scratch:
  1. Collect colors, images and graphics that represent the brand, for example from Pinterest or a mood board software.
  2. Place these items together in one document.
  3. Review the combined look to see how the brand could look and feel.
  4. Adjust the selection until the style is clear, for example vintage with muted colors.

  [L1044.032]
- A mood board collects images and graphics that show the look and feel the brand should have. It is optional, but it helps align the designer, the copywriter and the owner on one visual direction before creating the logo and content. [L1071.019]

### Retro and premium style direction

- A retro or vintage look can be described by combining classic, traditional style imagery with muted, low-saturation color schemes. Collecting such images in a mood board before briefing a designer gives a clear visual direction. It is an optional step that makes starting from a blank page much easier. [L1044.042]
- Brand perception should be chosen as a set of adjectives, for example luxury, premium, wholesome or witty, and every visual and written element should match that choice. Pick the perception you want visitors to form before you produce ads. [L1267.003]
- Brand perception is a single descriptive tone chosen on purpose, such as luxury, premium, wholesome or witty. Choosing one tone keeps product pages, ads and copy consistent so visitors form the same impression across every touchpoint. Example for a pet accessories store: wholesome suggests warm, family-focused language, while premium suggests refined photography and restrained wording. Example for a fitness apparel brand: witty suggests playful captions and light humor in ads. [L1268.007]
- Brand image should match the company's real story. A company positioned as a small underdog should look and feel small through its materials and a folksy tone, because the image itself carries the concept. [L1366.029, L1366.105]

### Tagline and messaging

- Tagline formula: use one to three words built around a strong action verb that signals determination and movement. It works because it is short enough to repeat across ads, packaging, emails and the about page. Example taglines in the same pattern for an outdoor gear store: Stretch further. Own the trail. [L1044.037]
- Tagline formula: a short phrase of a few words that is memorable and encourages the customer to act or feel determined. It works because short, action-oriented phrases stick in memory and reinforce the brand's promise. Example in this pattern: Start Faster for a productivity app. Second example: Go Further Today for an outdoor clothing brand. [L1071.016]

### Keeping the document up to date

- Hold a brand workshop once per year as part of an annual company conference (e.g., UDI Conference). Include additional team connection activities like lunch and learns throughout the year. [L0260.013]
- Use brand workshops to reconnect the team with company vision, review market trends and content created that year, and verify alignment to brand objectives. [L0260.014]
- Rolling out a brand to the store:
  1. Write a brand message and a tagline before switching the store to a brand.
  2. Stick to the same brand colors so customers recognize the brand easily.
  3. Do not change the brand look or direction too quickly; keep to the plan.

  [L1469.015]

## Naming the business and brand

### Core criteria for the store name

- Keep the name to three words or fewer, because shorter names are easier to remember and better for branding. [L0336.006]
- For a general store, aim for 5 to 10 letters that are easy to say and spell. Run the radio test: a listener should be able to hear the name once and spell it correctly. [L0989.003]
- Make the name short, memorable and easy to say, without hyphens. Including the niche helps but is not required. [L0468.104] *(Source A, 2020, older advice)*
- Make the name short, memorable and easy to recall so customers can find you again. Avoid random letters or numbers, and make the name sensible. [L1109.062] *(Source A, 2018, older advice)*
- Say the candidate aloud in normal conversation and imagine someone repeating it to a friend. A good name is one people naturally reference when they talk about the brand. [L0990.013]
- Keep the name neutral or positive. Only avoid names that are outwardly negative or offensive, because the actions, service and conduct of the business shape brand perception far more than the name does. [L0990.001, L0990.011]
- Do not give the name a deliberately misspelled or creative spelling, such as Kandi instead of Candy. Suppliers and customers see it as unprofessional, and people who search from memory type the correct spelling and miss the store. [L0336.007]
- Spell every word as it appears in the dictionary. In a non-English market, use the correct spelling in the local language. [L0336.008]
- Choose a name that is clear and simple, not clever. It does not need a logical link to the products, because many successful companies have names unrelated to what they sell. [L0336.010]
- Avoid frivolous or overly unique names for a serious product, since they damage its perceived seriousness. Choose a name that suits the seriousness of the purchase. [L1261.109]
- Keep the store name neutral and not vague, and do not tie it to the owner's personal name, because these stores are not meant to be personality-driven. [L0834.113] *(Source A, 2021, older advice)*
- Time-box the naming decision. Overthinking delays the launch and has only a small effect on success. [L0336.011] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*

### Sound, meaning and tone

- If the brand should mean something, the sound matters. Soft, rounded sounds suggest friendly, bubbly or feminine brands. Sharp, hard sounds suggest aggressive, extreme or masculine brands. Choose the phonetics deliberately to match the intended tone. [L0990.002]
- Sound-shape associations are consistent across people. When asked to match a rounded shape and a spiky shape to the made-up words "bouba" and "kiki", most people pair rounded with "bouba" and spiky with "kiki". Soft-sounding names suggest rounded, gentle brands, and sharp-sounding names suggest angular, intense brands. [L0990.014]
- Define the brand tone with specific adjectives before you brainstorm. Tone words such as playful, manly, competitive, moody, exciting, sweet, bubbly, feminine and aggressive each call for different sounds and word choices. [L0990.016]
- Choose the name only after the niche and the initial product range are settled, so it reflects the market the store actually serves. [L0336.015] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*
- Avoid building the name around a word many competitors already use, such as "pro" in golf. Common niche words add little distinctiveness, so check how often a candidate word appears in the niche's product names. [L0990.015]
- Check that the name has neutral or positive meaning in major languages. [L0989.004]
- A brand name that describes a benefit or tells a short story can be part of the advertising itself, because names that tell a story are usually shown prominently. [L1261.106]
- Names that describe a benefit or product in a way that is memorable can be shown prominently. [L1261.106]

### Brandable names and word construction

- Pick a brand name made of two simple words: one describing the product and one describing the differentiator. This makes it easy to remember and lets it extend to a family of products. [L0334.004]
- Keep product formulas simple, for example 4 to 5 ingredients each, so customers can combine several products into one routine. [L0334.004]
- Store names work best when they include the niche keyword in the brand name plus a brandable word, and the name must be available. Descriptive words about the product that do not contain the keyword can also work. [L0359.062]
- A good brandable word can be a term that people already inside the niche recognise, such as a local gesture or slang word. This makes the name feel authentic while staying short, unique and easy to remember. [L0504.020]
- Use a brandable store name and domain instead of an exact-match name that copies the product keyword. Exact-match domains are the outdated approach. Brandable names are easy to remember and do not lock the store to one product, so the niche can widen later without renaming. [L0356.001] *(Source A, 2019, older advice)*
- A brand name that suggests the product's main benefit, such as speed, victory or care, works because customers link the name to the product without extra explanation. Example pattern: a name built around "swift" for a fast-delivery product, or "haven" for a home-comfort product. [L1071.010]
- Names that only describe an ingredient or category invite substitution and make it hard to hold price, because rivals can use the same name. Avoid names competitors can claim as their own. [L1261.108]
- A coined name with no meaning is exclusive and protectable, but it needs long, consistent advertising before it carries meaning. Most coined names never reach that status, so choose them with care and budget. [L1261.107]
- A pioneer who creates a new product category should give the product an exclusive name that can become the common term for the category, so rivals cannot use it. Launching a generic brand lets rivals take the demand. [L1261.112]
- A name tied to a patent may become common property when the patent expires. Plan names and brand protection separately from patent protection. [L1261.110]
- When a product must be sold under a common name, add a person's name as a secondary identifier. A named creator signals pride and authority and gives a generic product a distinct identity. [L1261.111]

### Generic versus niche-signaling names

**Generic names (not tied to one category)**

- Brand names for dropshipping stores must be generic and not tied to a specific product category. Naming a pet store "Paw Paw Friend" restricts future expansion into other categories. [L0320.001]
- A generic, catchy brand name with a simple font logo is a flexible starting point, because it can be used for any product. [L0361.043]
- Generic brand names are easier to come up with and are a valid choice. [L0990.003]
- Choose a flexible name that is not tied to one product category, because a category-specific name limits the store once more products are added. [L0989.002]

**Niche-signaling names**

- The business name should signal the market it serves, so a target customer who reads it can immediately tell the business is about their interest. Pick a name tied to the market's category rather than a single item. [L0336.001] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*
- Niche-relevant names make it immediately clear which market the brand belongs to. [L0990.003]
- Brand names built around a product description (for example "golf swing trainer") are good for niche or product-led stores, because the name itself explains what is sold. [L0990.008]
- A niche-specific name and logo must be replaced when the product changes. [L0361.043]

**When the sources differ**

- Generic is the rule for a multi-category store. [L0320.001, L0989.002]
- For a niche or single-product store, a niche name is acceptable. It must be replaced if the product changes, and it is the better fit when the product description is the main selling point. [L0361.043, L0990.008]
- Both generic and niche names can work with either a generic or a niche store. [L0990.003]
- The store name should reflect the niche but does not need to name every product category. Buyers still click and buy a product they searched for. [L0801.175] *(Source A, 2021, older advice)*

**Structure of market names**

- Formula: [local marker, such as a country nickname] + [market keyword] + optional [broad category word]. The local marker signals a national business, which many buyers prefer. The market keyword tells the target customer what the store is about. Examples: "Nordic Pet Supply Co." for a Nordic pet market, and "Texas Grill Depot" for Texas grilling products. [L0336.013] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*
- Geographic and cultural references such as country names, mountain ranges and region names create generic brand names with no product association, which allows expansion into any category. Examples: Tatran (Tatra Mountains), Karpatia (Carpathian Mountains), Orava (region), Danubia (Danube River), Liptov, Spis. [L0320.002]
- Any country works equally well as inspiration, whether it is a major nation, a lesser-known country, a region or a geographic feature. Names derived from Slovenia, Peru or Estonia perform as well as names from Slovakia for multi-category stores. [L0320.007]
- A name built on one product, such as Logsplitters.com, makes little sense once the store adds snowblowers or generators. A name built on the market word, such as Power Pros or Power Superstore, works across the whole range. [L0336.016] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*
- A name built on one product type, such as surfboards, locks the store into that product and makes new categories feel unnatural. A broader market name, such as one that implies water sports, leaves room to grow. A single-product name is workable if necessary but not ideal. [L0336.009] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*

Naming framework by market (Source B, 2021-22, older advice). The domains in brackets were checked for the matching name. [L0336.012]

| Market | Products | Name examples |
|---|---|---|
| Surfing | Surfboards, wetsuits, surfboard bags | The Surfers Emporium; Surfing Direct (surfingdirect.com) |
| Power equipment | Log splitters, snowblowers, generators | The Power Pros; The Power Superstore |
| Off-grid living | Solar energy, waste disposal, water treatment, off-grid food alternatives | Oz Off-Grid (osoffgrid.com.au); Off Grid Warehouse |

In each row the name contains the central market word (surf, power, off-grid), so the store can add product categories later without the name becoming misleading. [L0336.012]

**Case: Oz Off-Grid (Source B, 2021-22, older advice)**

An Australian off-grid living store started from an approved niche covering solar energy, waste disposal, water treatment and off-grid food alternatives. The owner considered several names and chose Oz Off-Grid. Off-Grid names the market, and Oz signals an Australian business that local buyers often prefer. The alternative Off Grid Warehouse was considered, but the matching domain osoffgrid.com.au was already owned, so the business name and domain matched exactly. The store was planned to serve the Australian market first, with the name leaving room for many more product categories. [L0336.017]

### Generating names with AI

**Tools**

- Use ChatGPT or Gemini (Google's AI) to brainstorm brand names for dropshipping stores. [L0320.003]
- An AI assistant such as ChatGPT can help generate unique brand and product names when naming is difficult. [L1015.011]

**Basic prompt**

```
Give me 10 brand names for my branded drop shipping store

For a different quantity or geographic inspiration:
Give me 15 brand names similar to [country name]
```
[L0320.004]

**Procedure: basic brainstorming**

1. Open ChatGPT or Gemini.
2. Submit a prompt requesting brand names, optionally specifying a number and a country for geographic inspiration.
3. Keep testing with different parameters to generate more options.
4. Select and use your chosen brand name.

[L0320.005]

**Deep research prompt (system role)**

```
You work as a senior brand namer and strategist.
```
[L0989.007]

**Deep research procedure (AI research model)**

1. Open a ChatGPT thinking model with deep research enabled.
2. Paste the brand name prompt and fill in the product description and the product list.
3. Run it, then review the 12-name longlist, the 4 positioning routes and the Top 3 shortlist.
4. Search each candidate's.com in a registrar's domain search (GoDaddy was used) and confirm it is available.
5. Add the chosen domain to the cart to confirm it is still free.
6. Search the name on Trademarkia and on Google for conflicts or unwanted meanings.
7. Choose the brand name and its matching.com domain.

[L0989.009]

**Input and output rules for AI research**

- Deep research often asks what exactly you sell, so put the actual product list into the prompt rather than a general category. Pair the list with a short description of the quality and the feeling the customer should get, because the names are built from those benefits. [L0989.008]
- Ask for two groups in one run: about six premium single-word names and about six names that are very likely to be free, such as two-word combinations of a brand word and a descriptive word. This gives aspirational options and realistic fallbacks in one list. [L0989.028]
- If the generated positioning routes all sound alike, the prompt is too vague. Ask again for bolder positioning and a more specific target audience. [L0989.011]
- For a broad niche store, the first name and positioning are provisional. Launch a basic store, test products, and sharpen the positioning later based on what sells and what the ads say. [L0989.010]

**Framework for a meaningful name (Source C)**

1. Define the niche, since it can shape the name.
2. Identify the community the product serves and what that audience likes to do.
3. Look for puns or wordplay usable with those interests.
4. Describe what the product does and how it helps people.
5. List the product's benefits and pull some of them into the name.
6. Define the brand tone or brand values, for example moody or exciting, as this strongly affects the name.

[L0990.005]

Worked example for a golf swing aid: the product is a swing trainer in the golf niche. The community is amateur men who want to improve their game. The product leaves a mark on a pad showing swing accuracy and direction. The benefits are a better swing, fewer strokes per hole and beating friends. The desired brand tone was playful, manly and competitive. [L0990.006]

**Product-specific name pattern**

Combine a word for the core benefit or improvement (such as "pro", "swing" or "precision") with the product type word ("trainer", "pad", "aid", "mate"). Optionally add a word that signals the desired tone. Examples: "Putt Rhythm Pro" for a putting-rhythm trainer, and "Iron Line Coach" for an iron-shot alignment guide. [L0990.007]

Variations of the product name, such as swapping the product word or reordering it, quickly produce a large list of candidates. [L0990.008]

**Iterating on AI output**

1. Review the list and mark the names you like or dislike.
2. Tell the AI what you dislike about the rejected names.
3. Ask for another batch based on that feedback.
4. Repeat until the candidate list is large enough.

[L0990.010]

Do not accept the first suggestion. Choose the final name yourself from the AI's list by picking the most memorable one that fits the niche best, and check the others first. [L0521.008]

### Checks before you commit

**Domain match**

- The business name must match the domain exactly. Customers usually remember the name and type it into the browser with a suffix such as.com,.com.au or another country-specific ending. If the matching domain is already taken, choose a different name. [L0336.004] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*
- Brandable names beat keyword-copy domains, so a matching domain should be brandable rather than an exact keyword match. [L0356.001, L0336.004] *(Source A, 2019, older advice)*
- In high-ticket dropshipping, buyers visit the site several times before purchasing. The first visit often comes from an ad, but later visits typically happen by typing the remembered name, so the name must be easy to recall and type. [L0336.005] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*

**Competitor similarity**

- The name must be fairly unique within the intended market. Search the product keywords on Google and Google Shopping, review the names of the sellers in the results, and avoid names or URLs that are close variants of a competitor's. [L0336.002] *(Source B, 2021-22, older advice)*
- Avoid names that look like or closely copy a competitor's name, since rivals can use the same name and make it hard to hold price. [L1261.108]
- A store name that matches an unrelated local business, such as a gym with the same name, is not a major concern. Clarity and memorability matter more. [L1109.063] *(Source A, 2018, older advice)*

**Trademark and languages**

- A quick trademark check should be part of the naming step. [L0989.004]
- Search the name on Trademarkia and on Google for conflicts or unwanted meanings. [L0989.009]
- Choose the name carefully, because it appears on the store, invoices, ads and domain. Check domain availability and possible trademark conflicts before launch. [L1007.008]
- A trademark-style name, which is a unique invented name combined with a product word, gives a unique domain for the Shopify store and ring-fences competition. Searches for the brand name face less competition than searches for the generic product name. Add the TM symbol to the end of such a name, since it is trademarked. [L0990.004]

### Multi-store and second-store naming

- Several websites selling the same products in the same niche split the effort. Put all products under one brand and one domain. According to the example given, one site with the same total work builds about ten times the authority in Google's eyes compared to ten separate sites. [L0467.056] *(Source A, 2019, older advice)*
- When one owner runs several related services or product lines, one brand usually gets a higher response rate because customers see a single, consistent company. Separate brands are an option, but the single brand is easier to run and tends to convert better. [L1076.025] *(Source A, undated)*
- A second store in a related niche can be named after its category with a "plus" suffix. For example, a dining room furniture store paired with a home lighting store. Both stores can share one theme, with only the logo and colour scheme changed. [L0623.027] *(Source A, undated)*

**Test stores that become brands**

1. Start with a general, catchy brand name on one store to test multiple products.
2. When a product proves to be a winner, keep the same website and change the domain, name and logo to build a real brand.

[L0361.045]

### Company and LLC names

- Choose the company name carefully, because it appears on the store, invoices, ads and domain. [L1007.008]
- A company name does not have to match the website name. Many businesses run one or more stores under a different name, which is not a problem for approvals or operations. [L0968.114] *(Source A, 2021, older advice)*
- Choose a generic company name for the LLC so that several related stores can later share it, rather than naming the company after one store or domain. [L1090.082] *(Source A, 2021, older advice)*
- Avoid the word "consulting" in a company name chosen from the start, since it does not help the business. If an existing company already has it, keeping the name is generally harmless. [L1090.083] *(Source A, 2021, older advice)*
- Create one legal business entity for each group of related businesses, for example one for fitness equipment and one for office furniture. This keeps accounting separate and makes selling a group easier. [L0468.105] *(Source A, 2020, older advice)*

## Domain selection and registration

### Domain structure and formulas

- Combine one brandable word with the niche keyword. Put either first: a brandable word plus the niche, or the niche plus a brandable word. Both patterns work equally well. [L0079.002]
- Formula: [brandable word] + [niche keyword], or the reverse. The brandable part makes the name unique and ownable, and the keyword tells visitors and search engines the topic at a glance. [L0521.001, L0521.005]
- Default order: brandable word first, niche second. If that.com is taken, swap them. [L0504.001]
- Examples:
  - ShakaSurfboards.com pairs the brandable word Shaka with the niche keyword Surfboards. [L0079.004]
  - Verdant + plantstands gives verdantplantstands.com. If taken, plantstandsverdant.com can be tried. [L0504.001]
  - Tidewear + swimsuits gives tidewearswimsuits.com, or swimsuitstidewear.com as the fallback. [L0504.001]
  - Lumora + candles gives lumoracandles.com. [L0521.005]
  - Brindle + pet supply gives brindlepetsupply.com. [L0521.005]
- When a niche combines two products, do not put both product names plus a brandable word in the domain. Choose one short, memorable name or the main product name. (2021, older) [L0853.028]
- If a descriptive.com built on a full product phrase is taken, a shorter variant with a different adjective may still be free. In one case, the longer product-based name was unavailable as.com, but a shorter name built around "active" plus the product word was available. Test several variants before settling. [L0520.020]

### Extensions:.com and local domains

- Use.com as the default, with no hyphens, no misspellings, and no name nearly identical to a competitor's domain. (2021, older) [L0671.138, L0671.273]
- United States and Australian audiences:.com. [L0079.003]
- Australian market specifically:.com.au. [L0079.003]
- The two sources on Australia differ in emphasis. Use.com for a broad US and Australian audience, and.com.au when the Australian market is the specific target. [L0079.003]
- United Kingdom operations:.co.uk. [L0079.003]
- Use.co.uk when the store sells in the UK. (2021, older) [L0671.139, L0671.274]
- A pan-European English-language store fits a.com. Google Ads can target specific European countries and languages, so the store can still reach targeted traffic. (2021, older) [L0671.142, L0671.277]
- Check the.com together with the local extensions, such as.com.au and.co.uk, before committing. A name whose domains are open is usually also a name with fewer trademark conflicts. [L0989.001]

### Length, characters and wording

- Keep the domain to three words at most. [L0079.003, L0249.042, L0521.002]
- Some sources say two or three words at most. Two words are easier to say and remember, and three words are the upper limit. [L0504.002]
- Pick a name that is easy to pronounce, distinctive and memorable. A customer should be able to say it, spell it and find it again later. [L0079.003, L0504.002]
- A good name can be recalled after hearing it once. [L0521.002]
- Domain criteria: quick, memorable, readable,.com, no dashes, three words or fewer. [L0249.042]
- Exclude numbers, hyphens and complex letter combinations. [L0079.003]
- Avoid numbers and dashes. Both make the name harder to spell, say and remember. [L0521.004]
- Avoid generic filler such as best, cheap, online, store or shop, unless the word is genuinely brandable. Also avoid trademark-like names, awkward spelling, and long or confusing word chains. [L0537.004]
- Domain names cannot contain spaces. Join a multi-word niche into one lowercase string before you search, so "standup desks" becomes "standupdesks". Use the same joined form in generators and availability checks. [L0504.019]

### Using the niche keyword

- Sources differ on whether the niche must appear in the name.
- 2022 advice (older): include the niche name for authority if possible. If not possible, use clever wordplay or add small words before or after, such as Max or All. [L0249.042]
- 2024 advice: including the niche is preferable but not required. Stores with generic or niche-related domains have succeeded equally well. Prioritize a memorable name over a keyword-heavy one. [L0276.033]
- 2021 advice (older): the domain should reflect the niche, but it does not have to contain the exact keyword. [L0671.141, L0671.276]
- A generic approach: use one generic, brandable domain that does not refer to the niche, such as a short invented name under 10 characters. Check names with Namelix. [L1412.047]
- A short, memorable name associated with the niche is ideal, but an unrelated memorable name is acceptable. (2021, older) [L0469.076]
- Exact-match one-word domains no longer give a meaningful SEO advantage and are usually priced far above their value. Choose a memorable two- or three-word brandable domain instead, since brand recall drives direct visits. (2021, older) [L1279.056]

### Generating candidates: tools and procedures

- AI brainstorming tool with a domain-specific prompt. Customize it with two editable sections:
  - the niche keyword, for example surfboards, horse saddles or chandeliers;
  - optionally, a vibe: premium, coastal, adventure, eco-friendly, tech or family.
  The tool generates branded, niche-specific domain ideas. [L0079.005]
- Select names from AI results that match your niche naturally, are easy to remember and sound like a real brand. Test availability for your final choices. [L0079.008]
- Prompt for a 25-candidate list (use as written, replacing the niche placeholder):

```
You work as a professional domain name researcher who focuses on brandable eCommerce store names. Your task is to propose 25 brandable.com domains for an eCommerce store in this niche: [PASTE NICHE HERE]. Every domain must include the niche keyword exactly as written. For example, if the niche were surfboards, the word surfboards has to appear in each name, as in OceanSurfboards.com or SurfboardsHub.com. You are permitted, and even encouraged, to run live availability checks through real registrars such as Namecheap, GoDaddy, Cloudflare Registrar, or a WHOIS lookup.

Mandatory rules:
- Only.com extensions
- At most 3 words in total
- No hyphens
- No digits
- The exact niche word must appear in every domain
- The structure must be either BrandableWord + NicheKeyword (e.g., SummitSurfboards.com) or NicheKeyword + BrandableWord (e.g., SurfboardsSummit.com)
- Names must be short, easy to pronounce, easy to remember, and sound brandable and premium
- Avoid trademark-like names, awkward spellings, long or confusing word chains, and generic filler such as best, cheap, online, store, or shop, unless the word is genuinely brandable

Availability: run a live check on every domain and label it Available, Taken, or Premium/For Sale. If a name is Taken, keep generating until you have 25 strong ideas in total. If live checks cannot be run in this session, say so clearly and mark the status as Unverified instead of guessing.

Process: first list niche keyword variations where they apply, then list short, brandable modifier words (examples: Peak, Atlas, Nova, Ridge, Wave, Forge, Harbor, Oak, Lux, Coast, Aura, Sage, Vertex, Tide, Trail, Stone, Bloom, Haven, Apex, Union, Craft, Nest, Collective). Combine the modifiers with the niche in either order (BrandableWord + Niche or Niche + BrandableWord). Favor names that feel like a real business, sound authoritative and premium, and could grow into a larger brand.

Output: return exactly 25 results. For each, list Domain, Word Count, Structure, Availability, Brandability Score (1-10), and Notes. Example row: OceanSurfboards.com | 2 | Brandable + Niche | Available | 9 | Strong, clean, premium ocean association. Optional vibe: Premium / Coastal / Adventure.
```
[L0537.001]

- A store domain for a niche brand should be.com only, at most three words, with no dashes and no numbers. The exact niche keyword must appear in every candidate, placed before or after one brandable word. [L0537.002]
- Manual procedure:
  1. List niche keyword variations that fit the product niche. [L0537.003]
  2. List short, brandable modifier words that sound premium and are easy to say. [L0537.003]
  3. Combine each modifier with the niche keyword as BrandableWord + Niche and as Niche + BrandableWord. [L0537.003]
  4. Check each combined domain for availability and mark it Available, Taken, or Premium/For Sale. [L0537.003]
  5. Replace every Taken domain with a new candidate until 25 strong ideas remain. [L0537.003]
  6. Score each remaining domain for brandability from 1 to 10 and note its feel, such as premium, coastal or adventure. [L0537.003]
- Generator procedure:
  1. Open businessnamegenerator.com and enter your niche in the add keywords field. [L0504.010]
  2. Click generate and wait for the results to load. [L0504.010]
  3. Note the related keywords and brand ideas, and ignore the exact names shown. [L0504.010]
  4. Google "words associated with [niche]" and open the lists you find. [L0504.010]
  5. Combine the best words with your niche, using the brandable-word formula, in a document. [L0504.010]
  6. Keep many candidates so you are not stuck when one turns out to be taken. [L0504.010]
  7. Check availability in Shopify and run the safety checks before buying. [L0504.010]
- businessnamegenerator.com is a free tool. It returns related niche keywords and brand ideas for the niche you enter. Its results are raw material, not final names. Combine the useful words with your niche word, before or after it. [L0504.007]
- Google search "words associated with [niche]" returns online lists of terms related to your niche. These give extra words to place before or after the niche keyword. [L0504.009]
- Domain Soup generates random brandable domain names. Good names are short, have no literal meaning and sound like a brand. [L0637.036]
- Do not create a domain by taking a competitor's store name in the same niche and changing one or two letters. The name must be unique to your brand. [L0504.003]

### Checking availability

- AI tools cannot verify real-time availability. Suggested names may already be taken or listed as premium. Verify each domain by entering it in a browser or Shopify search before purchasing. [L0079.007]
- Confirm availability for every candidate at a registrar before you treat it as an option. [L0989.012]
- Before you finalize, add the domain to the cart to confirm it is still available. A domain that looks free can disappear. [L0989.016]
- Check the exact final spelling at the registrar. A slightly different spelling is a different domain and may be free or taken. A domain checked earlier can be forgotten when the name changes. [L0989.029]
- Test several candidates, since a single name is often taken. [L0504.010, L0520.020]

### Premium, taken and squatted domains

- Many generated.com names may be taken or listed for resale at high prices. [L0989.012]
- In businessnamegenerator.com results, a dollar symbol next to a.com name means the domain is taken or held by a squatter. [L0504.008]
- Do not pay a squatter more than the normal registration price of about $15 per year. [L0504.008]
- Example: a premium.com on a domain marketplace was listed at $2,995 (roughly $3,000). It was skipped rather than bought. Premium resale prices like this are a reason to look for other candidates. [L0989.013]
- Aftermarket pages on a taken premium.com can show a "Buy It Now" price plus a broker service fee on top. Add that fee to the listed price before comparing candidates, since the total cost is what matters. [L0989.030]
- If a premium name is taken, put "the" in front of a short brand name. This often makes the.com available, for example thecosery.com. [L0989.015]
- Two-word fallback: a brand word plus a descriptive word is usually free immediately, because few people register such combinations. Use it when single-word premium names are taken or overpriced, for example a brand word plus "comfort" or "plush". [L0989.014, L0989.033]

### Price limits

- The sources give different figures for the standard yearly price of a domain. Treat about $13 to $15 per year as the normal range. The 2021 figures are older.
