  - About $13 per year: a standard domain costs about this. Do not buy an expensive domain from a third party, such as paying around $1,000 for a better-looking name. (2021, older) [L0671.140, L0671.275]
  - About $14 per year: do not pay more than this, and avoid buying from people who hold and flip domains. (2021, older) [L0469.076]
  - About $15 per year: do not pay a squatter more than the normal registration price. (Undated source) [L0504.008]
- Compare total cost, including any broker fee, before you buy. [L0989.030]
- Do not pay premium resale prices such as $2,995 when a two-word or "the" variant is available. [L0989.013, L0989.014, L0989.015]

### Matching the domain to the name

- A brand name matters less than whether its matching domains are free. Check the.com and the local extensions before committing. [L0989.001]
- The domain should reflect the niche and be easy to remember. It should sound like a real brand. [L0079.008]
- Pick a memorable name over a keyword-heavy one. [L0276.033]
- Make the domain unique to your brand. Do not copy a competitor's store name. [L0504.003]
- Do not put the word Shopify in a domain name or store name. Shopify has contacted stores that used its name in a domain, and ads that targeted the word Shopify were taken down. (2023) [L0964.159]
- Check the exact spelling of the final domain and the store name you use, so they match. [L0989.029]

### Business email

- Set up business emails on the domain alongside the website and branding. When suppliers contact you, consistent emails show that the business is legitimate. Set this up with the site, not afterwards. (Source B, 2021-22, older) [L1060.004]

## Logo design and files

### Logo requirements

- Define the brand before designing the logo: its values, what it offers, the customer problems it solves and its tone of voice. These answers guide the logo's shape and style. [L0952.001]
- The logo is the face of the brand and is often the first thing customers link to it. It reinforces the feelings, beliefs and attitudes people already hold about the brand, which is why it matters for trust. [L0952.002]
- A good logo meets four criteria: simplicity, memorability, versatility and relevance. Each one is a test to apply when judging a design. [L0952.003]
- **Simplicity:** the logo is easy to recognize and remember. Simplified versions of objects work well, as the Apple and Twitter bird examples show. A simple mark is recognized at a glance and recalled later. [L0952.004]
- **Memorability:** the logo makes a strong impression at first glance and stands out enough that a viewer instantly recognizes it on later encounters. [L0952.005]
- **Versatility:** the logo must work in many formats (social media, websites, letterheads) and on both light and dark backgrounds. Test it in each context before finalizing. [L0952.006]
- **Relevance:** the logo should visually match the niche. For a dropshipping brand, use concepts from the product's niche, for example pet symbols for a pet brand or baby symbols for a baby brand, so the brand looks like it belongs to that niche. [L0952.008]
- A logo should be as simple as possible, because simpler logos produce better results. Minimal designs look more professional and more luxurious than busy ones. [L0953.002]
- A logo should be simple: text plus one small graphic, nothing showy, so it is noticed but does not compete with the page. Stores whose products are already colorful and busy benefit most from a simpler logo. [L0342.007, L1045.007]
- When unsure about a logo design, choose the simpler option and avoid over-engineering or overcomplicating it. Less is more. [L0952.009]
- A logo does not need to be professional at the start. It should be clean, the right size and not pixelated, and a simple typed wordmark is sufficient and cheap to make. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0663.069]
- The logo should use the same words as the domain name, so the store name is the most visible element. Check that the spelling matches the domain exactly. [L0342.006]
- The logo should use the same words as the domain name, with the store name front and center. This keeps the brand name consistent across the site. [L1045.006]
- Logo design should be primarily text-based, displaying the business name clearly and legibly as the dominant element. Decorative logo marks are optional and not necessary for success. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.007]
- The logo must be rectangular (wider than tall) to fit into website headers without consuming excessive vertical space. Circular logos are difficult to size and display properly. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.006]
- Your website logo appears in the header, footer and a few other pages. Rectangular orientation is critical because it fits naturally in the header space without consuming excessive height. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.024]
- A logo should be rectangular and no more than about 200 px high, with a width of around 400 to 500 px, so it loads quickly and displays well in multiple places on the site. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1371.030]
- Logo text style should match the target customer base. Male-dominated markets such as power equipment benefit from bold, chunky, thick fonts. Water sports and movement-focused markets suit softer, flowing typography. Luxury segments need elegant, refined letterforms. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.026]
- Logo design quality comes from a brand colour palette and a chosen typeface defined before the logo is built, so colours can be swapped quickly to test how the logo looks. [L0952.012]
- Build logos from core basic shapes (circles, squares, ellipses, rectangles) instead of drawing everything freehand. Complex icons can be reduced to a few simple shapes that fit together. [L0952.013]
- Taglines in the logo are optional and make no measurable difference to business success. Include one only if it genuinely resonates with the target market's values or positioning. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.008]
- Branding is not permanent. The logo design and colours can evolve over time without changing the business name or domain. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.009]

### Wordmark or icon: where to start

- A simple typed wordmark in a nice font is enough as a logo at the start. Logos matter less than the brand name, so do not spend much time on logo design. [L0361.044]
- At the early stage, use text-only wordmarks with no icon or symbol, because a mark is not needed for versatility yet. Each wordmark option should differ only in its letter case or type style. [L0954.006]
- Paying for a logo is optional during setup. A logo can cost about USD 20 or up to USD 100, and the choice adds to the USD 100 to 500 launch budget. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0438.007]
- A consistent symbol paired with the brand name builds recognition over time. When later products launch, customers who saw the icon before connect it with past purchases and expect to like the new product. [L1047.004]
- A brand logo must work across many surfaces, such as a website, packing slips, social media ads and other materials. It needs to be versatile and adaptable, not a single image that only fits one place. [L1047.001]
- Test a logo by mocking it up on the real surfaces where it will appear, such as an Instagram profile, before deciding. A wordmark can fit poorly in a small profile circle while a compact icon fits well. [L1047.003]
- There are two ways to build an icon: an automated route where AI brainstorms concepts, and a manual route where you sketch the icon yourself and have AI refine it. Use the AI brainstorm when you need options, and use the sketch when you already have a specific idea in mind. [L1047.005]
- Logo workflow in four steps: [L1047.002]
  1. Develop an icon that pairs with the brand name.
  2. Export the logo as a transparent PNG.
  3. Convert the PNG into a vector file.
  4. Finalize the logo into the variations you actually need.
- A logo concept should express the product's core meaning in a simple visual idea, such as motion for speed or growth for progress. A simple symbol is easier to remember and connects to the brand story. Example: a leaf-shaped mark for an eco-friendly cleaning product. Second example: an arrow shape forming a 'U' for an upgrade-focused electronics store. [L1071.011]
- Logo design pattern: a simple mark made of two or three basic shapes, where one shape is cut out of another (negative space) to suggest a recognizable object. The viewer completes the image in their mind, which makes the mark memorable and easy to recognize. Example of the same pattern: a coffee cup whose handle is formed by a cut-out circle in a solid square, and a leaf built from a single cut-out ellipse on a circle. [L0952.041]
- A pet-friendly brand logo can pair a rounded display typeface with a lighter, softer secondary typeface. Choose the typefaces together with the brand colours before building the mark, so the text and symbol share one visual tone. [L0952.052]

### Tools and routes

Choose the route by budget and time to market. [L1044.040]

| Option | Best when | How it is used |
|---|---|---|
| Canva logo templates | Fast launch, no design skill | Type logo into the search, edit text, fonts and icons, then take the palette from an icon in the design |
| Adobe Illustrator or Photoshop | Owner has design skills | Build the logo from scratch |
| 99designs | Need visual inspiration | Browse the logo library and recreate a style you like |
| Fiverr logo designer | Little time, budget available for hiring | Brief the designer on look and feel, tone of voice, brand values and example designs, then let them create the logo |

[L1044.040]

- Logo, colour and typography can be made in Canva, through a graphic design class, or by hiring a freelancer on a third-party service such as Fiverr. [L1071.012]
- Canva is a free design tool that can produce a simple logo. A professional designer is an option when more polished branding is needed. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0853.094]
- Canva can hold a brand kit that combines the logo, colour palette and typefaces in one place. [L1071.012]
- You do not need a finished logo concept before contacting a Fiverr designer. Provide three colour hex codes, the business name, what the business sells and the target market feel you want, then let the designer create options from that foundation. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.025]
- A logo can be built from overlapping shapes in Illustrator: [L0754.057]
  1. Set each new shape to about 50 percent transparency so overlapping shapes stay visible.
  2. Draw the basic shapes with the Shape tool and the Pen tool, and shape anchor points with the Direct Selection tool.
  3. Group the shapes you want to combine and open the Shape Builder tool.
  4. Drag across the areas you want to keep to merge them into one shape, and hold Alt or Option and drag across the areas you want to remove.
  5. Lock finished layers using the lock icon in the Layers panel so they cannot be moved by accident.
  6. Polish the outline with the Direct Selection tool, then set the transparency back to 100 percent.
  7. Collect several reference logos for inspiration and iterate until the shape feels balanced.

### Canva: store logo for the Source A theme

Canva procedure with the 800 x 300 px template: [L0342.005]
1. Open the logo template for the Source A theme (800 x 300 px) and click Use Template.
2. Click Text, then Add a heading, and type the store name exactly as the domain name.
3. Save the brand colors in Brand Kit first, then select the text and apply a brand color with the text color (A) icon.
4. Enlarge the text until it fills the template, choose a bold or extra-bold font and optionally capitalize it.
5. Adjust letter spacing and line spacing so the text fills the frame and the lines sit close together.
6. Open Elements, search Graphics for a simple icon such as a letter S, drag it in, resize it to fill the template and recolor it to match the brand colors.
7. Keep the logo to text plus one small graphic.
8. Download the logo as a PNG. On the free plan it keeps a white background.

A second version of the same Canva procedure: [L1045.009]
1. Open the logo template (800 x 300 pixels) through the linked template and click Use template.
2. Click Text, then Add a heading, and type the store's domain name.
3. Select the text and use the A text color button to pick one of the brand colors from the Brand kit.
4. Change the font, make it bold or extra bold, capitalize it if wanted, and enlarge it to fill the template. Adjust letter spacing and line spacing as needed.
5. Go to Elements, then Graphics, search for a simple letter or symbol and pick a free item (items with a crown are paid).
6. Drag the icon into the template, enlarge it to nearly the full template size and change its color to the brand palette.
7. Click Share or the download button, choose PNG and download.

### Canva: square template (Source D method)

- The Canva logo template used in this method is a square 500 × 500 px design. Keep the logo on a square canvas of this size. [L0953.007]
- Procedure in Canva Premium: [L0953.008]
  1. Decide the brand name first.
  2. In Canva Premium, open Designs in the top-left area.
  3. Search "Logo" in the "Search Logo templates" field and scroll through the results.
  4. Choose a simple, minimalist template that matches a professional look.
  5. Replace the template text with your brand name.
  6. Edit the template's letters to fit the brand name, for example by changing the first and last letter of the template text into an O-shape.
  7. Adjust the position of the logo elements (move up or down) until they are balanced.
  8. Adjust the size of the logo so it is clearly readable.
  9. Check the result against the simplicity rule and finish if it is simple and professional.
- Pick a template whose style is black and white, minimalist and simple, because that style matches the professional look a logo should have. Reject templates with many elements or strong decoration, even if they catch the eye first. [L0953.006]

### Wordmarks generated with ChatGPT

- For logo generation in ChatGPT, select the ChatGPT 5 Thinking model and the Heavy thinking mode in the composer before pasting the logo prompt. The full set of four wordmarks, four favicons and a palette took about one minute of thinking in this setup. [L0954.015]
- Before generating, finalise the brand name and its spelling. The wordmarks and favicons are built from the exact spelling. If the name changes later, all four wordmark options, favicons and the palette must be generated again with the new name. [L0954.017]
- Keep the wordmark to the brand name only and leave out the domain extension such as.com, even if the domain is pasted into the prompt. The domain is an input for the brand positioning, not part of the logo text. [L0954.016]
- Steps to create a logo set and palette with ChatGPT: [L0954.003]
  1. Build a master brand document with the product list, the chosen brand name, the domain name and one brand positioning (three short pillars, each a label with one sentence, plus a short mini story paragraph).
  2. Open a new chat in ChatGPT using the thinking model.
  3. Paste the logo generator prompt and replace the placeholders with the brand name, the domain and the positioning text.
  4. Wait until the generation is finished.
  5. Click download and download all assets: the 4 wordmark PNGs, the 4 favicon PNGs and the palette swatch PNG.
  6. Open the downloaded files in Finder to check that the wordmarks, favicons and palette swatch are all there.
  7. Use the logo, favicon and text options in the Shopify theme.
- Logo generator prompt, to be pasted with placeholders filled in: [L0954.004]

```text
Act as a senior logo designer. Produce 4 clean wordmarks for the brand [BRAND NAME] that use type alone: no icon, symbol, mascot or other mark, only the name set in lettering. Leave out drop shadows, gradients, 3D effects and ornaments. The background must be transparent.

Mandatory baseline rules:
- Every letter sits on one shared baseline. No letter may be moved vertically on its own.
- Reject any bouncy or playful baseline. Switch off any stylistic set that offsets glyphs.
- Keep the font's default kerning. Adjust tracking evenly across the whole word, never letter by letter.
- Fonts may overshoot for optical reasons, but the baseline must still look straight, with no stair-stepping.
- Run a baseline check before export and regenerate any option that shows vertical jitter.

Four style options:
- A: lowercase
- B: Title Case
- C: UPPERCASE, bold
- D: soft serif (small caps, expanded)

Inspiration, not imitation: draw on recent, high-quality brand aesthetics from the last 10 years. Choose new, free fonts that suit the brand positioning and the products sold.

Deliverables: PNG wordmarks on a transparent background, 3000 x 1200 px, wordmark centred with even padding on all sides. Also create 4 PNG favicons, one per option, each showing the first letter of the wordmark.

Spelling must match the input exactly. Use consistent tracking with optical kerning turned on.

Output: download links for the 4 wordmark PNGs and 1 palette swatch PNG. Under a 'Palette' heading, list 5 colours with HEX and RGB values. Include at least one dark and one light colour with strong contrast for buttons, and make sure the colours work with white. Under each option (A, B, C, D), give a short spec line.

Inputs (paste below):
Brand name: [BRAND NAME]
Domain: [DOMAIN]
Brand positioning: [POSITIONING 1: label and one sentence] [POSITIONING 2: label and one sentence] [POSITIONING 3: label and one sentence]
Mini story: [SHORT MINI STORY PARAGRAPH DESCRIBING THE PRODUCTS, THE CUSTOMER FEELING AND THE BRAND PROMISE]
```

[L0954.004]

- The four wordmark style options in the logo prompt: [L0954.005]

| Option | Style |
|---|---|
| A | lowercase |
| B | Title Case |
| C | UPPERCASE, bold |
| D | Soft serif, small caps, expanded |

All four are generated from the same brand name and positioning, so the owner can compare them and choose one. [L0954.005]

- All letters of a wordmark must sit on one uniform baseline, using the font's default kerning and even tracking across the whole word. Any generated option that shows vertical jitter or per-letter nudging must be regenerated before export. [L0954.007]
- Wordmark export specification: PNG, transparent background, 3000 x 1200 px, text centred with even padding on all sides. [L0954.008]
- Wordmark font options generated for a comfort apparel brand: [L1331.005]

| Option | Case | Font | Tracking | Color | Use |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | lowercase | DejaVu Sans Regular | +20 px (~+40/1000 em) | Ink #2E3A59 | Soft, modern, friendly; casual apparel |
| B | Title Case | DejaVu Serif Regular | +18 px (~+36/1000 em) | Cinnamon #B37F5B | Warm, crafted serif for comfort and care |
| C | UPPERCASE | DejaVu Sans Bold | +24 px (~+48/1000 em) | Ink #2E3A59 | Premium, assertive, legible on packaging |
| D | Serif, bold | DejaVu Serif Bold | +32 px (~+64/1000 em) | Fog #A4A9B5 | Editorial, for lookbooks and swing tags |

[L1331.005]

- Logo export checklist for wordmarks and favicons: [L1331.007]
  1. Use a single straight baseline with no per-letter vertical shifts.
  2. Keep default kerning and even tracking, with no manual glyph nudging.
  3. Make the canvas 3000 x 1200 px, centered, with at least 12% clear margin on all sides.
  4. Check that no letters overlap, there are no unwanted ligatures, and the mark stays clean at small sizes.
  5. Export transparent PNGs for wordmarks and favicons.

### Icon development with AI

- Run the icon or logo brainstorm in a chat that already holds the brand details, such as the name, target customer and style words. The AI then has the brand context and returns concepts that fit the brand instead of generic symbols. [L1047.042]
- Suggest icon ideas with this prompt: [L1047.006]

```text
Suggest several icon ideas that could stand for [BRAND NAME]. For each one, explain its shape, the feeling it conveys, and where it would appear, such as hang tags, waistbands, packaging, an app icon, or embroidered details. Keep the look consistent with [BRAND STYLE WORDS, e.g. soft, minimal, luxe, neutral, sporty, playful].
```

[L1047.006]

- If you want a full logo rather than a separate symbol, ask for logo concepts instead of icon concepts in the brainstorm prompt. This produces a cohesive logo design that already includes the brand name. [L1047.007]
- Choose the concept whose shape and mood match the target customer and the brand direction. A refined, luxury-leaning silhouette can fit a women's brand better than a more generic symbol, even if the generic one looks more striking at first. [L1047.008]
- Pick icons that match brand maturity. A sophisticated, techy or luxury brand fits abstract or geometric marks, while cutesy, cartoon-like graphics suit a playful, girly brand. Compare each candidate with competitor logos in the same niche to see where it lands. [L1047.017]
- An abstract symbol built from three flowing lines can carry several meanings at once, such as breathability, movement and luxury. This gives the brand a story it can reference in marketing and product messaging. [L1047.018]
- The icon must fit the existing brand elements, such as the font and existing icons. A geometric symbol pairs well with a squared-off typeface, while a playful rounded font suits a more cartoonish mark. [L1047.019]
- Judge an icon by how it looks at small sizes, such as a profile picture circle or an app icon, not only at full size. These are the places where the symbol appears alone, so it must stay recognizable there. [L1047.043]
- Manual icon method: [L1047.010]
  1. Decide on the exact icon idea you have in mind before you open the chat.
  2. Draw the logo on a sheet of paper. You do not need artistic skill, but expect several tries.
  3. Take a clear photo of the sketch.
  4. Drag and drop the photo into the ChatGPT chat.
  5. Ask ChatGPT to turn the sketch into the icon.
  6. Compare the AI result with the sketch and refine it if needed.
- Sketch-to-icon prompt: [L1047.011]

```text
Can you turn this sketch into the icon? Keep the exact outline, proportions and key details of my drawing, and turn it into a clean, simple shape that works as a logo.
```

[L1047.011]

- AI image generation can ignore details described in the text, such as a leg silhouette, and return a plain letter shape instead. For a specific shape, upload a sketch so the AI follows your drawing rather than a written description. [L1047.012]
- Smoothing prompt for a sketch-based icon: [L1047.015]

```text
Smooth this icon so the edges are clean and even. Then make it even smoother and more minimalistic, keeping the core shape and proportions unchanged.
```

[L1047.015]

- Logo pattern in use, from a case: [L1047.039]
  - **Starting point:** a women's luxury-leaning fleece and activewear brand had an original stylized logo that was judged not versatile enough for everyday brand use. A mockup on an Instagram profile showed the wordmark fit poorly where a compact icon would fit well.
  - **Step 1:** ChatGPT brainstormed icon concepts, including a curved C with a leg silhouette, a spiral C, a flame C, a soft mountain, a crescent moon, a monogram and a wave C. The curved C with leg silhouette was the first favorite, and it was generated as concept 1.
  - **Step 2:** Concept 1 ignored the leg shape and came out as a plain C. The team drew the leg by hand, photographed the sketch, and uploaded it so ChatGPT could turn it into an icon. The sketched leg icon was more recognizable but its first AI version was rough.
  - **Step 3:** The team went back to an earlier message and branched the conversation to generate the mountain concept as concept 6. The mountain concept was judged fine but without much personality. The crescent moon concept 9 was rejected, and the monogram concept 8 looked like a copyright symbol.
  - **Step 4:** The leg sketch was refined by asking for smoothing and a more minimalistic look, which made it cleaner. The wave C concept 4 was judged abstract, luxurious and suitable as a profile icon, with three lines that could suggest breathability, movement and luxury.
  - **Step 5:** The wave C was chosen over the leg icon. The leg icon was seen as more cutesy and playful, while the brand was judged more mature and techy, and the geometric wave C paired well with the existing geometric icon and squared-off font.
  - **Step 6:** ChatGPT regenerated the chosen icon as a transparent PNG, which was then vectorized in Kittl and tested by scaling up.
  - **Step 7:** Variations were built in a grid of icon, wordmark, stacked and left-aligned logos, each in black, white and color, plus black and white text over a color icon. The stacked logo used center alignment and a larger text than symbol.
  - **Result:** all variations were exported at 3x size as PNG and SVG files, and screenshots were collected on a FigJam board. The next planned step was a branding deck.

### Icon shape and style patterns

- A curved C icon: centered and left-aligned versions look strongest, though other alignments such as right-aligned are possible. [L1047.034]
- Choosing the alignment that suits the icon shape is a principle to follow. [L1047.034]

### Variations

- Build a logo variation grid in Kittl. Rows are layouts and columns are colour versions. [L1047.028]

| Layout | Black | White | Color | Black/Color | White/Color |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Icon | black icon | white icon | color icon | not used | not used |
| Wordmark (text only) | black text | white text | color text | not used | not used |
| Stacked (center aligned) | black | white | color | black text, color icon | white text, color icon |
| Left aligned | black | white | color | black text, color icon | white text, color icon |

Black/Color means black text with a color icon, and White/Color means white text with a color icon. [L1047.028]

- Plan colour versions from the start. A colour icon with black text or with white text is very common and is needed for many backgrounds. You will not realise you need these combinations until you try to use the logo. [L1047.033]
- Choose a logo colour version for each background type: a light version for light backgrounds and a dark version for dark backgrounds. A colour that works on white often needs to be lightened to work on black. [L0952.010]
- A minimum set of logo versions to launch a brand is one light version, one dark version and one social media profile version. [L0952.011]
- In a stacked logo, make the text larger than the symbol for a more balanced look. [L1047.030]
- Align the stacked and left-aligned logo versions by eye, judging by how they look visually, rather than following strict alignment hacks. This optical approach usually produces the best result. [L1047.031]
- Test versatility by checking the logo on each background and in each context before finalizing. [L0952.006]

### Formats and files

- A transparent PNG is an image with no background, so it can be placed on any colour or photo. A normal image with a white box cannot be used on top of other designs or surfaces. [L1047.020]
- A raster file such as PNG becomes pixelated and blurry when enlarged, so it fails on large uses such as building signs, vehicle graphics and billboards. A vector version is required for any logo that must be used at large sizes. [L1047.023]
- Use PNG files with a transparent background for logos and favicons. JPG files do not support transparency and show a white box around logos placed on a dark background. Only PNG and JPG files are generally needed for a Shopify site. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1371.028]
- Export the logo variations: [L1047.035]
  1. Click each variation in the group.
  2. Choose Quick Export.
  3. Pick the format: PNG for the transparent raster version, SVG for the vector version.
  4. Set the size to 3x.
  5. Click Export.
  6. Repeat for every variation in every group.
  7. Screenshot the finished variations and add them to a FigJam board for review.
- Name the downloaded logo files as [brandname]_wordmark_[A-D].png for the wordmarks, [brandname]_favicon_[A-D].png for the favicons and [brandname]_palette_swatch.png for the palette, all in lowercase. On a Mac these files land in the Downloads folder, where they can be checked in Finder before uploading. [L0954.018]
- Prepare the store logo for Shopify: [L1371.029]
  1. Open the transparent PNG logo in PicMonkey.
  2. Use Crop canvas to remove the extra white space around the logo.
  3. Use Resize to set the width to about 500 px, keeping the logo rectangular.
  4. Download as PNG.
  5. Upload it under the header logo and checkout logo in Shopify.
  *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- In the Shopify admin, the logo and the wide homepage header image can be downloaded from Content > Files by opening each file and clicking its download button. The wide header image is a separate file from a square image, so pick the wide version for the Facebook cover photo. [L0654.023]

### Favicons

- Create one favicon per wordmark option: a PNG showing the first letter of the brand name. The favicon is what appears in the browser tab, and a store without one looks unfinished. [L0954.009]
- A favicon is the small icon shown in the browser tab, and a site without one shows a default blank globe icon. Use an element of the logo so the browser tab looks polished and recognizable. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1371.027]
- Favicon image size: the Shopify Favicon field asks for a 32 x 32 px PNG, while the lesson resized the favicon to 40 x 40 px. Use 32 x 32 px where the Shopify field specifies it. The favicon should be a square PNG with a transparent background that represents the brand. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1371.026]
- Create a favicon in Shopify with PicMonkey: *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1371.025]
  1. Open the logo PNG with a transparent background.
  2. Use Crop canvas to make a square around one element of the logo, such as an icon, and click Apply.
  3. Click Resize, enter 40 in both width and height in pixels, and click Apply.
  4. Click Download, keep the PNG file type, and save the file.
  5. In Shopify Theme settings under Favicon, click Select Image, upload the file, and select it.
- Theme settings > Favicon provides several colour variants of the favicon image in the Files list. Pick the variant that matches the logo colours so the browser tab icon stays consistent with the brand. [L1309.070]

### Social profile versions

- For social media profile pictures, the logo should be square and have a lot of empty space around it so it fits the circular or square crop without being cut off. A simpler, centred version is usually better for this use. [L0952.007]
- A square profile version and a wide header version serve different places. The profile uses the square version, and the Facebook cover photo uses the wide header version from Content > Files. [L0654.023, L0952.007]
- Mock up the logo on the real profile before deciding. A wordmark can fit poorly in a small profile circle while a compact icon fits well. [L1047.003]
- Judge the profile version at small sizes such as a profile picture circle, because the symbol appears alone there and must stay recognizable. [L1047.043]

### Logo don'ts

- Avoid busy, cartoonish or sports-team style logos, because they look childish and unprofessional. A logo that looks amateur undermines trust in the store from the first impression. [L0953.004]
- Do not use a busy or heavily decorated template, even if it catches the eye first. [L0953.006]
- Do not over-engineer the logo. When in doubt, choose the simpler option. [L0952.009]
- Do not spend much time on logo design at the start. [L0361.044]
- Do not write the logo in lowercase where the brand expects capital letters. A logo written in lowercase can look careless. *(Lowercase is a valid option where it is the brand's chosen style, such as option A in the wordmark prompt.)* [L0656.024, L0954.005]
- Do not repeat the same element inside a logo, because it is distracting and weakens the mark. A monogram-style letter mark can also look like an existing copyright sign, so check how it reads before using it. [L1047.016]
- Do not make the symbol larger than the text in a stacked logo, because it often looks wonky. [L1047.030]
- Do not leave colour versions until later. Plan them from the start. [L1047.033]
- Do not expect AI to follow a written description of a specific shape. Upload a sketch instead. [L1047.012]
- Do not include the domain extension such as.com in the wordmark text. [L0954.016]
- Do not use a circular logo in a header-bound layout, because it is difficult to size and display properly. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.006]
- Do not add a tagline unless it genuinely resonates with the target market's values or positioning. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0323.008]
- Do not use a raster PNG at large sizes such as signs, vehicle graphics or billboards. Use a vector version. [L1047.023]
- Do not place a normal image with a white box on top of other designs or surfaces. Use a transparent PNG. [L1047.020]

#### Further details

- Premium brands often use a single clean mark or wordmark with no extra decoration, which signals luxury. To apply this, pick one clear element, such as a monogram or a single elegant serif wordmark, and leave out ornaments. Example 1: a black serif wordmark with one small letter-based symbol on a plain background. Example 2: a white minimal monogram of the brand's initials in a thin circle. [L0953.003]

## Color palette

### Why a tight palette builds trust

- Global theme colors control buttons, text, backgrounds, and UI across the entire site, so a tight palette of 2 to 4 brand colors with consistent application makes the store look professionally designed and trustworthy to suppliers [L0101.005].
- Inconsistent color use makes a store look untrustworthy [L0387.021].
- Exceeding the color limits creates visual confusion and reduces perceived trustworthiness [L0229.014].
- Many colors and fonts quickly make a store look messy, so reuse the same 2 to 4 brand colors everywhere [L1309.049].
- Older advice (2021-22): a multicolor website that looks like a rainbow appears unprofessional and confuses visitors who cannot focus on key information. Restrict yourself to three carefully chosen colors plus white [L0323.011].
- Older advice (2021-22): for startup brands, design uniqueness matters more than design quality. Your logo and colors must visually differentiate you from competitors rather than copy their approach [L0323.003].
- Before creating a brand, research the niche's branding. Check the colors, hex codes, fonts, and font sizes used by large brands in the same niche, since they have already tested these [L1158.027].

### Choosing the number of colors

Sources give different counts. Use the count that matches your situation:

| Situation | Color count | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Quick product test | Black, white, and one brand color, such as the product color | [L0361.042] |
| Standard store | Maximum of three to four colors: black, white, and one brand color (recommended palette) | [L0229.014, L0916.026] |
| Standard store, strict limit | At most four colors: white, black, and one brand color or brand color palette | [L1345.040] |
| Brand identity, main design | Three colors plus white (older, 2021-22) | [L0323.001, L0323.011] |
| Main colors plus supporting colors | Three main colors, with two or three supporting colors used sparingly | [L0387.021] |
| Brand palette reused across the store | Two to four brand colors | [L0101.005, L0775.009, L1309.049] |
| Brand colors with contrast button (older, 2021-22) | Three to four colors: one primary, one contrasting button color, and one minor accent | [L1371.009] |

- Keep the total to no more than four colors when you ask for a palette [L1259.063].
- Use one dominant neutral color with occasional bright accent colors to highlight products [L1071.013].
- Use only one bright accent color, reserved for highlighting products or calls to action. Keep the base mostly neutral, such as black and white [L1044.039].
- Keep one clearly separate brand color so the brand is recognized immediately. Do not use many accent colors across the site, because the brand color stops standing out [L0916.028].
- Colors matter less before the store sells. The goal at this stage is a reasonable-looking site rather than perfection (older, 2021-22) [L1371.011].

### Choosing colors from emotion and market

- Choose colors by considering what they mean to the brand, the customer, and the niche, since color carries emotion and signals. Colour choices should match the mood the brand wants to create [L1071.013].
- In color psychology, purple represents luxury, royalty, rest, and sleep. It reads as feminine but more mature than pink. For brands emphasizing comfort and trust, pair purple with warm, grounded, calm accent colors rather than high-contrast or vibrant colors [L0317.002].
- Choose the three brand colors to align with the target market's expectations and emotions (older, 2021-22): water-based products benefit from blues and greens, while luxury goods work with blacks, golds, and silvers [L0323.004].
- Color selection should reflect the market context. For water sports, use oceanic blues and greens with a contrasting button color like red. For luxury goods, use sophisticated blacks, golds, and silvers. This creates emotional alignment between your visual identity and customer expectations (older, 2021-22) [L0323.023].
- Older example (2021-22): Oz Off Grid uses three colors, green (primary), burnt orange (accent), and yellow. They were selected because they represent nature, survival, and off-grid living. The tagline Prepare. Survive. Prosper reinforces the target market of survivalists and environmentally conscious customers [L0323.022].
- Muting a strong red by shifting it slightly toward pink or blending it into a softer tone makes it less aggressive on a page. A gradient that moves into a softer shade of the same red has the same effect [L0659.052].
- Take the accent color from the product photo to keep the store palette consistent. A very saturated accent often reads better on web pages when you reduce its intensity and move it slightly toward gray [L1462.024].

### Creating a palette with tools and AI prompts

#### Procedure: selecting a brand color palette with ChatGPT and Color Hunt

1. Use ChatGPT to get color recommendations based on brand emotion and psychology [L0317.005].
2. Visit Color Hunt and search for palettes that match those color names or emotions [L0317.005].
3. Scroll through options to find a palette that resonates [L0317.005].
4. Hover over the chosen palette to reveal all hex codes [L0317.005].
5. Copy the hex codes for immediate use in design and website tools [L0317.005].

#### AI prompts

Use ChatGPT as a color psychologist and brand strategist:

```
As a color psychologist and brand strategist, what primary, secondary, and accent colors would best represent a brand called [name] that should feel [emotional goal, e.g., cozy, trustworthy, luxurious]?
```
[L0317.003]

ChatGPT returns color names with reasoning for each choice [L0317.003].

```
Suggest a color combination for an online [NICHE] store. Give me a palette of three colors (main color, accent color and background color), each with its HEX code, and describe the mood each color creates for the customer. Keep the palette consistent with the [NICHE] audience and the brand tone [TONE, e.g. playful or premium].
```
[L0605.052]

```
Please provide a color combination or color palette for my [NICHE] store. Include one main brand color, a lighter tint and a darker, bolder shade of that color for call-to-action buttons, plus white and black as base colors. Keep the total to no more than four colors and explain briefly where each color should be used.
```
[L1259.063]

```
Please provide me a color combination or color palette for my [niche] store. Give me one main brand color with a lighter and a darker variation, plus white and black as base colors. Explain which shade should be used for the call-to-action button and why.
```
[L1345.041]

#### Palette tools

- Use a palette site such as Color Combo, or ask ChatGPT for a palette that fits your niche, for example a jewelry store. Apply the same palette consistently across the store, including headings, backgrounds, and buttons [L1260.065].
- Use color-combos.com to find well-matched color palettes for the store and to pick the brand color that works with black and white [L0916.027, L0916.026].

#### Procedure: choosing brand colors without design skills (2023)

1. Search Google Images for a term such as modern blue color palette [L1283.070].
2. Open the image results and zoom in to see which colors combine well [L1283.070].
3. Save the palette and apply the chosen colors to the site [L1283.070].
4. Use a contrasting color, such as orange, for buttons so the call to action stands out [L1283.070].

### Hex, RGB, and exact codes

- Hex codes are six-character color codes, such as #540863 or #FFD3D5. They ensure exact color consistency across all brand materials: website, print, video, social media, and design work [L0317.006].
- The same hex code always produces the same color in Shopify, Kittl, web browsers, and print services [L0317.006].
- Hex codes are the universal format for specifying online colors: a hashtag symbol followed by six letters or numbers that produce the exact same color in any design software or website builder (older, 2021-22). The example #0DJ34K contains letters outside the hex range. Valid hex digits are only 0 to 9 and A to F [L0323.012, L1309.048].
- Color codes are six characters long, a mix of digits and letters in hex format. Enter the exact code from the brand file so the theme matches the logo and images [L1309.048].
- Hex codes and RGB values describe the same color in two notations: a six-digit hex code and three RGB values from 0 to 255. List both for each palette color so the color can be set in the store and reused consistently in creatives [L0954.011].
- Store the brand's hex color codes in a shared document so that anyone creating social media graphics uses the correct colors [L1000.061].
- Use the exact hex codes from the design tool that created your logo and images, so the store colors match the brand [L0775.009].

### Example palettes

- Cozy lifestyle brand: primary color #540863 (deep purple), secondary color #FFD3D5 (soft pink). Enter these hex codes into Kittl, Shopify, and all design tools to maintain consistency across the entire brand [L0317.007].

Example palette from a cozy apparel brand:

| Colour | Hex | RGB |
|---|---|---|
| Oat | #F2E9E1 | (242, 233, 225) |
| Blush | #EAD4C8 | (234, 212, 200) |
| Cinnamon | #B37F5B | (179, 127, 91) |
| Fog | #A4A9B5 | (164, 169, 181) |

The light tones (Oat, Blush) carry the background, and the darker tones (Cinnamon, Fog) are the contrast colors for buttons and text. A fifth dark color is required for a full palette [L0954.012].

Brand palette values from the logo brief:

| Name | Hex | RGB |
|---|---|---|
| Ink | #2E3A59 | 46, 58, 89 |
| Cinnamon | #B37F5B | 179, 127, 91 |
| Fog | #A4A9B5 | 164, 169, 181 |

[L1331.006]

### Roles of colors

- Older advice (2021-22): your brand identity uses a primary color for main design elements, an accent color for subtle highlights, and a contrasting button color reserved for clickable elements that must stand out from the rest of the page [L0323.001].
- Older advice (2021-22): choose one primary color used most often, one contrasting color for buttons, and a third color for minor accents [L1371.009].
- The palette needs a dark color and a light color that work with white, so the whole store does not end up in one color. The darker colors are used for buttons and contrast [L0954.010].
- Older advice (2021-22): keep most of the website background white. Use color only on small elements such as the menu, footer background, buttons, and link hover states, not on the whole page [L1371.010].
- Set the color scheme before designing the site. Brand colors are part of the palette you apply to headings, backgrounds, and buttons [L1260.065].

### Contrast and button colors

- Complementary colors sit opposite each other on a color wheel and create strong visual contrast. Use this principle to select your button color: blue contrasts with orange, purple with green (older, 2021-22) [L0323.005].
- Use a darker, bolder shade of the brand color for the call-to-action button so it stands out [L1345.040].
- Use a contrasting color, such as orange, for buttons so the call to action stands out [L1283.070].
- Keep the main text dark, such as black, so it stays easy to read (older, 2021-22) [L1371.009].
- On a dark section, build text hierarchy with tint. The title should have the strongest contrast, the subtitle slightly less, and the body text the least prominence while staying readable. This keeps the title as the first thing the eye reads [L0659.053].
- Dark backgrounds with light text can look jarring in a layout. Check colors together in a realistic website layout to avoid this problem [L1044.029].
- Test the palette on a sample website layout before finalizing it [L1044.039].
- A very dark brand color, such as deep purple, can look almost identical to black. Before finalizing, place the color version next to the black version and check that the difference is visible. If it is not, the color variant adds little [L1047.044].

### White space

- Older advice (2021-22): white space is your most important color on a website because it allows the human eye to process information clearly. Solid color blocks everywhere make pages hard to read and cause visitors to disengage and look elsewhere [L0323.002].
- Keep most of the website background white to create white space and make the site easy to read [L1371.010].

### Applying exact codes in the theme

- Global theme colors control buttons, text, backgrounds, and UI across the entire site [L0101.005].
- Use the exact hex codes from the brand file, and copy the codes from the design tool used for the logo, favicon variants, color-matched images, and banner visuals into the theme colors [L1309.045].
- Theme settings > Colors: solid button label white. Accent 1 is the primary brand color (pink in the example). Accent 2 is the secondary color (teal in the example). Gradients are not used [L1309.046].
- Theme settings > Colors > Secondary: text black, outline button in the brand blue, background a light gray slightly lighter than the default. The price for you save red stays at default and appears on product pages later [L1309.047].
- Header navigation menu colors in the example: background black, first level link white, sub menu background white, sub menu link the brand blue, sub menu border the brand pink, and active link color in main menu white. The same brand colors are reused across these fields rather than new ones [L0775.027].

### Applying exact codes in email, chat, and apps

- To set the button fill color in Shopify Messaging template branding, copy your store's hex color code from Shopify or a browser color picker and paste it into the button fill color field [L0147.006].
- The chat button brand color can be changed in the Shopify Inbox settings to match the store's color scheme. Take the exact hex code from the store's button color, for example via a free Chrome color picker extension, and paste it into the brand color field [L0948.007].

### Applying exact codes in design tools

- Set the color scheme before designing. In Canva, go to the home page, open Brand kit, and scroll to Brand colors. The free plan allows three brand colors, and black and white can be used alongside them [L1045.008].
- Brand colors saved in Canva's Brand kit appear as swatches in the text and element color pickers, so later designs can reuse the same palette without re-entering codes. The free plan allows three brand colors, and black and white can be used alongside them [L1045.049].
- In Adobe Illustrator, a palette saved as a color scheme in Adobe Color can be opened from the Libraries panel and applied directly to shapes and text. Save the palette there first so it is ready for the layout [L0659.045].
- Use the same hex codes in Kittl, Shopify, and all other design tools [L0317.007].

### Ebooks and documents

- An ebook palette example uses deep forest-ink, sage green, warm coral, and soft gold, with Fraunces for headings, Inter for body text, and Space Mono for small labels. Pick one palette and two or three typefaces that match the store brand, then use the same colors for headings, highlights, and dividers across all pages [L0861.045].
- A calm health or wellness ebook can use the same deep forest-ink, sage green, warm coral, and soft gold palette, with Fraunces for headings, Inter for body text, and Space Mono for small labels and eyebrows. This pairing looks editorial and slightly apothecary, and avoids a generic wellness cream look [L0862.037].
- In an ebook that reframes a myth into a fact, give the myth and the fix two different colors, for example coral for the myth and sage for the fix. The reader sees the reframe structure at a glance before reading the text [L0862.038].

### Common mistakes

- Using one color for the whole store makes it look flat and gives buttons no contrast. Always add darker colors for buttons and text on top of the light tones [L0954.013].
- Too many bright colors weaken the brand's identity [L1044.039].
- Do not use many accent colors across the site, because the brand color stops standing out [L0916.028].

## Typography

### Serif and sans-serif basics

- A serif is a small decorative line or flourish at the end of a letter stroke. Times New Roman has serifs. A sans-serif font has no serifs ("sans" means "without") and looks modern and clean. [L0317.012]
- A serif font has small flicks at the ends of its letters. A sans-serif font does not. [L1363.061]
- Sans-serif fonts create a clean, modern look. Serif fonts suggest tradition and formality. [L1071.014]
- Font classes differ in feel:
  - Monospaced fonts give every letter the same width.
  - Script fonts imitate handwriting.
  - Display fonts are decorative faces meant for headlines rather than long text.
  - Choose the class based on the mood and where the text will be read. [L0659.048]

### Choosing headline and body fonts against the logo

1. Identify whether the logo uses a serif or a sans-serif font. [L0317.012]
2. Select the opposite style from the logo:
   - If the logo uses sans-serif, choose serif fonts for headings and body text.
   - If the logo uses serif, choose sans-serif for headings.
   - This contrast creates a modern, professional, and visually pleasing result. [L0317.013]

Examples from the source:

| Brand | Logo style | Headings | Body | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Portal | Serif | Sans-serif | Not stated | L0317.014 |
| Summer Fridays | Sans-serif | Serif | Not stated | L0317.014 |
| Cozeria | Sans-serif | Noto Serif | Noto Sans | L0317.014 |

### Font pairing

- Pair a serif font for headings with a sans-serif font for body text, or the reverse, whichever fits the brand best. Replace the template's fonts on all headings and all body text in one batch. [L0992.003]
- Use one heading font and one body font, with one or two weights each. [L1309.052]
- Use one high-quality font consistently across the whole store. Consistent colour and font together build brand value. [L1345.042]
- Pick one typeface family and use it consistently across the brand. [L1071.014]
- Use default fonts for a store unless a designer is working on it. Mixing many fonts quickly makes a store look messy. (Older: Source A, 2021.) [L0853.045]
- Use one heading font across the site. A second font for headings is allowed only if it is still readable and not fancy. A bold, chunky font was chosen for the example store to suit its target market. (Older: Source B, 2021-22.) [L1371.015]
- Source conflict: some units say to use one family throughout the brand [L1071.014, L1345.042]. Others say to use a serif and sans-serif contrast between headings and body [L0317.013, L0992.003]. The sources do not state when to use one family and when to use a contrasting pair. Both approaches are given here as the sources state them.

Pairings to test as starting points. Check each pair in the actual layout, because a pair that works on a blog may not suit a specific niche. [L0659.049]

| Pair (as listed) | Source |
|---|---|
| Abril Fatface with Lato | L0659.049 |
| Allegreya Sans with Black Allegreya | L0659.049 |
| Source Sans Pro with Playfair Display | L0659.049 |
| Quattrocento with Quattrocento Sans | L0659.049 |
| Lustria with Lato | L0659.049 |

Worked pattern examples:
- A modern tech brand uses a clean sans-serif heading font with a simple body font. [L1071.014]
- A sports brand uses a bold display font for headlines with a plain sans-serif for body text. [L1071.014]

Procedure to find pairings for a niche:
1. Search the niche name together with "font pairing" or "luxury font". [L0659.051]
2. Look at real projects on design platforms such as Dribbble and Behance. [L0659.051]
3. Use those projects to see how fonts behave together in a finished design. [L0659.051]

### Font weights and emphasis

- Some serif families have no bold weight. A title that needs heavy emphasis should be set in a second typeface with a bold weight. [L0659.046]
- Check the available weights of a family before committing to it for headlines. [L0659.046]
- Body font setup in the example store (older: Source B, 2021-22):
  - Use Helvetica as the body font at 18 px, because it is common and easy to read.
  - Set the alternate bold font to Helvetica Bold.
  - Adjust letter spacing or heading fonts to taste. [L1371.013]

### Readability and body size

- Set the base font size to at least 18 px for all body copy. Smaller text is hard to read, and search engines do not favour it. (Older: Source B, 2021-22.) [L1371.012]
- The font size scale setting is the simplest way to adjust readability across the store. [L1309.052]
- Choose simple, standard, readable fonts. Avoid decorative, cursive, very thin, or unusual fonts such as Lobster, because they are harder to read. (Older: Source B, 2021-22.) [L1371.014]
- Text must be clean, easy to read and balanced in structure. [L1044.027]
- Readability limits decorative fonts:
  - Calligraphic or runic fonts that are hard to read should be used only for accents or images, not for body text.
  - Choose a readable serif (classic, with small flicks) for headings and for text where needed. [L1363.026]
  - Avoid hard-to-read decorative fonts for body text. [L1363.061]
- Mistake: do not use Comic Sans for website branding. It looks unprofessional, and poor-looking typefaces reduce trust in the store. [L1044.028]

### Niche fit

- Typefaces should fit the niche:
  - Luxury products use luxury fonts.
  - Friendly or playful brands use playful fonts.
  - Corporate or nature brands use clean fonts. [L1044.027]
- Fonts must match the niche's brand. Playful fonts suit baby or pet products. Elegant serif or thin luxury fonts suit jewellery. A font that changes between niches changes the brand feel drastically. [L1363.023]
- Luxury typography has moved away from calligraphic scripts toward high-contrast serifs. These combine very thin and very thick strokes with sharp terminals. The style reads as premium in most niches that target affluent buyers. [L0659.050]

### Licensing and Google Fonts

- Use commercially licensed fonts. Check the licence of fonts from Font Space before commercial use. Some fonts, for example ones copied from famous brands, cannot be used commercially. [L1363.024]
- Prefer Google Fonts for web pages. They are widely available in browsers and render reliably. [L1363.025]
- If a non-Google font is not installed on a visitor's device, the browser falls back to another font. [L1363.025]
- Use decorative fonts only in images, where the text is printed into the picture. [L1363.025]
- Source tension: the body font example uses Helvetica (older: Source B, 2021-22) [L1371.013]. The web rule prefers Google Fonts [L1363.025]. The sources do not reconcile these. Check the licence [L1363.024] and whether the font is a Google Font before using it on web pages.

### Setting fonts in the theme and page builder

Shopify theme (Typography settings):
1. Go to Theme settings > Typography > Change. [L1309.050]
2. Pick a heading font from Shopify's font library and click Select. [L1309.050]
3. The change shows in the homepage preview immediately. [L1309.050]
4. Set the body font under Typography > Body font. The example used Montserrat, with the weight changed from regular to medium for thicker text. [L1309.051]
5. Set the font scale to 110% to make all text larger. [L1309.051]

PageFly global styles:
- Set headings, body fonts and colours once under global styles. Changes then push across the whole PageFly site. [L1363.022]
- Upload brand fonts there, then set headings to the uploaded font. [L1363.022]
- Colour scheme used in the lesson:
  - Dark for primary.
  - Light and muted for secondary.
  - Light and slightly more vibrant for tertiary.
  - Plus charcoal, white and grey neutrals. [L1363.022]

## Brand guidelines and AI brand kit

### Brand system and consistency

- A brand encompasses far more than a logo. A complete brand system includes a color palette, typography, visual patterns, textures, and consistent messaging across all touchpoints. This consistency builds consumer trust and makes the business appear professional and cohesive. [L0317.001]
- Keep colours, shapes, typography and layout consistent across all brand visuals so customers link each element to the brand over time. A recognisable visual set makes the brand look more professional and makes it easy to recall. [L0755.002]
- Keep the tone, personality and visual identity of the brand consistent across all ads, because regular readers recognize and trust a stable identity. Change only when a test shows a different approach is needed. [L1261.113]
- After the logo is finished, build a branding deck that defines colors, typography, shapes and textures. This keeps all future graphics and marketing materials consistent with the logo. [L1047.038]

### Brand guidelines file: contents and export

- A brand guidelines document is a PDF containing all brand information: logo files in multiple formats and colors, exact color codes (hex and RGB), font names and pairing rules, visual patterns and textures, and brand voice guidelines. Share this with graphic designers, video editors, web developers, and team members to ensure they create consistent brand work. [L0317.008]
- A brand guidelines file should hold the brand photography, a moodboard, tone and style notes, brand use rules, the project color palette, typography and a logo panel. Keep the templates in the same file so every asset is built from the same source. [L0992.005]
- A brand guidelines file with defined colors, fonts, logo, photography and brand voice works as a plug-and-play base. Once it exists, a new template or ad can be assembled in minutes by dropping in those elements. The time goes into building the photography and voice first, not into each design. [L0992.001]
- Update the brand guidelines file whenever the brand changes, such as a new palette, font or voice, so that every new template is built from current rules. Treat the file as a living document rather than a fixed one. [L0992.028]
- Logo rule: the company logo comes in three versions that can be used interchangeably and resized as needed, but the logo must never be altered. Use only the official files from the shared brand folder. [L1467.001]
- Older advice (2021-22): keep the company logo as a PDF in a shared files section so any team member can send it to a vendor or customer who asks for it. Store it alongside the quote and invoice templates, so staff do not have to ask the owner each time. [L1396.067]

**Exporting the guidelines for the team and freelancers**

1. Open the completed brand guidelines document in Kittl.
2. Click the Download button.
3. Change the file format to PDF.
4. Select the "Combine into one file" option.
5. Download and share the PDF with all designers, video editors, developers, and team members. [L0317.022]

### Textures and patterns

**Creating textures and patterns for the brand guidelines**

1. Open your logo in Kittl.
2. Duplicate it and move the duplicate to a new "Textures" artboard.
3. Adjust the duplicate by changing opacity, adding colors, creating gradients, or layering elements.
4. Create 2-3 different texture variations.
5. Include these in the brand guidelines document so designers can use them for backgrounds, borders, and decorative elements across materials. [L0317.010]

### Brandfetch workflow

- Get API access from the right page: do not click "Get started" on the brandfetch.com homepage. Always click "Are you a developer? Get API keys" to access the developer dashboard and MCP setup. [L0322.011]
- The brand context step runs one Brandfetch call on the brand's domain to pull voice, colors and fonts. The result is saved as a brand-kit.json file and a separate voice file, and every later skill uses these as input. [L1361.020]
- Do not skip the transformation stage (stage 3), where you convert the Brandfetch raw data into a structured brand-kit.json and voice.md. This step is critical to avoiding generic AI outputs. [L0318.006]
- Converting raw Brandfetch API responses into structured JSON and Markdown files keeps later AI-generated emails, copy, and templates brand-specific (correct colors, voice attributes, and positioning) rather than generic. [L0318.016]

**Brandfetch run in one project**

1. Create a new folder in Claude Cowork and set it as your working folder.
2. Create a new project in Claude Cowork with a descriptive name (e.g., "email mastery course with AI").
3. In the project chat, invoke the Brand Fetch MCP using a prompt like "Using the Brandfetch MCP, research www.example.com.au".
4. Let the MCP fetch and display visual identity (logos, colors, fonts, social links) and brand context (tagline, mission, target audiences, voice attributes, style).
5. Review the output for contradictions between longDescription and actual product range, and note any data quality issues.
6. In the same project, use a mapping prompt to transform both Brandfetch responses into brand kit.json and voice.md files using the field mappings provided.
7. Save both files to your project folder.
8. Optionally, visit the brand's website directly with Claude in Chrome to verify fonts, currency, and positioning details. [L0318.008]

**Field mapping for brand kit.json**

| Field in brand kit.json | Source |
|---|---|
| brand.description | get_brand_context.identity.description |
| brand.tagline | get_brand_context.identity.tagline |
| brand.mission | get_brand_context.identity.mission |
| brand.tags | get_brand_context.identity.tags |
| positioning.value_proposition | get_brand_context.positioning.value_proposition |
| positioning.target_audience | get_brand_context.positioning.target_audience |
| positioning.products_and_services | get_brand_context.positioning.products_and_services |
| visual.logos, colors, fonts | get_brand.logos, colors, fonts |
| voice.summary, attributes, avoid | get_brand_context.brand.voice.* |
| style.summary, attributes | get_brand_context.brand.style.* |
| links.website, links.social | your domain, get_brand.links |

[L0318.009]

- Use get_brand_context.identity.description, not get_brand.longDescription. The latter is generic and may contradict your actual product range and positioning. [L0318.003]

### Data quality checks

- When Brandfetch returns an unclaimed profile (which is normal for most brands), treat colors and fonts as scraped estimates, not authoritative brand standards. Verify the actual typefaces and design details by visiting the site directly with Claude in a browser. [L0318.004]
- Using Brandfetch data as fully authoritative when the profile is unclaimed leads to inaccurate colors, fonts, and category descriptions. Always cross-reference with the actual website and live product listings. [L0318.012]
- Always validate that Brandfetch data, especially currency and product categories, matches your actual business. Example: if Brandfetch returns USD pricing but your domain is.com.au, verify whether you actually sell in AUD and correct the data. [L0318.005]
- Data quality score: the Silk The Label unclaimed profile scored 0.28. This is relatively low and indicates that colors and fonts were scraped rather than verified by the brand owner. [L0318.015]
- Worked example, Silk The Label: primary #5f021f (deep burgundy/oxblood), accent #ffffff (white). Fonts: Helvetica (title), custom body font (exposed as Shopify CSS variable --font-body-family, not directly readable). Logo available as PNG (820x96, transparent) and SVG. [L0318.014]
- If Brandfetch returns incomplete data (new or very small brand), ask the user directly for details rather than letting Claude guess or invent brand information. Do not allow Claude to invent missing brand data. If Brandfetch cannot detect a field (e.g., heading font) or returns incomplete information, request the data from the user instead of generating a value. Always verify colours, fonts, and links with the user before finalizing the JSON file. [L0322.009, L0322.012]

### Brand kit JSON and voice file

**Prompt for the brand kit and voice file** (verbatim)

```
Develop your brand identity and communication style for email marketing through a structured workflow, saving all outputs to your brand folder. Begin by using Brandfetch to retrieve your website's branding data: logo, color values in hexadecimal format, typeface selections, and social media connections. If Brandfetch returns limited information-which is typical for recently launched businesses-ask directly for the details rather than assuming. Never guess at brand attributes or rely on outdated cache; always verify by asking when uncertain. Create a JSON file called brand-kit.json in the brand folder using this exact structure: a brand object containing name, domain, tagline, and description; a positioning object with value proposition; a visual object with logos, three hex colors (primary, secondary, accent), and font details split by type (title, body); a meta object listing the URL and social platform accounts such as Facebook and Instagram; an industries field; and a location object with city and country. Verify the extracted colors directly against your live website, paying special attention to the primary hex code that styles your primary buttons, and confirm that both fonts and all shop and social links are current. Remove any inaccuracies. Determine your brand voice by asking how your organization should be perceived (options ranging from friendly to formal, or playful to premium), collecting 2-3 email or product description samples you consider successful, then drafting voice.md in the brand folder that documents a concise voice statement, 4-6 defining voice characteristics, representative language patterns your team uses, and a brief list of communication practices to avoid.
```

[L0322.006]

- Worked example, Silk the Label (www.silkthelabel.com.au): Brandfetch returned the logo, primary colour #5f021f (deep burgundy), location (Brisbane, Australia), and Instagram/Facebook links. The user confirmed the hex code matched their main button colour and provided 2-3 email examples. Claude then drafted voice.md with tone attributes matching those examples. [L0322.007]
- Both output files are mandatory before proceeding to the next course module. Email generation and product descriptions cannot be on-brand without both files. [L0322.010]
- File naming varies across sources: "brand-kit.json" is the name used in the template, "brandkit.json" appears in the mandatory-files rule, and "brand kit.json" appears in the transformation rule and mapping table. Use brand-kit.json, as named in the template. [L0322.006, L0322.010, L0318.006, L0318.009]

**voice.md contents**

| Element | Description | Source |
|---------|-------------|--------|
| Voice summary | One-line statement of how the brand sounds (e.g., "Playful, premium, eco-focused") | User input |
| Voice attributes | 4-6 defining traits (friendly/formal, authoritative/approachable, minimal/warm) | From 2-3 brand email or product examples |
| Language we use | Specific words, phrases, tone patterns, punctuation style | Extracted from provided examples |
| Never do this | Guardrails: tone and language to avoid (e.g., "never use slang", "never make unverified claims") | User guidance + example analysis |

[L0322.014]

- Converting the mapped data into a brand kit and voice file ensures the brand's voice, colors and positioning carry into everything written from it. [L0318.016]

### Keeping work inside one project

- Keep all brand kit work (prompt, Brandfetch data, JSON, voice file revisions) inside one Claude Project so the model maintains full context. Splitting work across separate chats breaks context continuity and triggers hallucination. [L0322.008]

### Applying guidelines to templates and assets

- Keep every template in the brand palette. Pick each color from the document's brand colors rather than the template defaults. Apply the same color system across all formats so social, email and web assets look like one brand. [L0992.002]
- Brand guidelines are a guide, not a rulebook. Small deviations from the palette are acceptable when they improve fit, for example shifting a pink accent toward a purple already in the brand. Check that the change still reads as the brand. [L0992.004]
- The brand's main colour must appear in every rebranded layout, and that colour should stay consistent across the batch. If the brand uses a different colour from the reference, ask Claude to rewrite the instruction so it uses your colour. [L0794.024]
- A logo or brand name on a generated ad is optional. Leaving it off gives more room for the product message. Remove any brand line that repeats the headline or crowds the layout. [L0794.025]

**Setting up email template branding in Shopify Email**

1. Open the Shopify Email settings and scroll to the template branding section at the bottom.
2. Click Customize.
3. Upload the store logo in the logo field.
4. Enter the store name in the store name field.
5. Choose the same font used in the Shopify theme settings in the typography field.
6. Set the colors to match the theme settings: the text color, the button and link color, and the color for labels and outlines.
7. Save all settings. [L1307.012]

- Email template branding should match the store's website: same logo, colors, and fonts. Once it is set in the template branding, all future email automations and campaigns use it by default, so it only needs to be configured once. [L1307.013]

**Reference style set (Source A, a different brand, as an example of the format)**

- Brand fonts: body text and tagline use Gotham Book, with Montserrat Light as the free alternative. The website font is Proxima Nova, and the logo font is Jenna Sue. [L1467.002]
- Brand colours: logo Red #e91e47 and logo Yellow #f7e100. Body text is dark grey #484341 instead of black. Buttons and links use Light Blue #17dbe1. The wordmark is True Black #000000. [L1467.003]

## Brand voice and messaging

### Choose the brand voice before writing

- Pick adjectives that describe how the brand should be perceived, such as trustworthy, reliable, powerful, friendly, inspirational, bold or empowering, and use the same tone in all copy, social posts and comment replies. [L1044.019, L1071.015]
- Decide how the brand should be perceived (for example trustworthy and reliable, powerful, or friendly) and make every message match those choices. [L1071.015]
- Choose the tone before launch to fit the niche: empathetic, empowering or sweet for a parenting store, or cool and slightly cheeky for a younger audience. Refine it later as real customer responses show what resonates. [L1044.038]
- Brand messaging, including the tagline, copy style and product descriptions, comes from the brand personality and tone. Product descriptions emphasise the benefits and features that support the brand's reason for existing. [L1044.020]
- The customer is the hero of the story and the brand is the guide that helps them reach their goal. If the owner does not feel this way, the store must at least appear caring and focused on the customer. [L1044.013]
- Speak to the audience like a friend, not like a brand speaking to a consumer. Corporate jargon and glossy, over-polished messaging are quickly seen through, especially by younger audiences who use TikTok. [L0576.002]
- All website copy should read as a conversation with a friend, aimed at connecting with customers. Keep the tone conversational but avoid slang and colloquial expressions. [L1467.004]
- Use a personal, first-person voice (I, you, me): the copy should read as one person speaking directly to one reader. A byline with a real person's name strengthens this, and the writer's honesty and personality should show through. [L1366.109]
- Use the wording the target customer uses and relates to, so the reader feels the writer speaks their language. [L1366.110]
- Write business-to-customer copy as if speaking to one person standing in front of you, like a good salesperson in a physical store, in a conversational or fun tone if that fits the brand. Business-to-business copy can be more formal. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0396.004]
- Use emojis in announcement bars and email subject lines only when the brand is playful and the audience expects that tone. Keep professional stores free of emojis. (Older advice, Source A 2021.) [L0853.043]
- Do not use the word *dropshipping* in consumer-facing brand copy. [L0989.005]
- Be careful with the word *luxury* in headlines. A quieter premium tone can present price and quality clearly without making the first impression feel too upfront. [L1016.020]
- A premium brand can keep its brand equity and still test sales-focused creatives: run distinctive on-brand creatives, such as a signature sound, ASMR-style audio or a recognisable visual style, alongside the direct-response ads. [L1016.018]
- Copywriting is the core skill for selling digital products: the right words name the customer's problem and create the need to buy. Strong copy also improves results for physical dropshipping, shapes brand image, signals company values, and balances information with relatability. [L0513.005]

### Rewrite existing copy in brand voice

- Rewriting website copy to match the brand voice multiplies the impact of a visual rebrand. Without changing the site structure, updating headlines and body text to match brand personality makes the site feel more authentic, human and trustworthy. [L0317.017]
- Generate on-brand headings with this prompt, then pick from the several options ChatGPT returns. [L0317.018]

```
What is a good heading that matches our brand voice for [situation or current heading]? Two to three words, give multiple options.
```

[L0317.018]

- Rewrite all site sections in one pass with this prompt. ChatGPT returns versions that match the brand personality and tone, ready to copy back to the website. [L0317.019]

```
Rewrite each of these sections in our brand voice: [paste all website copy sections].
```

[L0317.019]

- Small punctuation and word-order changes shift perceived tone. Changing *Wash, wear and repeat* to *Wash. Wear. Repeat.* creates a punchier, more confident, more authentic tone. [L0317.020]
- Before generating a voice file, collect 2-3 email or product description examples from the brand. They teach the AI the real tone, language patterns and style the brand uses. [L0322.013]
- Validate the extracted voice with Apify: download the brand's Instagram feed and analyse caption language, product descriptions and engagement tone. Compare the result with Brand Fetch's voice analysis to confirm the actual voice matches the strategic definition. [L0318.021]
- Supplier feature lists describe what a product has, but buyers respond to what the feature does for them. Turn each feature into a benefit that explains why it matters, for example *stable* becomes *perfect for beginners*. [L1130.027]
- Feature-to-benefit examples: *inflatable design* becomes *easy storage and transportation*, and *durable construction* becomes a reason why durability matters to the buyer. Add a zoomed-in image of key construction details next to the text. [L1130.028]
- Copying supplier descriptions word for word is a quick start but leaves the page weak. Rewrite features as benefits and add images and examples as early as possible. [L1130.029]
- Product descriptions must be written for the customer, not copied from the manufacturer. Manufacturer copy is technical and generic, and visitors leave a product page when the text is unclear or identical to competitors. [L1293.010]
- Check how top competitors name a product before changing your title format. If they do not use a hyphen or lead with the brand name, consider removing the hyphen and the brand from the title. (Older advice, Source A 2022.) [L0963.073]
- Supplier product information supplies most of the store's content. Write a direct-response style store and shape the copy around what buyers need to read in order to buy, even if you are not the product expert. (Older advice, Source A 2019.) [L0663.070]
- Use a clear brand story framework, such as the StoryBrand approach by Donald Miller, to guide content ideas and messaging. Tactic-specific social media books date quickly, so prefer strategy-level books. (Older advice, Source A 2020.) [L0664.080]

### Product naming

- Name products after the dream outcome, not only the item. *Stylish Africa map necklace* sells appearance and self-expression, while *Africa map necklace* only describes the item. Benefit-focused naming directly increases perceived value. [L0233.010]
- Use the words of the dream outcome in product names, such as *stylish africa map necklace* instead of *africa necklace*, so the value is clear at first glance. [L0502.012]
- Name the product after one clear benefit. Mixing two benefits in one name, such as sleep improvement and compression, confuses buyers about what they are getting. [L1194.044]
- A name should communicate a concrete benefit within a defined timeframe. A clear timeframe and outcome make a name more attractive than a technical or vague description, and the same product can sell better under a benefit-led name. [L0683.015]
- Benefit-led naming pattern:

  **[Specific result] + [defined timeframe] + [product type]**

  The timeframe makes the outcome measurable, and the result tells the customer what they gain. Test several names against each other and keep the best performer. [L0683.018]

- Name examples:

  | Name | Assessment | Reason |
  |---|---|---|
  | *Float Tank Center Session* | Weak | Describes the service but gives no outcome [L0683.016] |
  | *Free 6 Week Stress Release Challenge* | Stronger | States a time interval and the result customers get after it [L0683.016] |
  | *48 Hour Neck Ease Massager* | Pattern example for a neck massager | Names a specific result and a defined period, which makes the promise concrete [L0683.017] |
  | *Sleep Through the Night Pillow in 7 Days* | Pattern example | Specific result within a defined period [L0683.018] |
  | *Back Pain Relief Stretch Kit in 14 Days* | Pattern example | Specific result within a defined period [L0683.018] |

- Use this prompt in AI tools to generate product names and a description:

