10. Click Switch to professional account and select Business. [L0654.013]
11. Click Next twice. [L0654.013]
12. Choose the category Shopping and Retail and click Done. [L0654.013]
13. Confirm that you want to switch to a professional account. [L0654.013]

Path: More > Settings > Account type and tools > Switch to professional account > Business > category Shopping and Retail > Done. [L0654.014]

**Profile photo and bio on Instagram**
- Use the same profile photo as on Facebook. [L0654.015]

### Pinterest profile

**Setup**

1. Set the username to the store name for a general store, or to the product name if the store sells one product. [L1135.020]
2. Replace the profile picture with the store logo or a product image. [L1135.020]
3. Write an About section of five to six lines describing the store. The lesson suggests using ChatGPT to draft it. [L1135.020]
4. Add a cover image, such as a store banner or a product-related image. [L1135.020]

**Treat the profile like a small website**
- Treat the Pinterest profile like a small website and include the same types of content you would show on your store. [L1136.014]
- Every board and pin should contain links back to the store, because a profile without links sends no traffic. [L1136.014]
- Each board works like its own niche category. The board a visitor saves a pin to determines where that pin is organised on the profile, so organise boards by category so each one is relevant to a specific audience segment. [L1136.038]

**Board types**
- Product boards cover products, new releases and best sellers, but they are not enough on their own. Storytelling or inspiration boards perform better because they show the result a customer wants and place the products inside that story. [L1136.015]
- Build a board around the outcome a customer wants, such as better sleep, and fill it with relevant images and tips. Place the product as one piece of that routine, so it is seen as the means to the outcome, not just an item for sale. [L1136.016]
- Resource boards collect useful how-to and tip content related to the products, either from your own blog or from other good sources. Their purpose is to add value and build trust, which makes visitors more likely to buy. [L1136.017]
- Behind-the-scenes boards show real people, such as the founders, team or daily life behind the brand. This humanises the store and builds trust, because people prefer to buy from people. [L1136.018]
- Testimonial pins combine a customer quote with a photo of the customer, designed as an image. Place them on a dedicated testimonial board on the profile, the same way testimonials appear on the website. [L1136.019]

**Reach**
- A single viral Pinterest pin can drive enough traffic to build a business, and some brands have built most of their marketing on Pinterest. Pinterest should still be integrated into places where the audience already visits, such as the site, email and other social networks, rather than becoming the main focus of the homepage. [L1136.041]

### Posting cadence

The sources give different frequencies. The 2-3 per week figure appears in the setup and trust-check sources, while 1-2 per week appears in the 2025 and 2022 sources. Both are reported. Use the table to see when each applies.

| Source | Frequency | Platforms | When it applies |
|---|---|---|---|
| L0653.006 | 2 to 3 per week, organic, no paid promotion | Facebook and Instagram | Credibility setup for a new store |
| L0470.087 (2023) | 2 to 3 per week | Facebook and Instagram | Brand checked after a Google search; expensive products bought after research |
| L0645.040 | No weekly posts needed | Facebook page | Dropshipping ads with impulse buyers, of whom about 10 to 15 percent may check the page; a few posts and a reasonable follower base help trust |
| L1108.003 | A few posts before testing | Facebook page | Before a test, so the page looks legitimate; spend little time on social presence during validation |
| L1346.028 | At least once a week, with a link to a product page; daily posting not required | Facebook, Twitter, Instagram and Pinterest | Accounts must show signs of activity |
| L0857.048 (2025) | One or two basic posts a week | Facebook and Instagram | Credibility when people search for the brand; posts do not drive sales directly |
| L0665.069 (2022, older advice) | One to two times per week at most | Social media in general | Show the business is active: new collections, judge.me reviews, restocks or sale announcements |
| L0963.075 (2022, older advice) | At least once a week | Social media in general | Keeps the account credible |
| L1367.007 | Feed first holds about 20 images, then 1 to 3 posts per week | Instagram on a new page | Before running any reels |

- Posting an inactive profile leaves a visible gap that undermines the credibility purpose of the accounts. [L0653.018]
- Routine posts are enough to support trust and remarketing, so content can stay simple. [L0963.076]

### Organic content ideas

- Content for a small store's organic posts follows a simple pattern: show a product, announce a seasonal or holiday promotion, announce a newly approved supplier, announce a product back in stock, or announce a product now available in another colour. No large content production is needed. [L0653.007]
- Examples: a product photo with a short lead line for a winter sale, a post about a new supplier that was just approved, a restock notice for a bestseller, and a note that a product now comes in a new colour. [L0653.008]
- Reviewing the Facebook pages of large dropshipping stores is a useful source of ideas for what type of posts a store should publish. [L0653.009]
- Instagram feed: fill the normal feed with images, not reels, such as lifestyle images, offers and product details. [L1367.005]
- Use Instagram Highlights for social proof and offers. Keep a Reviews highlight with supplier reviews and an Offers highlight that gets a new announcement each week. [L1367.017]

**Instagram setup procedure for high click-through rate**

1. Before creating the account, connect to a USA VPN and keep it on at all times while using the account. [L1367.005]
2. Create a brand new Instagram account using a unique phone number and email address that have not been used before. [L1367.005]
3. Convert the account to a business page. [L1367.005]
4. Write a clear offer in the bio that states the product or brand, the shipping promise, social proof, scarcity and a call to action pointing to the link. [L1367.005]
5. Create two highlights: Reviews, where you post the supplier's reviews to build trust, and Offers, where you post the new offer each week as an announcement. [L1367.005]
6. Fill the normal feed with images, not reels, such as lifestyle images, offers and product details. [L1367.005]

**Reels**
- Before running any reels on a new Instagram page, the feed should already hold about 20 images. After that, add 1 to 3 posts per week. [L1367.007]

### Follower count and authenticity

- A page with many followers but very few likes and comments suggests bought followers. A healthy page should show about 10 percent engagement on posts, so 100,000 followers would mean roughly 6,000 to 10,000 likes and comments. [L0645.041]
- Buying a few thousand followers for an Instagram or Facebook page was reported to cost under $50, often around $10. This is a trust factor, not a sales driver, and it does not affect the ad account. [L0645.042]
- Do not buy followers. [L0857.048] *(2025.)* This conflicts with the cost report in L0645.042. The 2025 source advises against it, and the Source C source reports it as a trust factor with no effect on the ad account. Choose based on the trade-off between the cost reported in L0645.042 and the advice in L0857.048.
- Paid campaigns to build a few hundred likes for social proof are a different approach from buying followers. [L0429.070] *(2022.)*
- A low follower count of about 5 to 10 on a new page did not cause problems in practice. Ad viewers mainly decide based on the ad and the website, so more followers are only a benefit later. [L1108.004]

### Linking social accounts to ads

- Accounts must be active before paid traffic is launched. [L0040.011]
- When launching test ads, do not connect an Instagram profile to the Business Manager. Clicks from Instagram ads then go straight to the website instead of an inactive profile. [L1108.001]
- An Instagram profile that has not posted for a long time loses trust once it is linked to running ads. Keep it unlinked until there is a plan to grow a long-term brand audience. [L1108.002]
- Before testing, publish a few posts on the Facebook page so it looks legitimate. [L1108.003]
- Ad sign: enter the store name and upload the store logo in the ad sign field. [L1378.020]

The two sources differ on linking. L0040.011 requires active accounts before paid traffic for a complete brand presence. L1108.001 and L1108.002 advise keeping an inactive Instagram profile unlinked during testing. Both apply: the first to a finished brand presence, the second to test campaigns on a profile that is not yet active.

### Brand name search signals (older advice)

*Older advice (2022).*

Procedure for a store name that resembles a well-known store:

1. Create profiles on Facebook, Instagram, Pinterest, YouTube, TikTok and Snapchat, even if you post little. [L0963.074]
2. Write a 100-character description of the store with a link to the homepage on each profile. [L0963.074]
3. Get listed in shopping directories with the store name as link text pointing to the homepage. [L0963.074]

## Content, community and personal branding

### Building authority and trust through content

- Authority is a long-term project that runs in the background, not a day-one step. Move from silver suppliers to the best (gold) suppliers in the niche first, then expand into buying guides and tutorials. [L0806.057]
- Build authority and trust through content in this order:
  1. Create thought-leadership articles on industry trends and best practices.
  2. Share your company values through content (e.g., sustainability, charity donations).
  3. Feature customer testimonials and case studies prominently.
  4. Post guides and tutorials that educate, not just sell.
  5. Establish yourself as a helpful expert, not just a retailer. [L0226.033]
- Before planning content, define the goal of each content column or pillar, such as brand awareness, audience sentiment, trend reach, education or trust. Each video should have one clear job, such as nurturing, giving value, educating or building trust. [L1457.006]
- Content should offer a unique perspective rather than repeat what everyone in the niche says. Debatable, distinctive viewpoints attract podcast hosts and readers far more than generic posts. [L0413.026]
- Do not copy your niche or write generic content that everyone else publishes. Such content is boring and will not attract podcast hosts, readers or journalists. [L0413.047]
- Become the recognized expert in your niche before pushing sales: answer questions in Facebook groups, on TikTok and in forums, and explain why your product is different. This active presence brings early sales when paid channels are not yet working. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0768.023]
- Brands that post only helpful, polished content without a visible person behind it tend to feel distant to followers. Being human matters as much as being useful. [L0576.026]

### Sharing process and handling criticism

- Sharing your process publicly, including works in progress, tips and small glimpses of how you work, builds trust and attracts an audience. You control what to reveal and can keep specific details private while still showing progress. [L1343.017]
- Success follows a two-step formula:
  1. Do consistently good work, which takes daily effort and accepts a period of weak results.
  2. Publish that work online.
  Skipping either step means little visibility or little substance. [L1343.018]
- Build audience trust by sharing small useful items, such as a practical tip, a relevant article link or a book you are reading, instead of only promoting products. Reveal a part of your process at a time and keep specific details private if needed. [L1343.046]
- Do not reply to online attacks or spend time on public disputes, because that gives critics more visibility. Ignore negative commenters and be kind and generous toward others, since people tend to notice and respond to that. [L1343.021]

### Showing people behind the brand

- Show the people behind the brand and share behind-the-scenes content, because audiences connect most with visible human stories. Community, trust, connection and longevity are what separate the brands that perform best over time. [L0576.025]
- Personal branding makes the owner the public voice of the business through a blog, guest posts, podcast appearances and a YouTube channel. The more the owner speaks about the business and its industry, the more traffic, sales and industry connections it tends to attract. [L0413.025]

### Customer-generated content and social proof

- Leverage customer-generated content (CGC) in this order:
  1. Ask satisfied customers for testimonials, photos, or short videos.
  2. Offer coupons, free accessories, or discounts in exchange for their content.
  3. Feature their stories prominently on blog and product pages.
  4. Implement Q&A apps (similar to Amazon's format) where customers ask and answer questions about products.
  5. This provides authentic social proof and improves SEO. [L0226.034]
- Build community by giving customers reasons to post their own photos, videos, recipes, or stories, because their posts become content and social proof. Host these contributions in a group where people can easily upload them. [L0487.009]
- Adult coloring books built a brand community where customers share their finished artwork on Facebook, Instagram and Pinterest. The seller's goal is to make customers spread word of mouth so the brand becomes more valuable when sold. [L1153.040]
- Few e-commerce stores collect post-purchase feedback and then tell customers what changed because of it. Doing this consistently gives a store a differentiator in its market, because customers rarely see it from competitors. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1052.019]

### Brand vocabulary and community experience

- Own your vocabulary by naming your places, routines, and events with your own terms. Repeated use of those terms ties them to your brand so people think of you when they hear them. [L0487.007]
- Customer experience in a brand community should feel like belonging, with members talking to each other and forming friendships. Consistency across locations and touchpoints makes the experience predictable, which builds loyalty. [L0487.008]
- Brand consistency across articles and images matters, but a site built over several years may have inconsistent images until a brand standard is set and followed. Keep images consistent from that point forward. [L0382.048]
- A fitness training brand built a distinct identity: its training locations look like warehouses or old factories rather than normal gyms, and it uses its own terms for locations, workouts, and events. Its marketing is polarizing, so people either love or hate it, and members experience a family-like community where they talk and make friends. Consistent experiences across different locations worldwide make members spread the word, and when the brand name was searched on a video platform, eight of the top ten recent results came from people other than the brand itself. [L0487.012]

### Measuring organic conversation

- A brand is working when other people talk about it without prompting: they use the hashtag, post photos, submit videos, and mention the brand in their own content. Track these signals as the proof that the brand is growing. [L0487.010]
- Word of mouth from friends and family drove unpaid sales. After stock arrived, around 1,000 USD in revenue came in without ad spend. [L0492.058]
- To check whether a brand is being talked about on YouTube:
  1. Search the brand name or category term on YouTube.
  2. Filter the results to videos uploaded in the past 24 hours.
  3. Count how many of the top ten results come from people other than the brand itself.
  If most results are from third-party creators, the brand is generating organic conversation. [L0487.070]

### Testing interest before a launch

- Before a product is for sale, a blurred photo of an unreleased product can be posted on Facebook with a "guess what I'm selling" question. Wrong guesses still create comments and show which subjects spark curiosity. This is a cheap way to test interest and start conversation before a launch. [L0487.068]

### Engaging loyal followers and live communities

- Brands that win on Instagram focus on building a community of people invested in the brand's journey, not only on raising follower counts. A strong community turns passive viewers into repeat visitors who keep coming back. [L0576.001]
- Ways to celebrate the most engaged followers:
  - Shout them out in Stories or comment replies.
  - Repost their DMs (only with their permission).
  - Thank them by name or with a custom voice-note reply.
  - Tag them in posts when they contributed an idea. [L0576.022]
- The most engaged followers can be made moderators in a broadcast channel or invited to a separate group chat for super-fans. This makes top followers feel invested in the brand and rewarded for their activity. [L0576.023]
- Set all brand data and messaging in Livecom before a live stream starts, since the brand is controlled through this setup and not during the broadcast. The pre-stream channel page is where the stream is promoted and the community is built. [L0951.009]
- Active participation in online e-commerce and dropshipping communities builds a reputation that attracts clients and requests for paid help. Posting about results and lessons learned can lead to inbound demand before any formal marketing is set up. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1014.015]

### Social media as a producer

- Social media should be used as a producer rather than a consumer: the focus is on creating business-relevant engagement and relationships, not on passive scrolling of personal content. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0526.002]
- Reuse the same presenter image, layout, logo placement and video length across every episode of a faceless channel. A consistent format makes the channel recognizable and lets each new video be produced faster from a fixed template. [L0888.034]
- Write each video as if speaking to one viewer, not a group, because each person watches alone. Choose the platform based on where the target audience spends time, since each platform has a slightly different tone. [L1457.008]
- Build audience profiles in this order:
  1. Describe one audience persona with a daily routine, interests, income situation and life stage.
  2. Write a second persona for another segment of the audience in the same way.
  3. For each persona, list the search terms it would type.
  4. For each persona, list the video formats it would identify with.
  5. Use the personas as the reference when deciding topics and formats. [L1457.009]
- Audience profiles differ by platform and tone. A teenage viewer of makeup tutorials on YouTube, a thirty-something man looking for anti-aging routines, and a founder audience on LinkedIn each need a different voice and topic angle. Define the platform, age and interests before writing the script. [L1457.024]
- TikTok statistics cited for branded video:
  - 84% of people said a brand video convinced them to buy a product or service.
  - 67% want varied storytelling from brands.
  - 52% of internet users want brands to be authentic. [L1429.003]
- Once a seller can reliably generate views on TikTok, the same skill can be applied to a genuinely good product to build a lasting brand, not only short-term viral sales. Early tests on weak products are treated as paid practice for this skill. [L1429.052]

### Podcasts and content series

- Build a podcast or content series for existing loyal customers, not as a new-customer acquisition channel. In the example, weekly episodes of around 5,000 listens per episode per month did not bring a surge of new customers, but the most loyal buyers (30 to 40 orders) listened to every episode, which made it a retention tool. This model may not fit every industry. [L0334.007]

### Personal branding

- Start a YouTube channel for reach around your products or industry, because Google indexes videos very quickly. Publish consistently on one channel instead of posting occasionally. [L0413.028]
- Build personal branding in this order:
  1. Start a blog on your own store site and write guest posts for other industry sites.
  2. Use the unique perspective from those posts to get invited onto other people's podcasts, since debate-driven conversations attract listeners.
  3. Start a YouTube channel about your products or industry and keep posting on it consistently, because Google indexes videos very quickly. [L0413.052]

### PR and press releases

- Issue a press release when you add a new supplier and tell that supplier about it, which can bring links back to your site. Publications that feature your store can be listed in a resource section on your site. [L0413.024]
- helperreporterout.com connects you with journalists who look for industry experts. Register, write a bio with your areas of expertise and reporters can contact you for news stories and features. [L0413.027]
- A brand-owned store can own its brand name in search, but it must create demand for its products through PR, brand mentions and content. Without these, product-name searches stay small. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0949.060]

### Trade shows and events

- Trade show strategy:
  1. Attend industry trade shows to find suppliers and learn trends.
  2. If unable to attend, contact suppliers and ask them to take photos of your product or logo at the show.
  3. Post articles featuring your products at the trade show (e.g., "Our Products at [Trade Show Name]").
  4. This creates content, backlinks, and authority without direct attendance. [L0226.035]

### Dream 100 relationship building

- Build connections with each Dream 100 contact through the platform where they are active. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0526.006]
  1. Follow them on Instagram, Facebook or other social networks where they post.
  2. Subscribe to or like their YouTube channel, depending on what makes their posts appear in your feed.
  3. On LinkedIn, follow them if your business is B2B.
  4. Join their Facebook groups and forums.
  5. Subscribe to the email list of anyone who has a website or blog.
- Join Facebook groups and forums as a personal profile, never as a Facebook page, because many groups refuse page accounts and some admins block them outright. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0526.007]
- Relationships are built between people, so operators should be visible as the real person behind the business. Using a fake name is possible but is considered misleading, and open ownership of the business supports the Dream 100 approach. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0526.008]
- Use a dedicated email address, separate from the main business or personal address, for signing up to Dream 100 email lists. A new Gmail or other mail provider account is suitable. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0526.009]
- Subscribing to a prospect's email list shows which topics and content their audience cares about, which can guide your own email and site content. The content should be modelled, not copied. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0526.010]
- In outreach emails, describe your site by the problem it helps the reader solve, not by the products it sells. For example, say you help people build a dream bathroom rather than that you sell bathtubs. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0946.013]

### Sponsorships and community partnerships

- Sponsoring community events is not the only option. Sponsorship and visibility can also come from ads or placements on blog posts run by members of your industry community. [L0413.029]
- Community partnerships cost money, and higher sales usually require investing more in the business. The community is the network of your industry, and being visible in it means people link to you and mention your name in conversations. [L0413.030]
- Set a realistic monthly or yearly sponsorship budget before accepting any sponsorship request. Saying yes to every request is a fast way to spend money without results. [L0413.031]
- Once you publicly offer to sponsor events, many organizers and companies will ask you to join, so you need a fixed sponsorship budget before you respond. Pick a monthly or yearly amount first, then choose the opportunities with the largest reach within it. [L0413.050]
- Plan community sponsorships in this order:
  1. Decide how much money you will spend each month or year.
  2. Identify the sponsorship or event opportunities in your industry with the largest reach, such as sponsoring events, trade shows or meetups, or hosting your own.
  3. Differentiate your sponsorship with a unique format instead of only placing your name on a banner. [L0413.032]
- Hosting your own recurring events, such as dinners or social games for your industry community, as the sole sponsor sets you apart from banner sponsors. It also builds your name in the community. [L0413.033]
- Do not sponsor events with only your name on a banner. Undifferentiated sponsorships do little to raise your profile and cost money without a clear return. [L0413.048]
- No single community or sponsorship tactic works for every industry. Choose tactics based on what your specific niche and customers respond to. [L0413.034]

## Influencer, endorsement and persona-led branding

### Set the goal and audience before choosing a partner

Before searching for influencers, define the following in order:
1. Brand objective: decide whether the goal is brand awareness, driving sales conversions, or engaging with the community. The objective changes which type of creator fits.
2. Target audience: analyze demographics, interests and behaviors of ideal customers.
3. Preferred social platforms: determine which platforms the target audience uses and pick creators who have a presence there.
4. Influencer selection: choose creators whose audience and messaging match the brand objective and target audience, keeping messaging authentic.

[L0505.007]

Clear objectives give influencer marketing direction and purpose, which helps maximize the impact of each collaboration. Each partnership should contribute meaningfully to the overall marketing strategy rather than running as an isolated post. [L0868.010]

Before choosing an influencer, define your target audience in this order:
1. Define the demographic (age, location and similar traits) of your target customers.
2. Define their interests.
3. Define their behaviour.

Use these three points to find the influencer whose audience resonates most with yours. [L0480.005]

### Match the influencer to the campaign goal

- **Brand awareness:** choose an influencer with broad reach and a large, diverse audience. [L0480.007]
- **Engagement:** choose an influencer whose audience is active and interacts through comments, likes, shares and story reposts. Such an audience can be smaller than a mass-reach audience and still be more valuable. [L0480.008]
- **Conversion or sales:** choose an influencer with a proven record of influencing purchase decisions. Look for in-depth product reviews, demonstrated product use, or exclusive discounts for their followers. [L0480.009]
- **Product launch:** pick an influencer who has a history of introducing new products to their audience. A promotion or giveaway with that influencer is a suitable launch approach. [L0480.010]

A large follower count alone does not make an influencer a good fit. The influencer's audience must relate to your target demographic, because a big following does not automatically mean the followers will buy. [L0480.006]

Influencers must share the brand's values and messaging style, not only its audience. A creator's content tone and behavior reflect on partner brands, so a family-friendly brand should avoid creators who use profanity or post inappropriate content. Creators whose lifestyle matches the brand's message are better partners than creators who only cover a related topic. [L0505.011]

### Pitch and work with influencers

- Be transparent about what the collaboration involves from the start, and tell the influencer it is mutually beneficial. Stay flexible on how the work is done, especially with larger influencers, to build a positive working relationship. [L0782.008]
- In the first outreach, clearly state how the collaboration fits your brand and the influencer's goals. Clarity about this alignment is a core part of a successful pitch. [L0782.009]
- Brand physical products and digital products heavily when working with influencers, because influencers avoid promoting cheap-looking products. Digital products need mockups, a polished website and a branded PDF with the influencer's photos. [L0789.043]
- For dropshipped products used in influencer promotions, at least one branded unit must be sent to the influencer. Terms and conditions can state that branded packaging depends on stock, so unbranded packaging may be sent when stock is missing. [L0789.044]
- Influencers test products before promoting them, so send a high-quality, fast-delivered sample. Packaging quality directly affects whether they will promote the product. [L0789.046]
- Partner with people who already have authority in the community instead of positioning your brand against others as the right choice. Bringing established voices in builds trust faster than claiming to be better. [L0487.011]

### Affiliate partners

*Older source (Source A, 2021).* To set up an affiliate program for your store:
1. Decide if you will use Refersion app (paid, not free) to manage it.
2. Create a small affiliate page that explains why you have an affiliate program.
3. Describe the type of business partner you want (niche, audience size, etc.).
4. Include an email address or contact form for interested partners; do not auto-approve.
5. Manually review and approve only qualified affiliates who align with your brand.

[L0270.055]

### Authority endorsements and credibility transfer

- Credibility transfers from respected institutions, professional bodies, and recognized symbols to a product that carries their endorsement. A prospect who respects the endorser often stops checking the claims themselves. [L0340.019]
- *Older source (Source B, 2021-22).* As a newer business, aligning with trusted market figures transfers some of their credibility to you through association, significantly accelerating your own authority development compared to building alone. [L0297.004]
- The most powerful endorsement is an official approval from a respected institution, and it can be backed by a seal or logo that signals review. Where a full endorsement is impossible, a recognizable symbol of authority can still provide a similar boost. [L0340.020]
- Health and science products gain credibility from authority cues such as a figure in a white lab coat presenting statistics. Use only authority cues that are real and relevant, since the trust they carry depends on the reader's respect for that authority. [L0340.021]
- Signed ads, a named spokesperson or a clear personal authority make claims feel more credible than corporate voice. Use a real person to represent the brand when the claim depends on personal expertise. [L1261.114]

Use authority endorsements in this order:
1. List the people, groups, and institutions in your industry whose respect your target buyers already have.
2. Ask one of them for a testimonial, endorsement, or official approval of your product.
3. Get written permission to use their name and image in your marketing.
4. Optionally pay for a short video of them explaining the product for your website or a DVD.
5. Place a list of verifiable facts, figures, and charts next to the endorsement so the authority supports the argument rather than replacing it.

[L0340.085]

### Identity and group appeals

- Buyers often purchase to enhance their self-image and to close a perceived gap in identity. Ads can therefore show the traits the target customer wants to display, such as attractiveness, intelligence, economic success, or status, and link them to the product. [L0340.017]
- Ways to create identity appeal without persuasive argument: link the product to respected users in the same field, such as a martial arts school naming well-known fighters who train there, or a printer offering free work to local public figures and naming them afterward. A security firm can also cite the choices of professionals who have stricter needs than the average buyer. In each case the copy shows the image the buyer wants rather than proving a claim. [L0340.018]
- Groups shape purchase decisions in three ways: aspirational (people the buyer wants to become), associative (people who share the buyer's values), and dissociative (groups the buyer wants to avoid). Link the product to one or more of these to push the buyer toward a decision made on group identity. [L0340.022]
- For an aspirational group appeal, the prospect must be able to recognize and identify with the people shown. Featuring people who are too distant, too old, or too ordinary for the target market weakens the appeal. [L0340.023]
- Associative group appeals work by speaking directly to the attitudes of one segment, or by distancing the product from a rival group, which can make a product feel cooler or more accepted to younger buyers. [L0340.024]
- A brand name or tagline can signal distance from a group the buyer does not want to resemble, such as an older generation, which attracts a younger segment that wants to look different. This dissociative signal works best when the target group clearly identifies with the contrast and the brand delivers a product that fits it. [L0340.086]

### Attractive characters

- An Attractive Character is a persona that draws people to you across any platform, whether email, Facebook or YouTube. It does not have to be physically attractive. What matters is that the persona gives audiences a reason to follow and trust. [L0523.092]
- Build an Attractive Character from three parts: elements, identity and storylines. Each part is needed to create a character people will like and follow. Start by assembling the elements and identity before writing storylines. [L0523.093]
- Every Attractive Character needs a backstory that connects directly to the product being sold. For weight-loss products, use a weight-loss story, and for financial advice, use a financial story. The backstory creates the hook that makes people willing to follow the path you lay out. [L0523.094]
- If you lack a relevant backstory, borrow a story from a customer, student or client success case and use that person as the character. The character does not have to be the founder, but the backstory must be real and relevant. Success stories from others are a valid source of character material. [L0523.095]
- Use parables, which are short memorable stories from your life, to illustrate the lessons you want customers to learn. People remember stories longer than bare facts, so teaching through parables makes messages stick. Look through your own experiences for these stories. [L0523.096]
- People value what they pay for and act on it more than what they receive free. A small charge can increase follow-through, which is why a parable about paying for a free-looking gift works so well when asking for investment. Use this idea when you want customers to commit. [L0523.097]
- Share real character flaws and vulnerabilities, because perfect characters are hard to relate to. Audiences empathize with imperfect people and trust them more. Hiding every weakness makes you seem untouchable. [L0523.098]
- Take clear positions on hard issues, because a polarizing character creates supporters, neutral people and opponents. Supporters become loyal fans who share and buy repeatedly. Staying neutral to please everyone reaches almost no one. [L0523.099]
- Personal life details, such as a family milestone, can open an entirely new segment of buyers who relate to that part of your persona. This new segment often trusts you enough to buy for the first time. Personal stories widen your audience beyond your original niche. [L0523.111]

#### Identities

- **Leader:** guides the audience from one place to another. The leader has walked a similar backstory path, knows the hurdles and has achieved the result the audience wants, so people follow for help along that path. [L0523.100]
- **Adventurer or Crusader:** curious, goes on a journey to discover truth, then brings back answers to share with the audience. **Reporter or Evangelist:** interviews many people to learn and share their stories. Interviewing experts is an effective way to build authority in a niche you do not yet know well. [L0523.101]
- **Reluctant Hero:** humble and does not seek attention, but feels a duty to share important knowledge despite being uncomfortable in the spotlight. This identity suits people who have hard-won secrets and a sense of responsibility to share them. Choose the identity that fits you naturally and build your character around it. [L0523.102]

### Storyline templates

```
Loss-and-redemption narrative skeleton: 'I used to have it all and felt like I was at the peak. Life was going well. Then [event] struck. I was forced to find a way out of [situation]. Still, it ended up being a silver lining, since I got through [experience] and came away with [lesson or result]. These days I'm [current position].' The narrative shows how hardship can lead to gain. If you don't have a personal story like this, take one from a follower, a client, or mainstream media.
```
[L0523.103]

```
Before and after storyline template: 'First I was [starting condition]. Now I am [current condition].' Use it to show transformation in any market, for example 'First I was [pain point]. Then I tried [product]. Now I [desired result].' Transformation stories are simple to write, can often be shown with pictures alone, and are strong motivators.
```
[L0523.104]

```
Amazing discovery storyline template, for selling webinars and teleseminars: 'Everyone, wait until you hear about this amazing new thing I just discovered! You will not believe it, but I got results on my very first try. I was not sure it would work, but it is amazing, so you have to try it [specific call to action].' This story helps people believe the answer they have been searching for is finally available.
```
[L0523.105]

```
Secret-driven narrative skeleton: 'I'm keeping a secret. To learn what it is, you have to [specific action].' Intrigue about a concealed method pulls readers deeper into your funnel and higher up your value ladder. A secret-based narrative performs well in most markets when the secret connects to what you sell.
```
[L0523.106]

- Us versus them storylines set your audience against outsiders, such as talkers versus doers, and create a rallying cry for loyal followers. Use them to polarize and to pull your fans closer. Make the choice of identity clear so people decide which side they are on. [L0523.107]
- Third-person testimonials provide social proof. Gather as many customer, client and student success stories as possible, then weave them into your messages or use them as standalone case studies. Other people's results often persuade more than your own claims. [L0523.108]

#### Cases

- **Fast-food chain (weight-loss character):** a traditional fast-food chain shifted its marketing around a customer who had lost weight from about 425 pounds to about 190 pounds by eating from the chain twice a day. The company used his before and after story in commercials and on billboards, which set the brand apart from competitors. The positioning moved the chain from generic fast food toward a weight-loss plan, and the same character kept bringing in business for more than fifteen years. [L0523.109]
- **Course company (student as character):** a course company built its continuity program around a student's success story as the Attractive Character instead of its founder's story. The launch sold over 8,000 units and generated more than 1,000,000 USD in sales within two weeks. A later workshop for buyers was split about 50/50 between men and women, where it was usually about 90% men, and women said they wanted to be like the featured student. [L0523.110]

### Persona-led ads and challenge formats

- Ads framed as a person recommending something are expected to earn higher engagement than brand-voiced ads for the same product and offer. Treat this as a hypothesis and verify it with your own split tests before committing budget. [L1475.005]
- A famous personality can work better as a challenge or opponent than as an endorser, because the challenge creates a story and the endorsement only adds a name. Build the ad around the contest and let the product be what gets challenged. [L1366.025]
- Chess computer campaign (1978): the product sold on features and was not moving well against competitors. The ad was reframed as a public challenge to a famous chess champion, run as three ads: the first announced the challenge, the second responded to a dispute over permission, and the third showed the champion declining to play but endorsing the product. The three ads with different concepts sold more than 20,000 units, while competitors selling only the product did not succeed. [L1366.026, L1366.108]

### Whitelist character pages

- A whitelist page is a Facebook page you own that presents a fictional character with perceived authority in your niche, instead of your corporate logo. Ads run from that page so the same product and offer look like a recommendation from a person. The page, audience and pixel all stay in your own account. [L1476.001]
- Every whitelist character needs five elements: a name, a face, a voice, a story and a purpose. If one of them is missing, the page reads as a brand and the illusion of a person breaks. [L1476.005]

| Component | Rule | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Name | Real first name, casual last name if needed, optional title such as Coach, Doc or Trainer | Coach Sam, Doc Ellie, Trainer Mike |
| Face | Clear headshot, direct eye contact, warm expression, AI-generated or licensed stock; the same photo across the page and all ads | One consistent portrait |
| Voice | Choose 3 personality traits and keep them in every post and ad; direct, first-person | Direct, self-deprecating, practical |
| Story | Personal experience with the exact problem the product solves; the origin story that earns credibility | Fixed own posture after 10 years at a desk |
| Purpose | One clear helping goal tied directly to the product category, not multi-niche | 'Back pain from desk work', not 'wellness' |

[L1475.009]

#### Name

- The character name is a real first name with an optional last name and an optional title such as Coach, Doc or Trainer. Titles signal authority without impersonating anyone. Avoid titles like Doc if the character has no real basis for implying that credential. [L1475.010]
- Give the character a real-sounding first name, an optional casual last name, and a title such as Coach or Trainer when it fits. Avoid 'Doc' and other medical titles, because they conflict with the medical caution for character pages. [L1476.006]
- Note on the conflict: the example table above uses 'Doc Ellie', but for character pages the medical caution in L1476.006 applies, so do not use Doc or other medical titles there. [L1475.009, L1476.006]

#### Page category

- Set the page category to a person-like label such as Coach, Enthusiast, Blog or Personal Trainer. Never use E-commerce or Retail as the category, because that makes the page look like a store and breaks the person illusion. [L1475.013, L1476.007]

#### Face and cover

- Use a clear headshot with direct eye contact and a warm, friendly expression for the profile photo. It can be AI-generated or licensed stock, but never a logo or a product shot. Use the same photo on the page and in all ads. [L1476.008]
- Use a lifestyle scene from the character's world as the cover photo, such as a desk setup, gym or outdoors. Never use a product shot or a discount graphic as the cover. [L1476.009]
- A face instead of a logo does about 80 percent of the work of building trust, even if the character profile is not perfect. This is why the whitelist page is worth starting on day one of testing. [L1476.033]

#### Voice

- Choose three personality traits for the character and keep them consistent across every post, primary text and ad. Use them as the framework for how the copy is written. Mixing tones between posts and ads breaks the illusion that a real person is speaking. [L1475.011, L1476.010]

#### Story and purpose

- The character's story is a personal experience with the exact problem the product solves, which builds credibility. Pin a personal organic post to the top of the page explaining why the character started recommending the product. [L1476.011]
- Each character needs one clear purpose tied directly to the product category, such as helping with back pain from desk work. Avoid vague purposes like 'wellness' and never cover several niches with one character. [L1475.012, L1476.012]

#### Bio

- The page bio is a short first-person backstory about the character's own experience with the product's problem. It should be 60 to 100 words, sound like a real person, and contain no hashtags, emojis or salesy language. [L1475.014, L1476.013]
- The bio opens with a backstory hook tied to the product's problem area and ends with what the character shares or helps with. [L1475.014]

### Choosing the archetype

- Pick the archetype by asking who the buyer already trusts, such as a coach, a peer, a parent figure or a quiet expert. Match the character to that trust, not to the product itself. Base this on the product and avatar research. [L1475.006, L1476.014]
- A quiet expert does not need medical qualifications to be trusted. For example, a herbal product could be recommended by an older gardener with decades of hands-on experience rather than by a doctor. [L1476.016]

Workflow:
1. Read your product and avatar research: who buys, their age, life stage, self-image and insecurities.
2. Ask who this buyer already trusts: a peer, a coach, a parent figure or a quiet expert.
3. Pick the archetype that fits that trust source.
4. Attach your customer research and product documents to Claude.
5. Paste the full generator prompt (see the prompt unit for this topic) and let Claude read the research, pick the archetype and generate the assets.
6. Check the 3-line research summary Claude prints to confirm it understood the buyer and product correctly.
7. Copy the outputs: profile photo prompt, cover photo prompt and page bio.
8. Paste the profile and cover photo prompts into ChatGPT, DALL-E or Midjourney to generate the images.
9. Upload the images and the bio to the character page.

[L1475.007]

Product category to archetype (examples):

| Product category | Archetype |
|---|---|
| Fitness, supplements | Coach / Ex-athlete |
| Baby, parenting | Mom / Dad of 3 |
| Beauty, skincare | Beauty enthusiast |
| Health, wellness | Wellness advocate |
| Pet products | Pet parent |
| Home, cleaning | Home organizer |
| Identity-driven products | Retired professional / hobbyist |

Additional archetype styles named for the generator: editor (beauty), nurse-vibe (health and self-care), rescue owner (pets), clean-girl (home).

[L1475.008]

Typical archetype by niche:

| Niche | Archetype |
|---|---|
| Fitness, supplements, performance | Coach / Ex-athlete |
| Baby, parenting, family | Mom / Dad of X |
| Beauty, skincare | Beauty enthusiast / editor |
| Health, self-care | Wellness advocate (be careful, no medical claims) |
| Pet products | Pet parent / rescue owner |
| Home, cleaning | Home organizer |
| Identity-driven products | Retired professional / hobbyist |

[L1476.015]

### Whitelist page results and common mistakes

- Starting point: a brand page held a steady ROAS of 1.8 to 1.92. Change: a whitelist page with a fictional character was introduced for the ads. Result: ROAS rose to about 2.1 to 2.3, a marginal gain that matters more at scale. [L1476.023]
- Common mistakes with character pages: using a logo or product shot as profile or cover photo, using E-commerce or Retail as the category, giving the character several niches as its purpose, using stock-photo perfection or polished corporate headshots, and impersonating a real person. [L1476.032]

### Clubs, teams and local partnerships

- For a brand focused on local awareness, approach large local sports teams to propose collaborations. These partnerships build brand recognition in a home market. They are aimed at awareness rather than direct sales. [L0661.069]
- A brand can be built around a local market by partnering with local sports teams or clubs for custom-branded merchandise, such as fan hoodies or skiing-related products. Custom logos on hoodies at the supplier used required orders of at least 500 pieces. [L1212.054]
- Custom-branded versions of a product can be sold at a higher price than standard designs. Club or partner logos add value and give fans a reason to buy. [L0492.055]

Club partnership procedure:
1. Identify a local football club with passionate fans and a sold-out stadium, relevant to the brand's audience.
2. Email the club's marketing department with a simple collaboration proposal.
3. Hold a call with the club representative to discuss terms.
4. Agree on a one-time payment for one year of logo usage rights, reported at about 10,000 USD.
5. Produce custom designs with the club logo and sell them to fans at a higher price.

[L0492.054]

## Packaging, inserts and customization levels

### Why the unboxing matters

- The unboxing is the customer's first physical contact with the brand, and a generic supplier box makes a store look like a temporary dropshipping operation. For a short test of a few weeks, plain packaging is acceptable, but for a long-term brand the customer experience must be designed deliberately. [L0403.001]
- Customers pay for the overall experience, not only the product, so better packaging can raise repeat purchase rates and build a loyal customer base at relatively low cost. [L0403.003]
- Packaging is the physical touchpoint when the customer receives the order, so it should continue the brand look and voice from the website and photos. A branded unboxing experience strengthens the brand. [L0884.016]
- Packaging copy should follow the same seasonal or emotional message as the website, so the physical product and the online store feel like one brand. [L0884.017]
- Small surprises, such as an unexpected extra item or a thank-you gift in the parcel, make customers more likely to enjoy the purchase and return. Build surprise into the unboxing experience. [L0345.003]
- The full packaged presentation (box, personalized message, unique product insert and possibly a free gift) creates an emotional connection at unboxing, when the customer first interacts with the brand, and can lead to higher brand loyalty. [L0403.023]
- Personalized packaging, inserts and notes are small changes that matter for customer loyalty. A store that launches new products to a loyal base can gradually become a brand. [L0760.023]
- Brand presentation matters more than price in differentiating a dropshipping product: a custom box can remove most generic competitors from the comparison. [L0577.024]
- Marketing angles that separate a dropshipping store from competitors help build the brand early. Small brand elements such as custom boxes and thank-you cards also count, since every touchpoint from store to delivered parcel shapes the customer experience. [L1469.020]
- Custom branded packaging, such as a bag with your logo, is a low-cost way to make the unboxing feel more premium than a plain cardboard box. [L0387.030]

### When to start customizing

- Branding can be added in four levels: front-end branding with custom images, videos and ads; store customization; packaging such as custom boxes and thank-you cards; and omnichannel presence across platforms. Add each level as the product proves itself, since the mentor raises prices after branding. [L0335.053]
- For dropshipping, wait at least two weeks of consistent, profitable results for a product before investing in custom packaging. Work on one clear winner product at a time rather than customizing many products at once. [L0403.004]
- A brand that manufactures or owns its own product should start customization from day one to build trust. [L0403.005]
- Do not ship a branded product as dropshipping. Doing so removes the chance to make the customer feel the owner's brand at unboxing, which reduces word of mouth. [L0492.057]
- Early on, plain unbranded packaging was used without customer complaints, so the priority is to secure the first sales. Once a supplier is in place, move to branded packaging with your logo. [L1381.054]
- Ultimate branded customization (logo printed on the product itself) requires at least two to three months of consistent profitable sales before investing. [L0403.020]

### The customization ladder by effort and budget

The order below runs from least to most effort and budget.

1. Thank-you cards and tags: minimal cost and effort. [L0403.007]
2. Custom box designs shown on the website as mockups, without building real boxes. [L0403.007]
3. Real custom boxes, once a product is a clear winner. [L0403.007]
4. Product customization with the brand logo on the product itself. This requires at least two to three months of consistent profitable sales before investing. [L0403.007, L0403.020]

Once thank-you cards or custom boxes are in place and the product is profitable, the next level is a custom manual, branded cards and better-quality boxes. [L0403.016]

### Thank-you notes and branded packaging

- Brand the packaging with the logo and include a thank-you note from the owner. This creates a better experience and drives word of mouth. [L0492.056]
- Use Placeit or a similar mockup tool to find packaging that matches the product's size, and create the design on that template. Choose a box that fits the product, since an oversized box for a small product makes customers doubt that the product is real. [L0577.021]
- Unify branding can be done on a supplier's packaging template. The design is created in Canva or Photoshop, and the cost is 1 to 3 dollars per product. [L0577.023]
- A custom box design from Fiverr at $5 to $10 can be printed by a supplier for about $0.50 per box, so 100 boxes cost about $50. [L1436.066]

### Custom manuals

- Supplier manuals often have poor English and no branding. A custom manual educates the customer about product features and the brand, and it stays with the customer for a long time, which builds lasting trust. [L0403.016, L0403.017]
- A custom manual costs about the same as thank-you cards or tags. [L0403.017]

### Mockups and synthetic branding

**Minimal-investment branding procedure**

1. Create design mockups or design images of custom boxes.
2. Show them on the website.
3. Ship in simple plain boxes at the start.

This increases the store's credibility without the cost of real custom packaging. [L0403.006]

**Synthetic branding risk**

- Synthetic branding, where a product is shown in a branded box while the delivered item is unbranded, is risky. A stated disclaimer does not remove the mismatch. [L0577.022]
- The lesson's approach was to start with synthetic branding and switch to real packaging once sales succeed. [L0577.022]

**Reconciling the two positions**

- The mockup-only stage (level 2 of the ladder) is presented as a credible early step. It is meant as a bridge: start with mockups, ship plain, then switch to real packaging once sales succeed. [L0403.006, L0403.007, L0577.022]
- The risk flagged for synthetic branding applies when the delivered item looks different from what the customer was shown. Keep the stage short and move to real packaging once sales succeed. [L0577.022]

### Packaging copy and inserts

- Pair two opposite ideas across the package, such as how it feels inside and how it looks outside, to create a clever twist that stays on brand. Two examples: "Warm to open, sharp to look at" and "Plain from the door, cozy once it is open." [L0884.018]
- Place the main playful line on the front of the packaging and a second line on the side or back. This keeps the front clean and gives the packaging a bit more depth. [L0884.020]

Prompt for generating a packaging mockup (use unchanged, fill the placeholders):

```
Generate a mockup of this packaging. I've attached examples of custom packaging and the brand guideline for [BRAND NAME]. Use the attached cover page and color palette as the brand reference. Front: [FRONT TEXT]. Side: [SIDE TEXT]. Keep the layout simple, use the brand colors and a serif font for the text, and show the packaging as a [PACKAGING TYPE] for [PRODUCT].
```

[L0884.022]

### Influencer shipping with branded packaging

Procedure:

1. Ask the supplier or agent for the size of the box.
2. Hire a designer on Fiverr to design a custom box and obtain a printable file.
3. Print one branded box at a local print shop.
4. Pack the product in the box, add a handwritten card and a small gift such as chocolate.
5. Ship it quickly by post, since fast delivery matters to influencers.

[L0789.045]

Use the custom box and branded packaging in creator content so viewers imagine the brand and the service, not only the product. [L1436.067]

### Authorized retailers and brand-shipped orders (older advice, 2020)

- As an authorized retailer, you do not need your store name on the shipping box, because the brand ships the order and the customer expects the brand's name. Do not include your own invoice in the package, since the brand does not either. [L0961.009]
- A branded postcard with your store name or marketing message in each order is optional and only worth doing at scale, when you are one of the brand's top sellers. Its purpose should be marketing, such as a mailing list signup, a coupon, a free gift or a refer-a-friend offer, not just brand name visibility. [L0961.010]

### Customizing books and other products

**Deciding whether to customize**

- For book products, customizing the cover and the inner content is strongly recommended once the product has been validated, because it gives the book a distinct, personal identity. Customization is optional if the goal is only quick testing. [L0333.017]
- Customize a product only when it has a specific pain point that its audience cares about. Most books without a clear pain point are weak candidates for building a brand. [L1030.019]
- Before investing in customization, check whether the product can support a brand and whether the pain point is real. Customizing a product that only sells on a short spike does not build a lasting store. [L1030.021]
- Customized versions build longer-lasting brand equity and longevity, which makes this a long-term positioning approach rather than a pump-and-dump. The benefit depends on actually customizing the product. [L1255.005]

**Why customizing separates you from competitors**

- A customized book separates you from the many sellers who run the same generic version, which is the main competitive advantage of customizing. Unchanged copies compete head-to-head with every other seller of that version. [L1255.003]
- Customizing turns a found product into your own version instead of a repackaged template. A customized listing is harder for competitors to copy directly. [L1255.004]

**Case: from proof of concept to custom brand**

- The source's own book product started with a proof of concept found in Kalodata data and was then changed into a custom brand. The product was tested first and only rebranded after it showed it could sell. [L1175.023]

**Cover and content changes**

- Changing only the cover colour and one title phrase can leave a customized book looking almost identical to the original listing. Add further differences, such as wording variations and decorative elements, before judging whether the cover stands apart. [L0333.026]
- Keep an accent element in a colour that contrasts with the cover rather than matching a cover colour that is already the same, such as red accents on a red cover. Matching colours in that way can look odd on the finished product. [L0333.009]
- Decorative additions to a customized cover look more authentic when they are hand-drawn and imperfect rather than professionally polished. A raw look makes the book seem personally written. [L0333.011]
- Example: a gut-health book with a cure-focused title can be retitled toward a beginner audience, such as mothers, using a "101" style introduction. This shifts the angle while keeping the same underlying book. [L1255.008]
- Example: a simple cover change is shifting a blue cover to a darker blue shade to create a visible variation. A full recolour of the cover is also an option if a bigger change is wanted. [L1255.009]
- AI image edits can change parts of the image that were not requested, such as the colour of an unrelated element. Check every returned image against the original and send a narrower follow-up request to restore the unwanted change. [L0333.008]

#### Further details

- Dropshipping allows you to customize packaging and branding (custom boxes, inserts, personalized notes) without manufacturing complexity. This lets you build a recognizable brand identity gradually over time. [L0007.024]

## Private label and building your own product brand

### Private label versus white label

- **White label:** The manufacturer already makes the product and sells it to many buyers. The buyer adds their own label and sometimes changes packaging or minor details to sell it as their own. [L1151.001]
- **Private label:** The product is made to the buyer's specification. The buyer decides on ingredients or design parts, packaging and label design, so the product is unique to their brand. [L1151.002]
- **Older (2021-22):** A white label product is sold by many competing brands at once. In the example, one product was sold by up to 90 sellers, so competition came mainly through price. [L1151.009]
- **Older (2021-22):** Differentiation depends on having the best version of a product. Private label is the path to a unique product. White label works best when the product is a commodity with little room for change. [L1151.010]
- **Older (2021-22):** Putting your own label on a white label product does not make it a distinct brand. If many sellers offer the same item, you compete mainly on price, so the product itself must change to stand out. [L1151.014]
- **Older (2021-22):** A private label product must be clearly different from what already exists in its market, though the difference can be small. It should be brandable, so customers can quickly see why it is better or different from alternatives in the niche. [L1154.002]
- **Older (2021-22):** A private-label copy of a product that every competitor already sells does not differentiate you, because a manufacturer will simply stamp your name onto the same item. Offer a distinct feature, design or bundle that makes customers seek out your brand specifically. [L1155.002]
- **Older (2021-22):** A private-label version should stay recognizable as the same product category. It needs one clear advantage over existing options, such as a missing feature or a fixed weakness, so customers ask for it by your brand name. [L1155.020]
- **Older (2021-22):** Selling an unbranded product is a failure pattern. A well-funded unbranded e-commerce company that sold everything without a brand later shut down, so a product needs a recognizable brand identity to survive. [L1155.003]

### Deciding between a brand name and private label

- **Older (2018):** About 10% of the brands the expert worked with allow private labeling. These are usually lesser-known brands. Start private label only after the store has data and sales. [L1274.043]
- **Older (2021):** Before choosing private label, check whether you can sell under the brand name at current market prices. Private labeling makes sense only when competitors undercut each other so much that margins disappear. [L0247.044]
- **Older (2021):** Do not remove a searchable brand name, product name or SKU just to attract higher-end suppliers. Private labeling only makes sense for vague, generic product names that nobody searches for. [L0834.115]
- **Older (2021):** Keep the supplier name and original product names where customers search for them. Renaming products to be more appealing can reduce search traffic. [L0962.043]
- **Older (2020) procedure:** Deciding between brand names and private label for suppliers:
  1. Check whether each supplier requires private label. If it does, focus on building a strong private label brand around those products. [L0852.055]
  2. Where the supplier does not enforce MAP, check supplier by supplier whether competitors have pushed prices so low that margins are gone. [L0852.055]
  3. Where margins are still intact, list the products under the supplier's brand name and run ads as usual. [L0852.055]
  4. Where margins are cannibalized, add bonuses or offers, or consider private labeling those products. [L0852.055]
- **Private labeling as an alternative:** When a product has a good market but its price level is too low for the 2x rule or it is being undercut, add your own brand name to the unbranded supplier product. You then no longer compete on the brand's search terms or on price. This works when the product is unique or high quality and enough markup is possible for a healthy margin. [L1051.012]
- **Options for products that fail the margin test:** Either do not list them, or check whether they can be private labeled. Private labeling requires the supplier to offer something unique or high quality and the market to support a higher markup. [L1051.013]
- **Brand-less suppliers (2023):** Private labeling for brand-less suppliers is a later step, not one from day one:
  1. Get a small batch of products from a manufacturer who has no brand names. [L0804.011]
  2. Create your own brand name for the products. [L0804.011]
  3. Sell them as one of the brands on your store. [L0804.011]

### Testing the market before building your own brand

- Test the market with existing products first. Learn which products, prices, features and benefits the market responds to, and only then create your own product. [L1115.026]
- Ask your target customers what they want before producing a custom product. This validates demand and avoids producing a product that will not sell. [L0790.030]
- Choose products based on your own sales data: what sells, what customers ask about, what they complain about and which products have profit. Using this data reduces the risk of a new line. [L0413.038]
- Branded testing carries high risk. Large upfront investment and emotional attachment to a product can lead sellers to keep spending on a product without good market fit. It requires strong product-selection skill and a large budget. [L1158.005]
- Starting a brand from zero without dropshipping experience and cash flow usually wastes a lot of money. Gain experience and cash flow from dropshipping first. [L1469.019]
- After several successful dropshipping stores, the next step is building your own brand with custom branding and packaging. Early on, you may fulfill orders yourself to keep control of the customer experience. [L0388.021]
- **Older (2021-22):** Some high-ticket operators add their own manufactured products next to dropshipped items to build a brand. This is a growth step after the store is already profitable. [L0912.012]

### Preparing a private label product line

- **Definition:** Private label means taking a product that already sells well in your drop shipping stores, making minor changes, adding your own label and selling it as a new product line. You do not need to invent a new product, because the sales data shows what customers already buy. [L0413.035]
- **Size of the first line:** Start with about 3 to 5 products. Each product needs investment and, when importing, warehousing. Carrying many SKUs dilutes the line and adds cost without proportional results. [L0413.036]
- **Growth lever:** Private label is a major lever for moving a drop shipping business from about $50,000 in monthly sales to several hundred thousand. It requires a real investment in branded products and their stock. [L0413.051]
- **Market fit in branding:** Branded products need strong market fit. Invented brand names have no search demand at launch, so private label dropshipping only makes sense when margins are strong and buyers can find the brand another way. [L0963.071]

### Branding levels and the path to a unique product

- **Three levels of branding:**
  1. Apply your logo to an existing sourced product. [L0324.041]
  2. Partner with a manufacturer to modify an existing product, creating a unique version competitors cannot replicate. [L0324.041]
  3. Design a completely original product. This offers maximum differentiation but requires substantial upfront investment. [L0324.041]
- **Stages of private labeling:** First add your own labels and logos to a product, then create your own variant of the product. The variant stage is where most of the money can be made when selling the store or brand. [L1469.018]
- **Generic products as a stepping stone:** A generic product may not be brandable, but it can still earn profit and teach dropshipping skills. Treat early generic winners as a cash source and learning step. [L1171.028, L1171.057]

### Manufacturing a private label product with a supplier

- **Branding cost (Unify):** Unify branding typically costs 1 to 3 dollars extra per product, depending on the size of the product. [L0577.019]
- **Minimum order quantity (Unify):** Roughly 20 to 100 units. It can sometimes be pushed to about 20 to 50 units when the request comes through a mentor or course referral. [L0577.020]
- **Procedure: private labeling with Unify:**
  1. Contact the supplier (Unify) with a link to the product and request a price list for the relevant countries. [L0577.018]
  2. Ask the supplier to send the product's package size, product price, shipping fee and total cost per country and shipping times. [L0577.018]
  3. Check the numbers. Only once the numbers work, ask about branding. [L0577.018]
  4. Ask for a packaging template from the supplier. [L0577.018]
  5. Design the packaging in Canva, Photoshop or Placeit using the template dimensions (Placeit's packaging mockups provide the size). [L0577.018]
  6. Upload the finished design to the supplier. [L0577.018]
  7. Get the sizes and requirements back from the supplier. [L0577.018]
- **Customization:** Private suppliers can add customization such as logos and better packaging. This improves branding and can increase profitability as the store scales. [L0685.025]
- **Own name or color:** Put your own name or a custom color on the product through your manufacturer to create a brand. Customers trust and return to brands they recognize, so unbranded goods are hard to differentiate. [L0790.028]

### Brand names and product naming

- **Own brand name, not the store name:** A brand created for an imported generic product must have its own name, separate from your company or main store name. Competitors can then sell the same product under your brand. [L1090.084]
- **Store brand name on each store (2024):** Use a store brand name you own on each store and write unique product names and descriptions for them. Do not copy the supplier brand or product name when you white-label products for Google Shopping. [L1115.025]
- **Unique names:** Private label products need a unique brand name and a unique product name, such as brand plus product name. A purely descriptive title, such as material and size, makes the product look identical to the same item sold elsewhere. [L1015.010]
- **Brand on the page:** Put the brand name on the product page and in the product descriptions, not only on the physical product, so the seller brands the product. [L1015.012]
- **Procedure: creating a private label listing (2025):**
  1. Create your own brand name, not the store name. [L0857.049]
  2. Create unique product names. [L0857.049]
  3. Use the supplier's images. [L0857.049]
  4. Add the products to the store like products from any other brand. [L0857.049]

  Customers then see a normal brand, and the brand can later be sold by competitors. [L0857.049]
- **Conflict on renaming and names:** The 2021 rule keeps original product names where they carry search traffic. [L0962.043, L0834.115] The 2024 and 2025 approach replaces generic names with your own unique product names and brand. [L1115.025, L0857.049] Use original names where customers already search for them. Use your own unique names where the product name is generic and nobody searches for it.
- **Older (2020):** Private label products need unique product descriptions, reviews and specifications. Do not copy text from other sellers. Only the photos can be the same, and ideally you use unique images too. [L0852.056]

### Making product images unique

- **Conflict on images:** The 2025 procedure says to use the supplier's images. [L0857.049] The 2020 guidance says only the photos can be the same, and ideally you use unique images. [L0852.056] Reverse image search lets shoppers find identical products on other sites, but only a tiny fraction of buyers use it. For private label products, customizing the supplementary images is still worthwhile to make the product page look unique. [L1015.013]
- **Procedure: unique supplementary images in Canva:**
  1. Keep the main product image with a white background, since Google Shopping requires it and it cannot be customized. [L1015.014]
  2. Upload a supplementary product image to Canva. [L1015.014]
  3. Use the Magic eraser tool to remove the white background so the product is transparent. [L1015.014]
  4. Search for a backdrop image that fits the product, such as modern or minimalist office space for a standing desk. [L1015.014]
  5. Drag the backdrop behind the transparent product. [L1015.014]
  6. Export the result and upload it to the product page as an additional image. [L1015.014]

### Launching and selling private label products

- **Launch inside the existing store:** Launch privately labeled products as another brand inside the store, using your own logo, brand name, product names and descriptions. Their traffic comes from collections, the homepage, email and social, because nobody searches for a new brand yet. Build a separate brand store only once that brand has grown. [L1116.050]
- **Older (2022):** To build demand for private label products, use the traffic from your other brands, remarketing audiences and the email list. Promote the private label products through promotions such as a 20 percent discount. [L0963.072]
- **Older (2021):** Unbranded supplier products can be turned into your own brand through private labeling. Early traffic for such products comes mostly from remarketing, email and monthly promotions rather than Google Shopping. [L0968.085]
- **Value of a brand:** A store built around its own brand is worth far more than a store selling generic, non-branded products. A brand also gives you a more loyal audience to upsell. [L0790.029]

### Faceless brands in supplement and digital niches

- Build faceless supplement and digital product brands focused on specific emotional or performance benefits, such as energy, focus, confidence, testosterone or libido. These brands do not need a personal brand or founder visibility. [L0307.034]
- Use white-label suppliers and position around solving a specific problem through benefits and proof, not personal authority. Problem-based marketing replaces personal brand. [L0307.034]
- This works well in supplement, e-learning and digital product niches, where solution effectiveness matters more than the founder story. [L0307.034]

### Upgrading and expanding the product line

- **Annual upgrades:** Once you manufacture a branded product, plan to improve and upgrade it annually. Example: a hearing aid manufacturer continuously upgraded its product each year after initial manufacturing through an Alibaba supplier. [L0280.034]
- **Collections:** Each collection should carry its own campaign and theme, with new ideas each time, so the brand stays fresh. Similar products in different colourways can still work when they suit the brand, but the collection should still change. [L1438.037]
- **Differentiation through quality and variants:** Many mass-market accessories in a niche are of low quality, so a higher-quality version of the same product is a clear way to differentiate. Differentiation can also come from new flavors for rubs or humorous sayings on products such as grill covers. [L1152.015]
- **Licensing:** Licensing is another differentiation route for accessories, for example licensed sports-team designs or licensed logos. [L1152.016]

### Niche brand examples

#### Coloring books

- **Case:** The coloring book seller saw that competitor books offered around 30 designs, sometimes up to 40, and that their paper was too thin. Markers bled through and ruined two or three designs. The seller hired designers, kept the best design work for the second book, and built each book on thicker paper and a larger design count of over 120 designs. Books were also split into beginner, intermediate and advanced designs, and the set was offered as a three-pack. The result was an improving product line across the nine-book plan. [L1153.041]
- **Paper quality:** Paper quality is a visible pain point in the coloring book niche. Thin paper lets markers and gel pens bleed through. Thicker paper reduces complaints and reviews that hurt conversion. [L1153.042]
- **Difficulty levels:** Offer a range of difficulty levels for the same niche, such as beginner, intermediate and advanced designs. Buyers want quick sessions as well as detailed work, so one difficulty level does not satisfy everyone. [L1153.043]
- **Theme range:** Offering a variety of themes, such as animals, mandalas and landmarks, helps buyers who choose books quickly in a store. Carrying a wide range of themes lets each customer find something they want to color. [L1153.044]

#### Clothing

- **Case:** A dropshipping seller who already had revenue from a dropshipping store started a first fashion brand in Switzerland. Instead of only reselling, the seller sourced the product directly, ran a self-funded photo shoot, and packed every order by hand from a local base. The next step was to order stock in bulk into their own warehouse, focus on a strong customer experience, and rely on word of mouth and referrals. The stated goal was to sell the brand for seven figures within the Swiss market in a few years. The outcome was not yet known at the time of the lesson. [L1469.044]

### Building a brand from a proven product

- **Conversion threshold:** A generic product that proves itself, reaching roughly $50,000 to $100,000 in revenue, is then worth turning into a brand. [L1171.028]
- **Procedure: turning a proven product into a brand:**
  1. Confirm the product has real revenue, around $50,000 to $100,000. [L1171.029]
  2. Add a brand identity to the product. [L1171.029]
  3. Create custom packaging. [L1171.029]
  4. Build a separate website for the product. [L1171.029]
